<meta>This is the title page for a work by the German mystic Jacob Böhme (1575–1624). Böhme was a shoemaker turned visionary whose complex "theosophical" writings influenced later philosophy and Romanticism.</meta>

# SIX THEOSOPHICAL POINTS,

->or<-

->On the<-

## High and Deep Grounding

## of Six <term>Theosophical</term> Points. <note>Theosophy here refers to "divine wisdom" (from the Greek 'theos' and 'sophia'), a tradition of seeking direct knowledge of the divine and the mysteries of nature.</note>

---

->An open gateway to all mysteries<- <note>original: "Heimlichkeiten," suggesting secrets or hidden interior truths.</note>
->of life, wherein the causes<-
->of all beings are recognized.<-

->Written<-

->by<-

### Jacob Böhme,

->otherwise called the <term>Teutonic</term>.<- <note>Böhme was often called "Philosophus Teutonicus" (the German Philosopher) because he wrote his profound metaphysical works in the German vernacular rather than the academic Latin of his time.</note>

->in the year 1620.<-

---

->Printed in the year of the great salvation brought forth<-

->1730.<-

<summary>This title page introduces Jacob Böhme’s 1620 treatise on the metaphysical "points" or foundations of existence, positioned as a key to understanding the hidden mysteries of life. This specific edition was printed in 1730, over a century after the author's death.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Theosophy, Teutonic Philosopher, mysticism, metaphysics, spiritual origins, German philosophy</keywords>

# Index of the Points and their Chapters.

The Author's Preface to the Reader. <margin>page 1</margin>

## The 1st Point.

Concerning the growth of the three principles, <note>In Böhme’s thought, the "Principles" are the fundamental forces of the universe: the dark fire-world (wrath), the light-world (love), and the physical world we inhabit.</note> what kind of tree or life each bears within and out of itself. How the ground of nature should be explored and known.

Chapter 1: Concerning the first growth and life from the first principle. <note>original Latin: "Principio"</note> To be weighed and considered as if it stood alone, and were not mixed with the others, regarding what its power might be: not to think that it exists uniquely in one figure or creature; but so that one learns to investigate and establish the <term>Center of Nature<note>original Latin: "Centrum Naturae" — a term referring to the point where spiritual forces manifest into physical reality</note></term>, and learns to distinguish the Divine Being from Nature. <margin>2</margin>

Chapter 2: Concerning the property of the Principle, what the Principle is, or what all three are. <margin>16</margin>

## The 2nd Point.

Concerning the mixed tree of Evil and Good: or the life of the three principles within one another, how they unite and agree.
Chapter 3. <margin>28</margin>

## The 3rd Point.

Concerning the origin of the adversity of growth, in which life becomes conflicted within itself.
Chapter 4. <margin>33</margin>

<meta>This page transitions from the table of contents into the formal preface and symbolic frontispiece of Jacob Böhme’s "Six Theosophical Points." It establishes the intended audience: not those living in their animalistic impulses, but those striving for spiritual rebirth.</meta>

<header>VI.</header>

# SIX THEOSOPHICAL POINTS.
<note>original: "SEX PUNCTA THEOSOPHICA" — The title uses Latin, which was common for scholarly or mystical treatises in 17th-century Germany.</note>
### Or
# Concerning Six Theosophical Points.

### The Author’s Preface to the Reader.

We have not written this work for the unreasoning beasts, who possess a human form in their outward appearance but are evil and wild beasts in their spirit and image, which is revealed and displayed in their qualities. Rather, we write for those in the human image, who are sprouting out of the beastly image into a human likeness that belongs in God’s kingdom, and who would gladly live and grow in that human likeness—in the true human being. These people are often and greatly hindered by the contrary life, and thus remain stuck in a mixed life, <note>Böhme refers to the "mixed life" as the state of existence where good and evil, light and darkness, are entangled before spiritual purification.</note> struggling toward the birth of the holy life. It is for them that we have written these scripts; and we tell them not to regard it as impossible to recognize and know such mysteries. 

We offer them this comparison to consider: Imagine a life that had grown out of all other lives and was mixed; but another life grew within it, out of all those lives, which—even though it grew from them—was free from all those other lives, yet still stood within the very essences <note>original: "Essentien"</note> of those lives. This same other new life would be illuminated with the light, and only within itself, so that it could behold all the other lives; yet they (the other lives) could not behold or grasp the new life. So it is with everyone who, out of the mixed life of Good and Evil, is born again into and out of God. This same new image, born in the life of God, beholds all natural lives, and nothing is foreign or difficult to it, for it beholds only its own root from which it has grown—just as we can recognize how a beautiful flower grows out of the wild earth, which does not look like the earth at all,

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: explains</meta>

---

<header>VI.</header>

<image-desc>A complex mystical and symbolic engraving within a double-line border. At the top, a fluttering banner contains the text "Six POINTS." Below it is a large radiant circle representing a cosmic or divine eye, filled with many smaller eyes all directed toward the center. 

The top half of the circle's outer edge is inscribed with the words "THE GROUNDLESS. <note>original: "UNGRUND" — Böhme's term for the absolute, the nothingness, or the primal depth before all created being.</note> ETERNAL FREEDOM." The bottom half is inscribed with "GOD OUTSIDE NATURE AND CREATURE." 

Inside the radiant circle, there is a central vertical axis of overlapping geometric and symbolic elements:
1. At the top, a triangle within a circle containing the letters "I", "E", "S", "V", "A" <note>A variant of "JESUA" or Jesus, used as a sacred name.</note> arranged around a central "S".
2. In the middle, a large letter "H" <note>Likely representing "Hauch" (breath) or the Holy Spirit.</note> is positioned below the first circle and above a crescent shape. To the left is a small sun symbol and to the right is a small crescent moon symbol.
3. The inner circle is flanked by the text "MIRROR" on the left and "OF WISDOM" on the right.
4. At the bottom of the axis, a dark globe or sphere contains a triangle pointing downwards, within which are the letters "V" and "R" <note>Abbreviation for "Ur-" or original source.</note>.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues Böhme's preface, using the metaphor of a lily growing among thorns to describe the spiritual rebirth of man. It then transitions into the first formal chapter, which begins an analysis of the "Three Principles"—the foundational layers of existence in Böhme’s mystical cosmology.</meta>

<header>VI. Concerning Six Points. 1. Chap. 1.</header>

...but [the lily] declares by its beauty the potentiality <note>original: "Vermögenheit" — here referring to the hidden capacity or inherent power of the earth to manifest divine beauty despite its fallen state.</note> of the earth; and how it is mixed with good and evil: So also is every person who is born again from the animal, wild, earthly nature and quality back into the true image of GOD. To those who are now such a growth, and who press toward the beautiful lily in the Kingdom of GOD, and who stand in the process of birth, we have written this book, so that they may strengthen their essences <note>original: "Essentien" — the fundamental spiritual substances or qualities of a being.</note> therein, flourish in the life of GOD, grow in the tree of Paradise, and bear fruit. Since all children of GOD who grow in this tree are branches on that very same tree, we have wished to communicate our sap, fragrance, and essence to our fellow-branches and limbs in our tree, in which we all stand and from which we all grow; so that our tree of Paradise might become large, and we might rejoice with one another, and that each limb and branch might help to cover the others from the storm. We give this to all children of this growth in this tree to kindly consider, and we commend ourselves into their love and growth.

# The First Point.

## Concerning the growth of the three principles, <note>Böhme’s "Three Principles" are the three realms or dimensions of reality: the dark world (fire/will), the light world (love/spirit), and the outer world (nature/matter).</note> what kind of tree or life each one bears in and of itself. How one should investigate and know the ground of nature.

### The 1st Chapter.

Concerning the first growth and life, from the first principle.

This is to be considered and contemplated as if it stood alone, and were not mixed with the other, in order to see what its capacity might be. This is not to be thought of as if it were so solitary within a single figure or creature; rather, it is so that one might learn to search and establish the Center of Nature <note>original: "Centrum Naturæ" — the internal point of origin where natural life and divine energy first interact.</note> and learn to distinguish the Divine Being from
->nature.<-

<meta>catchword: Sum-</meta>
<meta>translator note: The catchword "Sum-" likely refers to "Summaria" or "Summary," indicating the start of the next section's overview.</meta>

<summary>Böhme uses the metaphor of a growing tree and a blooming lily to describe the collective spiritual development of "Children of God." He then introduces the First Principle, beginning a deep inquiry into how the Divine Essence distinguishes itself from the "Center of Nature."</summary>
<keywords>rebirth, image of God, tree of Paradise, three principles, Center of Nature, essence, spiritual growth</keywords>

<meta>This page provides a condensed outline or summary of the first chapter of Böhme’s "Six Theosophical Points." It serves as a table of contents for the metaphysical arguments that follow, moving from the primal nothingness (Ungrund) to the manifestation of the Trinity and the division of nature into light and dark principles.</meta>

<header>P. 1. Cap. 1. Theosophical Points.</header>

->Summaries.<-

Life stands in the will, in which there is a hidden fire. §. 1. 2. For without fire the will is powerless and mute; 3. 4. but in the essence, it is a being <note>original: "Wesen" — can mean being, substance, or essence depending on context.</note>. 5. In the will, desire takes its origin. 6. The 1st will is an *Ungrund* <note>original: "Ungrund" — Böhme's term for the "Groundless," the absolute abyss or void that precedes all existence and even the manifestation of God.</note>, and like a mirror or an eye, 7. 8. and the eternal *Ungrund* outside nature is a will; 9. but the image is free from the mirror. 10. In the eternal Wisdom of GOD, all things have stood hidden from eternity, from which she has her seeing. 11. For no spirit is without seeing, just as no seeing is without spirit; but the seeing creates the will, and the will creates the ground, and gives birth to its heart, 12. 13. and goes out with what is grasped. 14. The Father is the eternal will, who grasps the eye as the mirror; the Son is what is grasped in the mirror; 15. the Holy Spirit is the finder, 16. and Wisdom <note>referring to Sophia, the mirror of divine ideas.</note> is the spoken word. 17. The will-spirit has no ground. 18. The being of the Godhead is an eye or a wheel, 19. and dwells in itself in the *Ungrund*. 20. This threefold spirit is indivisible. 21. 22. In the Virgin <note>The Heavenly Sophia/Wisdom.</note> the eternal fire or principle is hidden, 23. in which mirror the image has appeared. 24. The 1st Principle is magical in its origin; 25. 26. its center is fire, which cannot exist without essence. 27. It comes from the desire of the will, which craves the revelation of its heart, 28-30. in order to reveal itself from its own ground in the light. 31. 32. Thus the Father first births nature, and then the 2nd Principle, 33. 34. for He desires light, 35. which cannot be birthed in any other way than through the fire. 36. Fire and light are indeed one, but in two lives and qualities <note>original: "Qualen" — Böhme uses this to mean both "qualities" and "agonies" or "source-pangs."</note>. 37. Desire creates darkness; 38. thus the will wants to be free from the darkness. 39. The more it now draws into itself, the greater the darkness becomes. 40. This 1st will is deeper than the center of the Word. 41. Origin of substantiality. 42. The 1st form is harshness, the 2nd bitterness. 43. The 3rd is anxiety. 44. Thus two wills are understood. 45. One in wrath; this is the 1st will of nature: the other in freedom; this is the 2nd will of Divine nature. 46. Effect of the 1st will, 47. as of the Eternal Mind. 48. Is the life of nature without understanding. 49. The 2nd will desires light, 50. and has freedom within itself. 51. This [will] desires revelation and properties. 52. 53. Thus the Father desires nature; 54. and in the "shock" <note>original: "Schreck" or "Schrack" — the sudden flash or terror that occurs when the dark desire meets the light, causing a transformation.</note> two beings arise. 55. This is the origin of nature. 56. The shock causes fire and light, as love, 57. which gives food to the fire. 58. Out of both wills goes the Holy Spirit into gentleness, 59. 60. and in the light dwells the Holy Trinity, in Sophia, 61. 62. in whose colors and virtues the Holy Spirit has opened everything. 63. The two beings in the shock divide into fire and light, 64. but the shock remains standing in itself; 65. there, also, mercy originates. 66. Consequently, there remains on one part a deadly life, 67. and on the other part, life goes out from death. 68. Thus the Father is found in the miracle: the Son in the power. 69. The gall <note>bitterness or bile, used here as a metaphysical quality of friction or heat.</note> causes the fire, and out of the fire, the life: 70. but out of the fire-life, the spirit, 71. which has its property in the light. 72. Thus fierce death is a cause of life, 73. just as this [life] is a cause of joy. 74.

<summary>This page lists 74 numbered points summarizing the first chapter's teaching on the birth of God and nature from the "Ungrund" (the Groundless). It explains how the divine will moves from a primal void into fire and light, establishing the "Three Principles" of existence.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page begins the detailed exposition of Chapter 1 of the "Six Theosophical Points." While the previous page provided a summary of these arguments, this page delves into the metaphysical mechanics of how the "will" relates to "essence" and the "Ungrund" (Groundless). It establishes the priority of the will as the father of all manifestation.</meta>

<header>VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 1.</header>

1. We see and find that every life is substantial <note>original: "essentialisch" — refers to having an actual essence or inner reality.</note>; and we also find that it stands in the will, for the will is the driving force of the essences <note>original: "Essentien" — the active, living powers that make up a being.</note>.

2. And thus we must imagine it as if a hidden fire lay within the will, wherein the will would continually raise itself toward the fire, seeking to awaken and ignite it.

3. For we understand that every will, without the awakening of the fiery essences, is an impotence <note>original: "Unvermögenheit"</note>, just as if it were mute and without life, containing no feeling, understanding, nor substantiality within it; for it resembles only a shadow without being <note>original: "Wesen"</note>, because it has no guide, but rather it sinks away and allows itself to be driven and led like a dead thing, as can be perceived in a shadow which is cast without having its own essence.

4. Thus, an in-essential <note>original: "un-essentialischer"</note> will is a mute being without comprehension or life; and yet it is a figure in the bottomless, eternal Nothing, for it hangs upon corporeal <note>original: "corporalischen"</note> things.

5. Just as the will without essence is mute and without being, so too in the essence it is a being and an image according to the essences, which is formed according to those essences; for the life of the will is born out of the essences.

6. Thus, life is the Son of the essences, and the will—in which the figure of life stands—is the Father of the essences, for no essence may arise without a will; for within the will, desire takes its origin <note>original: "geurständet"</note>, and within that desire, the essences spring forth <note>original: "urständen"</note>.

7. Since, then, the first will is a Groundless <note>original: "Ungrund" — Böhme's core term for the absolute abyss or void that precedes all existence and even God's self-manifestation.</note>, to be regarded as an eternal Nothing; we therefore recognize it as being like a mirror, in which one sees his own image, like a life, and yet it is not a life, but rather a figure of life and of the image within life.

8. Thus we recognize the eternal Groundless, outside of nature, to be like a mirror: for it is like an eye that sees, and yet carries nothing in its seeing with which it sees; for the seeing is without substance, even though it is born out of substance, namely out of the essential life.

9. Thus it is perceptible to us that the eternal Groundless outside of nature is a will, like an eye, wherein nature...

<vocab>substantial/essential, essences, impotence/powerlessness, Groundless, originated/took root, to spring forth, corporeal</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the relationship between the will and essence, arguing that the will is the father of all being and requires a 'fiery' awakening to move beyond a mere shadow-like existence. Böhme introduces the concept of the Ungrund (Groundless) as an eternal mirror or eye that exists outside of nature and substance.</summary>
<keywords>Will, Essence, Fire, Ungrund (Groundless), Mirror of Wisdom, Origin of Life, Divine Anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the nature of the "will" and its relationship to "essence." It specifically transitions from the idea of the will as a shadow or figure to the metaphor of the mirror, explaining how the "Ungrund" (Groundless) begins to perceive itself and form a "ground" or center of being.</meta>

<header>Part 1. Chapter 1. Theosophical Points.</header>

lies hidden therein: like a hidden fire that does not burn, which is there and yet is not there. It is not a spirit, but a form of the spirit, like the reflection <note>original: "Schiemen" — a shadowy image or reflection lacking physical substance.</note> in a mirror, where every form of a spirit is seen in the reflection or mirror, and yet is nothing that the eye or mirror sees; rather, its seeing is within itself, for there is nothing before it that could be deeper. It is like a mirror, which is a container for the sight of nature, and yet it does not grasp nature, nor does nature grasp the reflection of the image in the mirror.

10. And thus one is free from the other, and yet the mirror is truly the container of the image: it holds the image, and yet is powerless against the reflection, for it cannot retain the reflection. For when the image steps away from the mirror, the mirror is a clear sheen <note>original: "Glast" — a shining luster, radiance, or the reflective quality of glass.</note>, and its sheen is a nothingness, and yet all the forms of nature lie hidden within it like a nothing, and it is indeed real, but not substantial <note>original: "essentialisch" — relating to "essence" or "Wesen"; here it means lacking actualized being or matter.</note>.

11. Thus we are to recognize and understand this regarding the hidden, eternal Wisdom of <term>God</term> <note>God is presented here as the ultimate source of both the "Groundless" and the "Will."</note>, which likens itself to an eternal eye without being; She <note>Böhme traditionally personifies Wisdom (Sophia) as a feminine entity.</note> is the <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrund" — a foundational term in Böhme’s thought referring to the absolute, undifferentiated nothingness or "abyss" that precedes all created existence.</note>, and yet sees all; everything has stood hidden within her from eternity, from which she has her seeing. But she is not substantial, just as the sheen in the mirror is not substantial, which nonetheless grasps everything that appears before it.

12. And then, secondly, we are to understand the eternal Will, which is also without being, in the same way regarding the Spirit of <term>God</term>; for no seeing exists without Spirit, and no Spirit exists without seeing. And thus we understand that the seeing appears out of the Spirit, which is its eye and mirror, wherein the Will is manifest; for the seeing creates a Will, in which the <term>Groundless</term> of the depth without number knows not how to find any ground or goal; so its mirror goes into itself and creates a ground within itself, which is a Will.

13. Thus the mirror of the eternal eye appears in the Will, and brings forth for itself another eternal ground within itself: this is its center <note>original: "Centrum"</note> or heart, from which the seeing always originates from eternity, and through which the Will becomes active and leading—namely, in that which the center brings forth.

14. For everything is grasped in the Will, and is a

...essence, which eternally originates within itself in the eternal <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrund" — a central concept in Böhme’s thought, referring to the absolute, undifferentiated nothingness or "Abyss" from which God and the universe emerge.</note>, enters into itself, and creates a center within itself, grasping itself within itself; yet it goes out from itself with that which is grasped, reveals itself in the sheen <note>original: "Glasse" — the reflective luster or "glass" of the divine mirror mentioned in previous sections.</note> of the eye, and thus appears out of the essence within itself and out of itself. It is its own possession, and yet compared to nature it is as a nothing (understand this as speaking in relation to tangible, physical being), though it is actually everything, and everything originates from it.

15. And here within we understand the eternal essence of the <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: "Dreyheit" — literally "threeness," referring to the Triune nature of God.</note> of the Deity, along with the groundless Wisdom: For the eternal Will, which grasps the eye as the mirror, wherein the eternal seeing stands as his Wisdom, is Father; and that which is eternally grasped into Wisdom—where the grasping forms a ground or *Center* in itself out of the Groundless into the ground—is Son or Heart; for it is the Word of Life, or his essentiality, wherein the Will appears with the sheen.

16. And the "entering-into-self" toward the *Center* of the ground is Spirit, for he is the Finder, who eternally finds where nothing is; he goes out again from the *Center* of the ground and seeks in the Will. Now the mirror of the eye, as the Wisdom of the Father and the Son, becomes manifest. And so Wisdom stands before the Spirit of God, who reveals the Groundless within her; for her virtue—wherein the colors of the wonders appear—is revealed from the Father of the eternal Will through the *Center* of his Heart or ground, with the outgoing Spirit.

17. For she is the Outspoken <note>original: "Ausgesprochene" — that which has been expressed or spoken forth, like a Word manifest.</note>, which the Father speaks out from the *Center* of the Heart with the Holy Spirit, and she stands in the Divine formations and images, in the sight of the Holy Trinity of God: but as a virgin without giving birth; she does not give birth to the colors and figures that appear in her and stand manifest in the ground and essence: rather, it is all together an eternal *Magic* <note>original: "Magia" — Böhme uses this term to describe the spiritual power that brings forms into being without labor or substance.</note>, and dwells with the *Center* of the Heart within itself, and with the Spirit from the *Center* it goes out of itself, and reveals itself in the eye of the Virginal Wisdom into infinity.

18. For just as the essence of the Deity has no ground from which it might originate or come forth; so also the Will-Spirit has no ground where he might rest, where a place or

19. ### The Second Text.

20. Thus we understand that the Divine Essence in Trinity dwells within itself in the <term>Groundless</term>, and yet generates for itself a ground within itself as the eternal Word or Heart, which is the *Center* or goal of rest within the Deity; and yet this is not understood as a tangible substance, but as a Triune Spirit, where each person is the cause of the others' birth.

21. And this same Triune Spirit is not measurable, divisible, or fathomable <note>original: "gründlich" — meaning having a bottom, limit, or physical basis.</note>; for no place is found for it, and it is at once the <term>Groundless</term> of eternity, which generates itself within itself in the ground: and no place or spot can be imagined or found where the Spirit of the Trinity is not present and within all being; yet it is hidden from that being, dwelling within itself as an essence that fills everything at once, yet does not dwell *within* created existence, but rather has an essence of its own within itself: just as we are to contemplate the ground and the <term>Groundless</term>, and how the two are understood in relation to each other.

22. Thus we understand eternity: (1) How it was before the times of the creation of this world. (2) And understand further, what the Divine Essence is in itself without a *Principle* <note>original: "Principium" — a Latin term Böhme uses to denote a fundamental source or a starting point of a reality.</note>. (3) What the eternal beginning is in the <term>Groundless</term>, and the eternal end in its own, self-born ground, as the *Center* to the Word, which Word the Cen-

24. 25. And it is now to be understood by us that the first Principle in its primal state is magical, for it is born in the desire of the Will: therefore its longing and counter-will to bring forth is also magical, namely to bring forth the second Principle.

26. And since it is understood in the first and second Principles only as a spirit, without a tangible essence; there is a longing to further bring forth the third Principle, where the spirit of the two Principles might rest and reveal itself in an image. <note>The third Principle represents the physical, created universe where the spiritual tensions of the first two reach a visible "rest."</note>

27. And although it is true that every Principle has its own center; the first Principle nevertheless stands in magical <term>torment</term> <note>original: "Qual" — in this context, it refers to a dynamic, agonizing tension or the "source-quality" that drives the hunger for existence.</note>, and its center is fire, which cannot exist without essence; therefore its hunger and desire is for essence.

28. And concerning the first Principle, if we speak of it alone (though it is not solitary), it is to be understood that the groundless Will in the Center of the Groundless—in which the eternal Word is always born from eternity—is desiring; for the Will desires the Center as the Word or Heart.

29. And secondly, it desires that the Heart might be revealed; for in the Groundless there is no revelation, but an eternal Nothing, a stillness without essence or colors, nor any <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "Tugend" — here meaning power, effective force, or divine "strength."</note>; but in this desire, colors,

30. Thus we are to understand the essence of the deepest Godhead without and outside of nature.

31. 32. Now the Heart of light is pregnant, and the first will of nature is pregnant: and yet neither would be manifest if the <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principium" — the underlying foundation or starting point of a realm of existence.</note> were not born.

33. For it is to be reflected upon thus: that the Father births the first Principle out of the first will, as Nature, which comes to the highest perfection in the fire; and then He births the other Principle, in and out of the other will, to the Word, in that He desires the revelation of the Word in the light of majesty; where the fire of the second Principle in the light of majesty is a fulfillment of the other will: as <term>gentleness</term> <note>original: "Sanftmuth" — the soft, loving quality of the second Principle (Light/Son) that balances the harsh fire of the first.</note>, which is set against the fire of the first Principle, and extinguishes its <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimm" — the fierce, consuming energy of the first Principle.</note>, and places it into an essential being, as into an eternal life, where the fire is hidden in the light, and gives the light its power, strength, and might, where it is then together an eternal bond, and one would be nothing without the other.

...in His heart, that He might appear in Wisdom, and that the whole of God might thus be manifest within Himself, and with the outgoing Spirit from Himself, in the Virgin of His Wisdom <note>The "Virgin of Wisdom" (Sophia) in Böhme's thought represents the divine mirror or passive principle through which God beholds His own wonders.</note>; and that there might be an eternal, perfect joy, delight, and fulfillment in Him.

36. This cannot be brought forth in any other way than through fire, wherein the Will is placed into the deepest sharpness of Omnipotence, becoming consuming in the fire; whereas the light is a gentleness of the birth-giver of the <term>All-essentiality</term> <note>original: "Allwesenheit" — the state of containing all possible being or essences.</note>.

37. So the fire must also have a birth-giver in its origin and life: it now appears in two lives and <term>pangs</term> <note>original: "Qualen" — Böhme uses this term to describe the productive tension or "source-quality" that drives the birth of existence.</note>. And they are rightly called two <term>Principles</term> <note>original: "Principia" — the fundamental modes of divine reality: the dark/fire world and the light/love world.</note>, though it is also only One, but two kinds of pang in one Being; and because of the pang, it is regarded as two beings, as we can conceive of in fire and light.

38. So we now contemplate the Desire, and find that it is a rigorous attraction, like an eternal rising and moving: for it draws itself into itself, and <term>impregnates</term> itself, so that out of the thin freedom, where there is Nothing, a darkness arises; for the desiring Will becomes thick and full from the drawing-in, though it is still nothing but darkness.

39. Now the first Will wants to be free from the darkness, for it desires light, and yet cannot reach it thus; for the greater the desire for freedom is, the greater becomes the attraction and the sting of the essences, which originated in the drawing (or desiring).

40. Thus the Will draws ever more into itself, and its impregnation becomes ever greater; and yet the darkness cannot grasp the <term>Center of the Word</term>, or Heart of the <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: "Drey-Zahl"</note>, for that same Center is a degree deeper within itself, and is yet a bond.

41. But the first Will, in which the impregnation of nature originates, is still deeper than the Center of the Word, for it originates from the eternal <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrunde" — the abyss or primordial void before creation.</note> or Nothing: And thus the Center of the Heart is enclosed in the middle, as the first Will of the Father works toward the <term>fire-birth</term>.

42. Thus we are to recognize that in the rigorous attraction, a completely rigorous substance and being comes to be; then the essentiality originates from eternity, for the drawing gives the sting, and that which is drawn gives hardness, <term>matter</term> <note>original: "Materiam"</note>...

...out of the Nothingness, a substance and essence. Now the sting of the drawing dwells in that same essence; it pricks and breaks; and all this comes from the desiring Will, which draws.

43. Thus we recognize two forms of nature here: first, "Harshness"—which is the Desire—and then the "Sting," which causes a breaking and pricking within that Desire, from which feeling originates. This is "Bitterness," the second form of nature, and is the cause and origin of the essences in nature.

44. Since the first Will is neither satisfied with this nor set at rest, but is instead placed into a very great <term>anguish</term> <note>original: "Angst" — in Böhme's vocabulary, this implies a literal "narrowness" or "constriction" that creates intense pressure or anxiety.</note> (for it desires the freedom in the light, yet there is no radiance in the freedom), it now falls into a terrible anguish. It raises its desire so strongly toward freedom that the anguish—like a dying or a sinking through death—leads its Will into freedom, out of the breaking, pricking, and powerful drawing.

45. Thus we understand the Will in two ways here: one that rises up in fierceness toward the birth of the <term>wrath-fire</term> <note>original: "Grimmen-Feuer" — the harsh, consuming aspect of existence often associated with the first Principle.</note>; the other, which <term>directs its imagination</term> <note>original: "imaginiret" — a technical term meaning to creatively focus one's inner power or "image" into a new state.</note> toward the Center of the Word, and sinks out of the anguish—as if through a death—into the free life. In this way, it brings a life out of the tormenting anguish with it into freedom, so that the eternal <term>Groundless</term> <note>original: "Ungrund" — the bottomless, non-spatial origin of all things before manifestation.</note> is recognized as a life, and out of the Nothingness, an eternal life arises.

46. When the first path of the Will rises toward the birth of fire, we recognize it as the first nature—as the Father’s nature in fierce wrath; but the other entrance of the Will into freedom, into the Center of the Heart, we recognize as the Divine Nature—as the life in the light, in the power of the Godhead.

47. Thus it is now clear what the first Will works and does toward the fire: namely, severity, hardness, bitterness, and great anguish, which is the third form of nature. For anguish is like the center where life and the Will eternally originate. The Will wants to be free from the great anguish, yet it cannot: it wants to flee, but is held fast by the harshness. And the greater the Will’s desire to flee becomes, the greater becomes the bitter sting of the essences and multiplicity.

48. As it then cannot flee, nor rise above itself,

51. Thus that same other Will now has freedom within itself in the Word of Life, and the Will of anguish in the sharpness of Nature desires freedom, so that freedom might become manifest in the anguish of the fierce Mind.

52. From this then the anguish also arises: that the first Will wants to be free from the dark <term>harshness</term> <note>original: "Herbichkeit" — an astringent, contracting quality.</note>, and freedom desires revelation, for it cannot find itself within itself without sharpness or torment; for the Will of freedom, which is called Father, desires to reveal itself, and that it cannot do without properties.

53. Therefore he is desiring the properties which originate in the fire within the essences in the anguish, in order to reveal his wonders, power, and colors therewith, which cannot be without Nature.

...and is Freedom itself) of nature, and nature desires with a great longing for Freedom, so that she may be released from the torment of anguish; and she receives Freedom within her sharp wrath, in the <term>Imagination</term> <note>original: "Imagination" — for Böhme, this is the creative, formative power of the will to "image" or desire something into being.</note>, from which she is startled like a flash of lightning; for it is a shock of joy that she is released from the torment of anguish.

55. And in this shock, two beings arise: one mortal and one living, to be understood as follows.

56. The Will, called the Father, who possesses Freedom within himself, begets himself in nature in such a way that he is capable of nature, and that he is nature’s omnipotence.

57. The shock of his nature is a kindler of the fire; for when the dark anguish—as the truly earnest, severe being—receives Freedom into itself, it transforms itself within the shock inside Freedom into a lightning flash. This flash catches Freedom as <term>Gentleness</term> <note>original: "Sanftmuth" — the peaceful, loving quality of the second spiritual principle, associated with the Son.</note>. There the sting of death is broken, and in nature arises the other Will of the Father, which He had fashioned for himself before nature in the <term>Mirror of Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Spiegel der Weisheit" — the eternal "Sophia" or divine mirror that reflects God's potential wonders before they are manifest.</note>, as his Heart of Love, which is the desire of love and full of joy.

58. For in the Father’s Will, the fire is thus born, to which the second Will gives the power of gentleness and love. The fire takes this <term>quality of love</term> <note>original: "Liebe-Qual" — Böhme often uses "Qual" to mean both "torment" and "vibrant source/quality" (related to the German word *Quelle*), suggesting an active, driving property.</note> into its essence; this is now its food, so that it burns, and out of its consuming nature—out of the shock—it gives forth the joy-filled spirit.

59. That is to say, here the <term>Holy Spirit</term>, who in the <term>original state</term> <note>original: "Urstand" — the foundational or primordial condition before manifest nature.</note> before nature is the Will-Spirit of the Father, becomes manifest. Here He receives the power of wonders and thus goes forth from the Father (as from the first will toward nature) and from the second Will in nature out of the fire (as out of the shock). He goes forth joy-filled, within the active quality of love, into the substantiality of gentleness.

60. For gentleness has now also become "desiring" through the property of the fire, and this Desire draws the joy-filled gentleness into itself. This is now the Water of Eternal Life, which the fire drinks, and from which it gives the Light of Majesty.

61. And in that Light now dwells the Will of the Father and of the Son; and the <term>Holy Spirit</term> is the life within it. He now opens the power of the gentle substantiality in the Light, so that <unclear>...</unclear> and colors, wonders and virtues.

It explains how the dark, consuming fire of nature is transformed into a source of light and "gentle substantiality" through divine love.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Böhme's discourse on the "Shock" (Schreck) of creation, explaining how the divine nature divides into two distinct worlds: the dark world of fire and the light world of majesty. It further explores the role of the "Virgin Wisdom" as the mirror through which these spiritual realities are reflected and manifested.</meta>

->## VI. Concerning Six Points. Part 1, Chapter 1.<-

62. And this same is called the **Virgin Wisdom** <note>original: "Jungfräuliche Weisheit" — In Böhme's thought, Sophia or Wisdom is a passive, feminine principle that reflects God’s glory without creating on her own.</note>, for she is not a procreator, nor does she open anything of herself; rather, the **Holy Spirit** is the opener of her wonders. She is his garment and beautiful ornament, and she holds within her the wonders, colors, and virtues of the Divine world; she is the house of the **Holy Trinity**, and the ornament of the Divine and Angelic worlds.

63. And in her colors and virtues, the Holy Spirit has opened the choirs of the angels, as well as all the wonders of created things, all of which have been beheld from eternity in Wisdom—though indeed without **essence** <note>original: "Wesen" — here meaning substantial or physical existence.</note>. Yet they were in Wisdom as if in a mirror, according to their **figures**; these figures, through the movement of the Father, have passed into essence and into a creaturely state, all according to the wonders of Wisdom.

64. Thus, understand us now concerning the other being: how in the **shock** <note>original: "Schrack" (Schreck) — the sudden transition or "flash" where the dark will of nature meets the light of God.</note>, nature divides into two beings (as mentioned above): one with the Father’s will into the fire, as **(1) into the Fire-World**; and from the Father’s other created or in-himself-born will, **(2) into the Majestic Light-World**.

65. And the other being is the house of the shock within itself: in death, in the darkness of the hostile torment, which must remain so, that there may be an eternal longing in that same anguish to be free from the torment; for that same longing makes the first will into nature, eternally desiring to come to the aid of its being. From this, Mercy originates within the Father’s will, which enters into the anguish with **Freedom** <note>original: "Freyheit" — the state of God before or beyond the constraints of nature.</note>; but it does not wish to remain in the anguish, but rather goes out in the fire into the **torment of love** <note>original: "Liebe Qual" — Böhme uses "Qual" (torment/source) to describe the intense, vibrating energy of both divine love and natural wrath.</note>.

66. That is, his other will, as his **Heart**, goes out from within Him as a wellspring of love and mercy; from this, Mercy has its origin, so that there is a pity for the misery and wretchedness, and a compassion. Therein the Father’s will (which is nonetheless free) reveals itself in the **wrath of nature**, so that the wrath is softened.

67. But nonetheless, the anxious wheel of wrath remains on its own part; for in the shock, a killing occurs—not indeed a silent death, but a **deadly life**; it resembles the most bitter essence, like a **parting-water** <note>original: "Scheidewasser" — literally nitric acid; an alchemical solvent used to separate metals, symbolizing a harsh, corrosive force.</note> or poison within itself. For such a thing must exist if the **Center of Nature** <note>original: "Centrum Naturæ"</note> is to consist eternally.

<meta>catchword: 68. And</meta>

<summary>This page describes the dual nature of God's manifestation: the dark, agonizing "Fire-World" and the "Majestic Light-World," mediated by the "Virgin Wisdom" and the "Shock" of divine transition. It explains how the Father's will produces Mercy to soften the natural wrath, even as the "wheel of wrath" remains necessary for existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "shock" or "flash" of creation from page 14. Having established how the divine nature divides into the dark world of fire and the light world of majesty, Böhme now explores how life specifically emerges from the tension between death and fire.</meta>

->### Part 1. Chapter 1. Concerning Theosophical Points.<-

68. And on the other hand, life proceeds out of death, and death must therefore be a cause of life; for if there were no such poisonous, fierce **torment** <note>original: "Qual" — Böhme uses this term to mean both "torment" and "source-quality" (from the German *Quelle*), implying a painful but productive tension.</note>, the fire could not be born, and there would be no fire-sharpness or **essence** <note>original: "Essens" — a Germanized form of the Latin *essentia*, meaning substantial being.</note>; thus there would also be no light, nor any finding of life.

69. The first will, which is called the **Father**, is thus found in the wonder. And the other will, which is called the **Son**, is thus found in power. From this, the **Kingdom of Joy** <note>original: "Freudenreich"</note> originates; for if there were no woe, there would be no Kingdom of Joy: for the Kingdom of Joy consists in this: that life is redeemed from **anguish** <note>original: "Angst" — in Böhme’s system, this refers to the "narrowness" or constriction of the dark properties before they break through into light.</note>, even though life only originates in this way.

70. Therefore, creatures have poison within them for the sake of their life, acting as a **gall** <note>original: "Galle" — bile or gall was traditionally associated with bitterness and anger, which Böhme sees as the necessary "friction" that ignites the fire of life.</note>. The gall is the cause of there being a mobility, so that life may originate, for it causes the fire in the heart; and the true life is the fire, but it is not the **figure** of life.

71. Out of the fire-life, the true **spirit** first arises; it proceeds from the fire into the light. It is as free from the fire as the air is, which—though it originates from the fire—remains free from it.

72. For the true spirit—or the spirit within man which is born from the soul-fire—has its property in the light of life that burns from the fire; for it arises out of death, it proceeds out of the **dying**. The hostile torment has remained behind it in the fire, and continues beneath the fire, in the cause of the fire, as in fierce death.

73. Thus, fierce death is a root of life. And here, you humans, consider your own death, and also the death of **Christ**, who has born us again out of dying through the fire of **GOD**: for out of dying, the free life is born. Whatever can proceed out of dying is redeemed from death and the fierce torment. This is now his Kingdom of Joy: that no fierce torment is in him any longer; it has remained behind him in the dying, and thus life reaches the eternal freedom out of death, where there is no more fear or terror; for in life, the **shock** <note>original: "Schrack" (Schreck) — the terrifying "flash" or "crack" that occurs when the dark will transitions into the light world.</note> is broken.

74. The true life is a power of joys, an ever

66. That is, his other will—which is his **Heart**—goes forth in Him as a fountain of love and mercy. For such a thing must exist if the **Center of Nature** <note>original: "Centrum Naturæ"</note> is to stand eternally.

# Part 1. Chapter 2. Concerning Theosophical Points.

...Wrath into Love-Desire and the Kingdom of Joy. 44. Fruits of the Light, 45. are the works of the New Man. 46. The Principle of Light gives fertility and resists the <term>Turba</term> <note>original: "Turba" — the spirit of destruction or "commotion" that breaks down natural forms.</note>; it resists the devil and evil men. 47. The third principle, by contrast, brings forth good and evil fruit. 48. Hence the constant struggle between Love and <term>Wrath</term> <note>original: "Zorn" — the fierce, judging, or raging aspect of nature.</note>. In the same place: Cold wants to be master, 49. as does heat: In the same place: Thus the sun wants to rule over heat and cold; 50. and the fire gives the strength of the wind. In the same place: Accordingly, the Outer Principle is a constant war. 51. From this comes the growth of fruits, 52. and the malice of animals. 53. Whichever world man makes master within himself, that one remains master eternally: and the <term>mind</term> <note>original: "Gemüth" — the deep, underlying disposition of the soul.</note> can stand by whichever principle it chooses. 54. 55.

A <term>principle</term> <note>original: "Principium" — a fundamental source or origin of being.</note> is that in which a life and mobility is found where there was none; fire is a principle with its own property, and light is also a principle with its own property, for it is born out of the fire, and yet it is not the property of the fire; it also has its own life within itself, but the fire is the cause of it, and the <term>fierce anguish</term> <note>original: "grimmige Angst" — the tension or "squeeze" of nature that occurs before the light breaks through.</note> is a cause of them both.

2. But the will toward the anguish, which causes the nature of anguish and is called the Father, cannot be searched out: We only search out how He leads Himself into the highest perfection, into the essence of the Holy Trinity, and how He reveals Himself in three principles, and how the <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Essenz" — spiritual substance or being-ness.</note> of every <term>quality</term> <note>original: "Qual" — a central term for Böhme, playing on "quality" (Qualität) and "torment" (Qual); it refers to the driving source or "springing" of a thing's nature.</note> originates; what essence is, from which life with the senses originates, and the wonder of all beings. See p. 33.

3. Thus we recognize the third principle, as the quality of this world, with the stars and elements, as a creaturely thing [arising] out of the wonders of the eternal Wisdom.

4. The third principle reveals both of the first two, although each is manifest in itself; yet the eternal Being willed to reveal Himself in His wonders—which were beheld in Wisdom—in such a property as accords with the ground of eternity, following the quality of wrath and love; and He has created everything into a creaturely and figurative being, according to the eternal origin of good and evil: as is evident before our eyes, that in this world there is both good and evil, for which the devils are a great cause, who in their creation [and] fall possess the <term>fierce womb</term> <note>original: "grimme Matricem" — the harsh or "wrathful" matrix of nature from which material things are generated.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Three Principles," focusing on how the "Wrath" of the first principle influenced the creation of the physical world. It explains the origins of matter (earth and stones) as a result of spiritual contraction and tension.</meta>

### VI. Concerning Six Points 1. Chap. 1.
<meta>catchword: 10. How</meta>

...moved more violently in the <term>Wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimm" — the fierce, dark, and foundational fire of God's nature that exists before it is tempered by Light.</note>, because God, according to the property of the Wrath, moved Himself more intensely to cast them out from the Light into the death of fierceness. The heavenly essence was moved along with this, so that much which once stood in freedom was enclosed within the earthly essence.

5. We recognize this in gold and its <term>Tincture</term> <note>original: "Tinctur" — in alchemy and theosophy, the spiritual essence or "soul" of a metal that gives it its color and purity.</note>, which is free from the earthly essence. For it persists in the fire and in all manner of torment; no torment can subdue it, except for the will of God alone. Often, something must occur because of the world’s unworthiness.

6. And when we rightly consider the creation of this world and examine the Spirit of the <term>Third Principle</term> <note>original: "dritten Principii" — the visible, material world, which Böhme sees as a "mirror" or manifestation of the two hidden spiritual worlds of Light and Dark.</note>—that is, the Spirit of the Great World with its stars and elements—we find the qualities of the eternal world mingled together. This is like a great wonder through which God, the highest Good, intended to reveal the eternal wonders that were previously hidden, bringing them into actual being.

7. We find both Good and Evil, and in all things we find the <term>Center of Nature</term> <note>original: "Centrum Naturæ" — the internal point of origin where spiritual forces generate the tension necessary for life.</note>, which acts as the <term>Chamber of Anguish</term> <note>original: "Angst-Kammer" — the state of intense contraction and spiritual "hunger" that precedes the birth of Light.</note>. Primarily, however, we find the Spirit of the Great World in two "torments" <note>original: "Qualen" — refers to qualities or active driving forces.</note>, namely heat and cold. In the cold, we recognize the center of the harsh, sharp Wrath; in the heat, we recognize the source of fire. Yet these two have but one single origin, arising from one another.

8. Fire comes from the Wrath of the cold, and cold comes from the Center of Nature—that is, from the harsh, sharp anguish where the <term>Astringency</term> <note>original: "Herbichkeit" — the harsh, "sour" pulling force that Böhme believes thickens spirit into solid matter.</note> draws so forcefully into itself that it creates "essence" or substance. We can see that during the movement of the Father in the creation, this force produced earth and stones, even though there was no pre-existing matter. There was only His own essence, born in both desires—the Light-world and the Death-world.

9. Whatever the Wrath reached during that movement was formed into the globe of the Earth. This is why we find so many different things in it, both evil and good. It often happens that one can make the best thing out of the worst, because the Center of Nature resides within it. If one brings a substance into the fire, the "pure child" of the eternal essence can be brought forth from it as it is freed from death, just as we see with gold.

It introduces the "Center of Nature" as a place of anguish that generates the forces of heat and cold.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Three Principles," moving from the creation of the physical world to the internal relationship between the spiritual forces of Fire, Light, and Spirit. It defines the first two principles as the "Father" and "Son" and explains how they manifest in the human soul and the material world.</meta>

->P. 1. Chap. 2. Theosophical Points. 19<-
B 2
<meta>catchword: Be-</meta>

10. Although we cannot reach the eternal fire in this world, and therefore we can carry nothing out of this <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principio" — Böhme uses this to describe one of the three fundamental "realms" or "states of being" (Darkness, Light, and the Material World).</note>, that is the deficiency of the eternal fire, which we do not reach except only in the <term>Imagination</term> <note>original: "Imagination" — in this context, it refers to a deep, creative spiritual focus or the power of the mind to "form" reality, rather than just "make-believe."</note>, through which a person has the power to lead life out of death and bring it into Divine <term>Being</term> <note>original: "Wesenheit" — the essential, spiritual substance of God.</note>. This can happen only within a human being; however, whatever is outside of the human being belongs to God and remains until the <term>Renewal</term> <note>original: "Renovation" — the restorative transformation of the world at the end of time.</note> at the end of this age.

11. Thus we give to understand the nature and the property of the <term>Principles</term>. The first <term>Principle</term> stands in the <term>Will-Fire</term> <note>original: "Willen-Feuer" — the foundational, driving spark of divine will that acts as the source of all existence.</note> and is a cause of the other two, as well as of life and understanding, and a preservation of nature, as well as of all the properties of the Father.

12. And the second <term>Principle</term> stands in the Light, as in the fire of <term>Desire</term> <note>original: "Begierde" — the longing or attractive force that gives form to the raw energy of the first principle.</note>. This same Desire creates substance out of the property of the first Principle.

13. The first and second Principles are Father and Son in Eternity; one dwells in the other, and yet each retains his own property. There is no mixing in the <term>Essence</term> <note>original: "Essenz" — the underlying spiritual "stuff" or reality.</note>; rather, one receives the other in Desire, and the Light dwells in the fire’s Desire, so that the property of the fire gives its Desire into the Light, and the Light into the fire.

14. Thus it is One Being and not two, but two properties, where one is not the other, nor can ever become so eternally. Just as the property of the Spirit cannot be the fire or the light, and yet it proceeds from the fire through the light, and could not exist solely from either the fire or the light alone; the fire alone could not produce it, nor could the light, but both produce it. It is the life of both and is only One Being, but three properties, where none is the other—as you see this in fire, light, and air.

15. Thus you also understand the third <term>Principle</term>; it is and has these same properties; it also has fire, light, and spirit, which is air; and it is in all circumstances equal to the eternal Being. But it has a beginning and proceeds from the Eternal; it is a revelation of the Eternal, an awakening, an image, and a likeness of the Eternal. It is not the Eternal itself, but it has become a being within the Eternal.

<summary>Böhme explains the inner mechanics of the Trinity through the "Three Principles," describing the interaction between the Father (Will-Fire) and the Son (Light-Desire). He argues that the material world is a temporal reflection or "revelation" of these eternal spiritual dynamics.</summary>
<keywords>Three Principles, Will-Fire, Desire, Imagination, Trinity, Essence, Third Principle, Revelation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the three Principles. While page 19 focused on the internal spiritual dynamics of Fire and Light, page 20 moves toward a "theosophical physics," explaining how the tangible, material world was formed out of these spiritual forces through the power of "Desire" and "Imagination."</meta>

### VI. Regarding the Six Points: Part 1, Chapter 2.

**Desire:** Desire has revealed itself and led into a **Being** <note>original: "Wesen" — in Böhme’s writing, this refers to a substantial, spiritual "something" that has taken form.</note>, equal to the Eternal.

16. Reason says: God made this world out of nothing. The answer is: There was indeed no **Being** or **matter** <note>original: "Materia"</note> for it that was externally tangible; but there was such a form within the eternal power in the **Will**.

17. The creation of this world happened through an awakening of the **Will-Spirit**: The inner Will, which otherwise stands within itself, has stirred its own nature as the **Center** <note>original: "Centrum" — the point of origin for spiritual forces.</note> which is desiring out of itself, just like the Light, which is pressing out of the Center. Thus, the Center has grasped a **Being** out of itself in its **Desire**; that is, it has grasped or made for itself a **Being** within its own **Imagination** <term>Imagination</term> <note>Böhme uses "Imagination" not as "fantasy," but as the soul's power to "image" or pull spiritual energy into a specific form.</note> in the Desire, and has also seized the **Being** of the Light along with it.

18. It has seized the Eternal along with the beginning; therefore, the beings of this world must return to the Eternal with their figure, for they were seized along with the Eternal. But whatever was made or seized out of the "beginning" in the **Desire** goes back into its **Ether** <note>original: "Aether" — the subtle, invisible substance of the spiritual heavens.</note>, as if into nothing, merely back into the mirror of the **Imagination**. That is not of the Eternal, but belongs to the eternal **Magic** <note>original: "Magiæ" — for Böhme, "Magic" is the process by which the Will becomes a "Something."</note> in the Desire: Just as a fire swallows and consumes a substance until nothing remains, but it becomes again what it was when it was not yet a substance.

19. Thus we give you to understand what this world's **Being** is: it is nothing other than a **coagulated** <note>original: "coagulirter" — thickened or solidified from a fluid or vaporous state.</note> smoke out of the eternal **Ethers**, which thus has a manifestation like the Eternal. It encloses itself in a **Center** of a being, and finally consumes itself again: and it goes back into the eternal **Magic**, and is only for a time a wonder, as a revelation of the Eternal, through which the Eternal (which is manifest in itself) also manifests out of itself and pours out its **Imagination**. Thus it **renovates** <note>original: "renovire" — restores or makes new.</note> that which was grasped or made with the movement in the Desire, so that the end may enter into the beginning again.

20. For nothing can enter into the freedom of the Eternal unless it be like the Eternal, unless it consists in the **Will-Fire**, and be as subtle as the Light's **Substantiality** <note>original: "Wesenheit"</note>; that is, as a "water" that can dwell in a being where the Light can dwell within and lead its radiance through;

<summary>Böhme explains that the physical world is a temporary, "coagulated" manifestation of divine desire and imagination. He describes the material universe as a transient "wonder" that emerges from the eternal "magic" and must eventually return to its spiritual source.</summary>

## Part 1, Chapter 2. On Theosophical Points.

B 3

...it is not seized by the **Center of Nature** <note>original: "Centro Naturæ"</note>, and even though it is a property of Nature, it is nevertheless an Eternal thing.

21. Thus we give you to understand that everything ever born in this world, which has a **Being** <note>original: "Wesen" — referring to substantial existence</note> that does not originate from the eternal Being, does not inherit the Eternal. However, its figure remains standing magically in the eternal **Mystery** <note>original: "Mysterio"</note>. For in its **original state** <note>original: "Urstande"</note> with the Creation, it went forth from the Eternal; but its body and the whole being of its **torment** <term>Qual</term> <note>Böhme uses "Qual" to describe the agonizing, restless friction of the natural forces—the "source-spirits"—before they find the Light.</note> passes away, consuming itself like smoke, for it is from the "beginning" and goes toward the "end."

22. But whatever originates from the eternal Being, from the essence of the eternal Light, cannot pass away. Only that part of it passes away which entered from the "beginning" into the Eternal—such as the outer flesh, which was introduced into the Eternal within man through **Imagination** <term>Imagination</term> <note>The soul’s power to pull spiritual energy into a specific form or "image."</note>; that must consume itself like smoke.

23. But whatever is introduced into the Eternal from the eternal **Imagination**, that remains standing eternally; and whatever is born out of the Eternal (meaning out of eternal Nature), is the soul in man; that remains eternal, for it has its origin in the Eternal.

24. But if something is born out of the eternal **Center** <note>original: "Centro"</note> of wrath, it may enter into its **renewal** <note>original: "Renovation"</note> if it wills. Just as eternal Nature **renews** <note>original: "renoviret"</note> itself from the essence of outer nature and leaves behind what it made in the beginning, retaining only the magical image which it introduced into the external from the eternal Will through the **Word: "Let there be"** <note>original: "Verbo Fiat"</note> in the Creation; so also may man **renew** that which he makes. If he leaves the earthly behind, he may **renew** that which he has born out of the Eternal. But if it is not **renewed**, it remains in **torment**.

25. For everything that is not, or does not become, like the fire, light, and water, cannot endure in the **Freedom** <note>original: "Freyheit" — the divine stillness or "Ungrund" that exists beyond the friction of nature</note>, but remains in the **torment** of that which it has awakened or made within itself. Understand this as coming from the **Center of Nature**; from what it has introduced into the Will of Freedom. Thus, it will be a torment to him, and a gnawing or an aversion, which he has born for himself out of his own nature. With this, he has made the Freedom dark for himself, so that the light cannot shine through, and that will be his darkness.

<summary>This page distinguishes between the temporary material world and the eternal spiritual reality, explaining that only what originates from the Eternal or is "renewed" can persist. It describes how the soul and its "magical image" are preserved, while the physical body and the "torment" of natural friction eventually dissolve.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of how the spiritual will manifests into a "body" or substantial form. It develops the concept of the Three Principles—the Dark, the Light, and the Material—and explains how the tension between opposing forces like heat and cold allows life to "bloom" out of death and the eternal to be reflected in the temporal world.</meta>

# VI. Of Six Points, Chapter 1, Section 2.

26. For where the will is dark, there the essence <note>original: "Wesen" — referring to the substantial nature or "being" of a thing</note> of the will, as its body, is also dark; and where the will is in torment, there the body is also in torment: and for this reason, the children of the Light of freedom shall be separated from the children of darkness in the **anguish-torment** <term>Angst-Qual</term> <note>Böhme uses this compound to describe the painful, restless friction of the natural forces before they are transformed by Light.</note>, each into its own **principle** <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principium." Böhme describes three "Principles" or fundamental realms: 1. The Dark World (fire/will), 2. The Light World (love/spirit), and 3. This visible world (a mixture of both).</note>.

27. Thus we give you further to understand that every Principle gives birth to its own life according to its property: but the fire is the **goal of separation** <note>original: "Scheide-Ziel" — the point or boundary where one thing is distinguished from another</note> that satisfies the two eternal Principles, namely the darkness and the light; it gives to the darkness its sting and its woe, and to the light its sensitivity and its life.

28. Likewise, the third Principle also has two properties, namely heat and cold: Heat is the principle and gives its sting and woe to the cold; and to the light it gives life and sensitivity; and the light, in turn, gives its essence to the fire, so that it is friendly united with it; and the cold also gives its property and essence to the fire, and the fire breaks it apart and makes out of its essence death and a dying; therefore there is always an enmity between heat and cold, and they never become one.

29. But they achieve this in their enmity: that life must bloom for them through death; for out of heat and cold comes the growth of the third Principle (in which we live outwardly). From the cold comes fruit from the earth, as well as the body of all creatures, and the essence within the elements: From the heat, in its strife, comes life into the body of all creatures and plants; likewise in the depth of the elements, it gives the spirit of the **great world** <note>original: "grossen Welt" — referring to the Macrocosm or the universe as a whole</note> in many kinds of figures; for where the cold makes essence, the heat makes a spirit within it.

30. Thus all essence is in a struggle, so that the wonders of the eternal world may be revealed in fragility, and so that the eternal model in the **Wisdom of God** may lead itself into figures; and that these same models might stand eternally in the **eternal Magic** <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magia." In Böhme's thought, "Magic" is the spiritual power of the will that moves hidden possibilities into actual, visible forms.</note>, in the **Mystery** <note>original: "Mysterio"</note>, for the wonder of God and for the joy of angels and men—not indeed in their physical essence, but in the Mystery, in the Magic, as a shadow of the essence, so that it may be eternally known what God has worked, and what He can and is able to do.

<meta>catchword: 31. For</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains how the conflict between opposing forces—dark and light, heat and cold—is the engine of creation, allowing the eternal "shadows" or models of God's wisdom to manifest in the physical world. He describes fire as the dividing point between the eternal dark and light principles.</summary>

### Part 1, Chapter 2. Theosophical Points.
B 4

31. For after the breaking of this world, only the eternal remains in essence, as eternal spirits with the eternal substance of their bodies, along with the wonders performed here, which remain magically in their form; by these, the spirits shall recognize God's miraculous deeds and power.

32. Therefore, we must now consider the **Principles** with their wonders; these three are nothing other than the one God in his miraculous work, who has revealed himself through this world according to the property of his nature. And thus we should understand a three-fold essence, as three worlds within one another.

33. The first is the **Fire-World**, which originates from the <term>Center of Nature</term> <note>original: "Centro Naturæ"</note>, and nature originates from the desiring will, which originates in the eternal freedom in the <term>Abyss</term> <note>original: "Ungrunde" — Böhme’s term for the "groundless" or bottomless void that precedes all created things</note>, of which we have no knowledge or bearing. See above §. 2.

34. And the second is the **Light-World**, which dwells in the freedom within the Abyss, outside of nature, but comes forth from the Fire-World. It receives its life and sensitivity from the fire; it dwells in the fire, and yet the fire does not seize it: And that is the **Middle World**.

35. Before its ignition, the fire in the <term>Center of Nature</term> produces the dark world; however, upon its ignition, it is within itself the Light-World, as it separates itself into the light and leaves the center in darkness; for it is thus only a torment within itself and a cause of life.

36. It has creatures, but they are of that same fierce essence; they feel no pain, as the light would be a pain to them. But to the fallen devils, who were created in the <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principio"</note> of the Light-World, the darkness is a torment, and the fire is a power or strength, for it is their proper life—though according to many properties, by virtue of the <term>Center of Nature</term>, according to that same essence.

37. The third world is the outer one, in which we dwell according to the outer body, with the outer works and essences, which were created out of both the dark and the light worlds; therefore it is both evil and good, fierce and lovely. Adam was not meant to eat from this property, nor to imagine himself into it; rather, the three worlds were meant to stand within him in such order that none might seize the other, just as in God him-

<meta>catchword: him- (referring to "himself")</meta>

<summary>Böhme describes the universe as three interpenetrating worlds—the dark Fire-World, the divine Light-World, and our mixed Material-World—all emerging from the "Ungrund" or divine Abyss. He explains that while creatures of the dark feel no pain in their own realm, fallen angels suffer because they were originally made for the Light.</summary>

# VI. Concerning Six Points. Ch. 1, Sec. 2.

but: For Adam was created from all three worlds, a complete image and likeness of God;

38. But as he had eaten of the evil and the good, he introduced the external world into the "middle" <note>The "middle" refers to the Light-World or the soul's spiritual center.</note>. Now the external must break away from the middle, and a separation occurs, where the external must return to its own <term>Ether</term> <note>The receptive, atmospheric space of the physical elements.</note>, and the middle remains standing.

39. Thus, if one now sees a true human being standing, he may say: "Here I see three worlds standing, but not moving"; For the outer world moves with the outer body; however, the outer body has no power to move the Light-World; it has only introduced itself into the Light-World in such a way that the light was extinguished within the human; yet he has nevertheless remained only the dark world within himself, and the Light-World remains standing unmoving in him; it stands in him as if hidden.

40. But if he is a true human born of the **new birth**, then the Light-World stands within him: just as light shines through water and makes the <term>Essence</term> <note>The spiritual "substance" or life-force.</note> mobile and full of longing, so that the essence flourishes [greens], so also does the new human flourish in the light. And just as one cannot move the sun’s radiance, one also cannot move the eternal light—that is, the Light-World. It stands still and shines through everything that is capable of receiving it, through whatever is as "thin" as a nothingness; just as fire and water are, for although they are entirely substantial, compared to the outer world they are as a nothing.

41. Thus every **Principle** has its own growth out of itself, and this must be so, otherwise everything would be a nothing.

42. For instance, the Fire-Principle is the root; it grows in its own root, possessing in its properties harshness, bitterness, wrath, and anxiety. It grows in its own nature—in poison and death—into the anxious, severe life that produces darkness within itself because of its severe contraction. These properties form <term>Sulphur</term>, <term>Mercury</term>, and <term>Salt</term> <note>original: "Sulphur, Mercurium und Sal" — Böhme uses these alchemical terms to describe the fundamental forces of all matter: Sulphur (the combustible/soul), Mercury (the moving/spirit), and Salt (the solid/body).</note>, although the fire’s property does not create the *Sul* in the Sulphur; rather, the **will of freedom** creates the *Sul* within the *Phur* <note>Böhme splits the word Sulphur into 'Sul' (the light/oil) and 'Phur' (the fiery/bitter nature), symbolizing the union of divine light and natural fire.</note>, as the Principle moves forward.

43. But whatever goes into its own properties is only *Phur*—that is, severity—along with the other forms at the <term>Center</term> <note>original: "Centro" — the spiritual core where nature's forces are generated.</note>. This is the primary cause of life and of being.

<summary>Böhme explains that while the Fall obscured the divine Light-World within humanity, the "new birth" allows that light to shine through the physical person like sun through water. He also introduces a spiritual-alchemical analysis of life's "root" using the terms Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt.</summary>

# P. 1. Ch. 2. Theosophical Points.

of all things; although it is indeed evil in itself, it is nonetheless the most useful thing for life and the revelation of life: For no life could exist without this quality, and this Principle is grounded in the inner and outer world; in the inner world it is as if imperceptible, in the outer world it is perceptible through its <term>wrath</term> <note>Original: 'Grimme'. This refers to the harsh, contracting, and fierce nature of the first principle/dark world.</note>.

45. For the light ignites the essences with the <term>quality</term> <note>Original: 'Liebe-Qual'. Böhme uses 'Qual' to mean both a sensory quality and a 'source-springing' (Quelle), though it often sounds like 'torment' in modern German.</note> of love, so that they produce a growth out of themselves, in the spirit’s quality, such as a friendly will, morals, virtue, piety, patience in suffering, hope to be delivered from evil; to always speak, sound, sing, and rejoice in the desire and delight of God’s wondrous deeds; to always desire to do right, to resist evil and malice; to always desire to draw one’s neighbor into the Light-World with love; to flee from evil, to always subdue the evil <term>affections</term> <note>Disordered emotional impulses or worldly passions.</note> with patience in the hope of being redeemed; to rejoice in the hope of that which eyes do not see and outer reason does not know; to always press out from evil and lead the desire into the Divine Being, always wanting to eat of God's bread.

46. These qualities are carried by the **new man**, who is born again of the Light-World; these are his fruits, which the Light-World always brings forth in him, entirely hidden from the **old Adam**, and which always kills the old Adam of this world and is always in conflict with him. Thus, the old Adam must follow the new man; indeed, like a lazy donkey that must carry the sack while his master constantly whips him. This is what the new man does to the old: he forces him to do what he does not want to do. Whatever pertains to the joy of this world would be preferred by the old donkey, but he must therefore remain a servant.
