# VI. Of Six Points. Ch. 1. Sect. 2.

47. Secondly, the <term>Principle</term> <note>Böhme uses 'Principle' (Principium) to describe the three fundamental realms of existence: the dark fire-world, the light-world, and the external physical world.</note> has its growth, and yields its fruit into the third Principle in general—that is, into the spirit of the great world—so that the outer and inner <term>Turba</term> <note>Original: 'Turba'. In Böhme’s thought, this is the 'awakened wrath' or the spirit of destruction that causes confusion and decay when the elements of life fall out of balance.</note> is restrained. It penetrates through and grants fertility; it restrains the wrath of the stars and breaks the <term>constellation</term> <note>The astrological influence or 'set' of the stars and the firmament.</note> of both the spirits and the firmamental heaven; it resists the wrath of the Devil and the plots of wicked men, provided, however, that holy ones are found who are worthy of it.

48. And the third Principle also has its growth; within it, from the inner [world], the stars and elements were born and created, which in this <term>place</term> <note>Original: 'Loco' (Latin). Referring to this specific realm or dimension of existence.</note> are called the third Principle along with the Sun. For the two inner worlds—namely the Fire-World and the Light-World—have manifested themselves through the third Principle, and everything is intermingled: good and evil, love and enmity, life and death. In all life there is death and fire; yet, conversely, there is a desire for love, all according to the <term>quality</term> <note>Original: 'Eigenschaft'. Böhme uses this to mean the essential characteristic or 'property' of a thing.</note> of the inner world. Two kinds of fruit grow from this, evil and good, and every fruit possesses both qualities. They also manifest in all life of this world, such that wrath and evil <term>torment</term> <note>Original: 'Qual'. Böhme often links this word to 'Quelle' (source), implying a 'source-springing' or a working quality that can be painful if out of balance.</note> always struggle against love; there, every quality seeks and brings forth fruit. What the good creates, the evil destroys; and what the evil creates, the good destroys. It is a constant war and strife, for the qualities of both inner Principles are active in the outer; each brings and works fruit into the inner kingdom, and each desires to be master.

49. Coldness—as the outflow from the inner center, from the wrath of death—wishes to be master and always to enclose [things] in death; it always awakens the sting of death. And heat—as the outflow from the true fire—also wishes to be master; it wishes to subdue and consume everything, and wishes always to be raw without a body. It is a spirit and desires only a spirit-life; it gives the sting to the cold, for it often kills the cold so that it must let its right sink and surrender itself to the heat.

50. So the Sun, as the light, also wishes to have its right and wishes to be master; it overcomes heat and cold, for it

This page focuses on the conflict within the Third Principle (the physical world) and the unique position of humanity within this struggle.</meta>

# Part 1. Chapter 2. On Theosophical Points.

[The Light] creates water in its bright gentleness, and carries within the spirit of Light a friendly spirit, known as delight <note>Original: 'Lust'. In Böhme's specialized vocabulary, this refers to a divine joy or a pleasurable longing rather than carnal desire.</note>; although the fire provides the strength of the wind, and the Sun provides the gentle spirit, which is rightly called delight. It is indeed one, but has two properties: one according to the fire, as a terrifying rising, and one according to the light, as a gentle life.

51. Thus, the external <term>Principle</term> <note>The physical, material world we inhabit.</note> is only a constant war and strife, a building up and a breaking down; what the Sun, as the light, builds, the cold breaks, and the fire consumes entirely.

52. Within this struggle, its growth proceeds in sheer strife and discord: one thing draws its fertility from the earth, while another breaks or swallows it up again.

53. It creates malice and strife in all animals, for all animals and all life of this world—except for man—are only a fruit of the third <term>Principle</term>, and possess only the life of the third Principle; both its spirit and body are merely that same thing. And everything that moves and has its being in this world—including man with his spirit and visible body, in flesh and blood—is also only the fruit of that same essence, and nothing else at all.

54. But since [man] also has the two inner worlds within himself (which give him true understanding, senses, and <term>mind</term> <note>Original: 'Gemüthe'. A central concept in Böhme’s thought, referring to the deep seat of the soul, the disposition, or the "mind-heart" where the will originates.</note>; which also, during this time of the earthly and elementary body, lie in conflict with one another); he must take care which world he makes the master within himself, for that same world shall be his master eternally. He can break this current time, but no further; when the outward part breaks, everything then stands in its <term>Ether</term> <note>Original: 'Æther'. Here, Böhme refers to the subtle, spiritual medium or the essential state of being that remains after physical destruction.</note>. The mind is free; it is the pivot <note>Original: 'Angel' (meaning a hinge or a turning point). The soul's will acts as the hinge upon which a person's eternal destiny swings.</note> and possesses understanding; it may weigh itself toward whichever side it wishes, and may stand by whichever Principle it desires; into whichever Ether it enters, there it remains eternally.

55. And thus we understand the ground of the three Principles (just like the needle on a scale); what GOD and eternity are and are capable of, and what kind of growth each brings forth from itself and its own characteristic <note>Original: 'Eigenschaft'. The essential quality or 'signature' of a thing.</note>, and how one should search into the ground of nature.

->Thus the first part or point is finished.<-

VI. On the Six Points. Part 2. Chapter 3.

# The Second Point.
->**On the mixed Tree of Evil and Good: Or the life of the three Principles in one another, and how they unite and agree.**<-

## Chapter 3.
### Summaries.

1. In the Light-World, the first <term>Principle</term> <note>original: 'Principium'. Böhme uses this to describe the three fundamental realms of existence: the dark fire-world, the light world of love, and our visible physical world.</note> is not manifest, although it contains all the properties of the first Principle within itself. 2. The dark world is the opposite of the Light-World, and yet it is the foundation of the same. 3. 4. The Light-World is called God; the Fire-World is God's wrath. 5. The dark world is death and hell. 6. Hell has its greatest constellation in the darkness, 7. and various spirits are within it. 8. 9. There is no conflict within the Principles: the fire gives life to everything, and it gives substance to the abyss; 10. it gives its essence to the Light-World, as well as to the third Principle. 11. 12. Thus, no Principle fights against another; rather, the <term>Essence</term> <note>original: 'Essenz'. The fundamental substance or "being-ness" of a thing.</note> desires its own. 13. The will of freedom longs for revelation, from which all three Principles arise. 14. Wrath <note>original: 'Grimm'. The harsh, contracting, and driving force within Böhme's first principle.</note> is a mother of fire and light. 15. The Light-World and the outer world must have the darkness for their life, 16. 17. yet there is no conflict between the Principles, but rather in the essence. [In the same place:] The origin of enmity, 18. of the conflict in this world, 19. and of the conflict between the children of God and the children of this world. 20. 21. Therefore, man must constantly enter into the Light-World with his imagination. 22. For although the Tree of the Three Principles is in agreement, the creatures are not, 23. and the devil's envy also fights against the human race. 24.

IN God's kingdom, as in the Light-World, no more than one <term>Principle</term> is rightly recognized: For the light holds the government, and the other torments <note>original: 'Qualen'. In Böhme's specific vocabulary, these are the active, driving qualities or "source-spirits" of being.</note> and properties are all secret, as a <term>Mysterium</term> <note>A hidden, divine reality or spiritual secret.</note>, for they must all serve the light and yield their will into the light; therefore, the fierce essence is transformed in the light into a desire for light and love, into gentleness.

2. Although the properties—such as harshness, bitterness, anguish, and the bitter woe in the fire—remain eternally, even in the Light-World, none of them are manifest in their own property; rather, they are altogether only causes of the life of movement and joys.

It emphasizes that while the Principles themselves are in harmony, conflict arises within the individual essences and creatures.</summary>

3. What is a woe in the dark world is a benefit in the Light-World; and what is a stinging and enmity in the darkness is an exalted joy in the light; and what is fear, terror, and trembling in the darkness is a shouting of joy, a ringing and singing in the light: and this could not be, if there were not such a serious <term>source-labor</term> <note>original: 'Qual'. For Böhme, this word carries a double meaning: the 'torment' of the dark fire and the 'source-quality' (Quelle) that drives life into being.</note> in the <term>original state</term> <note>original: 'Urstand'. The primal foundation or fundamental condition of existence.</note>.

4. Therefore, the dark world is the ground and original state of the Light-World; and the anxious evil must be a cause of the good, and all belongs to God.

5. But the Light-World is called God alone, and the <term>Principle</term> <note>original: 'Principium'. Böhme uses this to describe the three fundamental realms: the dark fire-world, the light world of love, and the visible physical world.</note> between the Light-World and the Dark-World is called God's wrath and grimness; if this is awakened, as the devil did, and all evil people do, they are then abandoned by the light and fall into the dark world.

6. The dark world is called death, and hell, the abyss, a sting of death; a despair, a self-enmity and sadness; a life of malice and falsehood, a life in which one does not see or recognize the truth and the light; and within it dwell the devils and the damned souls, also the hellish worms, which the <term>Fiat</term> <note>Latin: 'let it be done'. The creative word or "shaping power" of God's will.</note> of death has shaped in the movement of the omnipresent Lord.

7. For hell has the greatest <term>constellation</term> <note>original: 'Constellation'. Here referring to the complex arrangement of spiritual forces and influences.</note> of serious power in the darkness; among them, everything is audible as a great sounding; what rings in the light thumps in the dark, as can be perceived in the substance one strikes so that it gives a sound: For the sound is not the substance itself; like a bell that one rings—this is not the sound itself, but only a hardness and a cause of the sound. The bell receives the blow as a thumping; and from the hard thumping, the sound goes forth: The cause of this is that within the <term>material of the bell</term> <note>original: 'Glocken Materia'.</note> there is an essence which, during creation in the movement of the omnipresent God, was enclosed in the hardness, as we can perceive in the <term>metallic tincture</term> <note>original: 'metallischen Tinctur'. In the language of alchemy, the 'tincture' is the inward, spiritual soul or life-force of a metal.</note>, if one would not be so mad and blind.

8. Thus we recognize that in hell, in the abyss, there are many and various spirits, not only the devils, but many hellish worms, according to the property of their constellation, without understanding; just as in this world there are irrational—

<summary>Böhme explains that the Dark World of "wrath" is the necessary foundation for the Light-World of "joy," using the physical example of a bell's hard metal being the prerequisite for its beautiful sound. He describes hell as a realm of "thumping" and "hardness" where the creative power of God is felt only as a sting or a "worm" without the perception of light.</summary>

### VI. On Six Points. Chapter 2. Point 3.

rational animals, worms, toads, and snakes: So also does the abyss in the grim world have such things; for everything wanted to take on a creaturely form, and entered into a being, so that the <term>Mirror of Wrath</term> <note>original: 'Zorn-Spiegel'. In Böhme's thought, this is the manifestation of God's stern, judgmental nature as a reflection or reality in the dark world.</note> also showed its wonders and revealed itself.

9. There is indeed no sensation of woe in the hellish worms, for they are of that same *essence* and property; it is their life, and is a being that stands hidden from the outer world; only the Spirit of God, who in all three principles is himself the *source-quality* <note>original: 'Qual'. As noted previously, this refers to both the 'torment' of the fire and the 'source-quality' (Quelle) that drives the energy of life.</note> according to each one's property, knows it and reveals it to whom He wills.

10. If we then wish to say how the three *principles* unite in one another, we must place the fire, as the highest strength, in the center; that brings to each *principle* a well-pleasing life, and a spirit which it desires. Therefore, there is no conflict between the principles, for the fire is the life of all principles—understand this as the cause of life, not the life itself. To the abyss it gives its woe, acting as the sting, so that death finds itself within a life; otherwise, the abyss would be a stillness. It gives to it its grimness, which is the life, mobility, and <term>original state</term> <note>original: 'Urstand'.</note> of the abyss; otherwise, it would be a still eternity and a nothingness.

11. And to the Light-world, the fire also gives its *essence*, otherwise there would be no sensation or light within it, and everything would be but One, and yet outside the fire a nothingness—like an eye of wonders that did not know itself, where no understanding would be present; but rather an eternal hiddenness, where no seeking or bringing-forth could occur.

12. And to the third *principle*, as the kingdom of this world, the fire also gives its *essence* and *quality*, from which all life and growth becomes active: All sensibility, and whatever is ever to come to anything, must have the fire; nothing springs forth from the earth without the fire’s *essence*. It is a cause of all three principles, and of everything that may be named.

13. Thus the fire makes a union of all three principles, and is for each the cause of its being. No *principle* strives against the other, but the *essence* of each desires only its own, and is always in conflict; and if that were not so, then all would be a still nothingness. Every

## Part 2. Chapter 3. On Theosophical Points.

each <term>Principle</term> <note>original: 'Principium'. Böhme uses this term to describe the three distinct realms of existence: the dark fire-world, the light holy-world, and the visible physical world.</note> gives to the other its power and form, and there is a constant peace between them.

14. The dark world has the great torment and anguish, which the fire causes, so that the will longs for freedom, and the freedom longs for the revelation, as if for the **essences**, and gives itself into **wrath** <note>original: 'Grimm'. In Böhme's system, this refers to the raw, harsh, and undifferentiated energy of the first principle.</note>, so that it may thus reveal itself: and so it leads itself into the fire, so that out of the wrath and the freedom a fire originates; and it thus gives itself to be swallowed by the wrath, as if into death; and yet it goes out from death, with the accepted **essences**, into a realm of its own, as into its own world or <term>source-quality</term> <note>original: 'Qual'. As previously noted, this carries the dual meaning of 'torment' and the 'quality' or 'vibration' of a specific life-force.</note>, and dwells within itself, ungraspable by death and the dark world, and is a light within itself.

15. Thus, death and wrath are a mother of the fire, and also a cause of the Light-World, as well as a cause of all the being of the third <term>Principle</term>, a cause of all **essences** in all life: how then would one <term>Principle</term> strive against the other, since each so vehemently desires the other.

16. For the Angelic Light-world, and also this our visible world, must have the **essence** of dark death for their life and <term>source-quality</term>: there is a constant hunger for it.

17. This alone is the case: each <term>Principle</term> creates the <term>source-quality</term> according to its own property: It gives its good to the evil, and unites itself with it, and makes one out of three, so that there is thus no strife between the three <term>Principles</term>. But in the **essence** there is strife, and that must be, or else everything would be a nothingness.

18. This alone is for us to consider: from whence does enmity originate? God has created **creatures** in each <term>Principle</term>, out of the being and property of that <term>Principle</term>, to remain therein: And if they do not remain therein, but rather lead another [principle] into themselves and into their property through their **imagination**, then that is an enmity and torment to them; such as the devil and fallen humans, both of whom departed from the Light-World—the devil into the abyss of the strong power of wrath out of pride, and man into this world, into the **mystery** of much-knowing, as if into the wonders.

19. Now man has distress and strife, so that he may go out again; and this world, into which he has gone, holds him, for it wants to have him: and if he [goes] out from it with power—

<summary>Böhme explains that the three principles of reality (Dark, Light, and Physical) are interdependently fueled by the "fire" and "essences" of the dark world, and that suffering arises when creatures—like devils or humans—stray from their original principle through pride or a desire for worldly knowledge.</summary>

### VI. On Six Points 2. Chapter 3.

<meta>catchword: Crea-</meta>

goes out, she <note>original: 'sie'. Böhme often genders the "spirit of this world" or "Nature" as feminine.</note> becomes hostile toward him, and strikes at him, and will not suffer him to remain within her.

20. This is why the children of this world hate, plague, strike, kill, and drive away the children of the light; for the spirit of this world drives them to it. The devil also helps in this, for he knows that this world stands upon the **Abyss**, and that he will receive the children of this world into his kingdom upon the shattering of this <term>Mystery</term> <note>original: 'Mysterium'. For Böhme, this refers to the hidden, underlying structure of the physical universe—the "womb" from which the visible world was brought forth.</note>. Therefore, he drives the children of GOD out of this world, so that they might not also lead his own "children of this world" with them into the **Light-World**.

21. If man had been created for this world alone, the devil would leave him well enough alone; but the devil always wishes to reclaim his royal throne, which he once held and from which he was cast out. And since he cannot attain it himself, he begrudges it to the children who are meant to possess it.

22. This is now a matter for man to consider deeply, and not to be so blind: although man has entered the <term>Mystery</term> of this world, he should not, for that reason, allow himself to be imprisoned within the earthly addiction to death. Rather, he should be a knower and a discerner of the **Mystery**, and not the devil’s **owl** or **fool** <note>In 17th-century symbolism, the owl represented those who are blind to the light of spiritual truth, while the fool or "ape" represented one who mimics outward religious forms without inner understanding.</note>. Instead, he should constantly go out again with his <term>Imagination</term> <note>original: 'Imagination'. In Böhmean thought, this is not "fantasy," but the concentrated power of the human will to shape the soul and focus it toward a specific spiritual realm.</note> into the **Light-World**, for which he was created, so that the Light may give him its radiance. Thus, he may know himself and see the outward **Mystery**. If he does this, he is a man; but if not, he is the devil’s fool and an **ape** of the Light-World. Just as an ape wants to appear clever and plays with all things, mimicking everything, so too is the "juggling-fencing" <note>original: 'Gauckel-Fechten'. Refers to a deceptive or sham sword-fight, symbolizing superficial spiritual effort.</note> of the earthly man with the Light-World. If he does not press into it with earnestness, but only plays with it, the devil mocks him and holds him for a fool—and so he is. He is an **Animal-Man**; so long as his will hangs upon outward things and he considers the goods of this world to be his treasure, he is only a man of this world’s essence, and not of GOD’S Light-World essence. He gives his body to this world, as to the earth, and his soul to the abyss of the dark world.

23. Thus, we give you to understand and recognize that the tree of the **three Principles** agrees quite well within itself, but the creatures do not; for each Principle's...

Creatures of one realm do not desire those of another: and for this reason, there is a firm boundary established between them, so that we do not know the other realms, nor are we meant to see them.

24. Only the Devil’s envy fights against the human race, for they <note>That is, humanity.</note> have occupied his throne <note>Böhme frequently references the idea that humans were created to fill the void in heaven left by the fall of Lucifer and his angels.</note>. Therefore it is said: Man, seek yourself, and see what you are, and guard yourself against the Devil. # The Third Point.
## On the Origin of Conflict in All
## Growth, in which Life Becomes
## Divided Against Itself.
### The 4th Chapter.
#### Summaries.

Many wills cause conflict, 1. 2. but the light alone is Lord. 
3. The mind is the fountain-source of the will; 4. in whatever it ignites, there the spirit and body burn. 5. Whatever quality the fire possesses, the light receives the same; 6. provided that the soul-fire does not ignite in wrath <note>original: 'Grimm'. In Böhme's vocabulary, 'Grimm' refers to the harsh, dark, and consuming fire of nature when it is not softened by divine Light.</note>, 7. by which we recognize our heavy fall. 8. Light comes from the fire, 9. and sends out a spirit from itself 10. good or evil, 11. according to what the substance of the fire is; 12. 13. from which we recognize which spirit attains freedom or not. 14. Love does not accept the wrath. In the same place <note>original: 'ibid.'</note>. Every spirit of the fire desires to enter into its own likeness, 15. from which we can see how enmity arises. 16. Therefore every person must watch over themselves and break their earthly will, 17. for after death there is no remedy <note>original: 'Rath', meaning counsel or a way to change one's course.</note>. 18. Thus we also understand the fall of the Devil. 19. Therefore man should guard himself against pride and greed. 20. God’s kingdom stands in love within clear light. 21. The inner man has these two worlds within himself; 22. whichever one he gives himself into during this time, in that one he lives. 23. Therefore we must go under the cross, 24. walk in love, and be a mirror to others; 25. also overcoming evil with good. 26. While it is permitted in times of need to ward off evil, yet now, in general, the Christian mind is to be set forth much more through earnest exhortation, so that the evildoer might convert. 27. 28.

A thing that is One, that has only One will, does not fight against itself. But where many wills are in one single thing, they become contentious, for each one wants to go its own chosen path. But if it happens that one is the lord of the others, and has full power over all the others, such that he may break them if they do not obey him; then it—

VI. On the Six Points, Point 3, Chapter 4.

...stands the multiplicity of the thing in one essence, for the many wills all give themselves in obedience to their Lord.

2. Thus we make known to you life’s adversity, for life stands in many wills; every essence <term>Essens: Böhme uses this term to describe the fundamental spiritual substances or "food" that sustain the soul’s inner life</term> may lead a will and does lead it. For Harshness, Bitterness, Anguish, and Sourness is an adverse source-quality <term>Qual: a central term for Böhme, punning on the German words for "quality" (Qualität) and "agony" or "torment" (Qual). It refers to the dynamic, vibrating tension required for life to manifest</term>, where each has its own property and is completely contrary to one another. Thus the fire is the enemy of all others, for it sets every quality into great anguish; so that there is a great adversity between them, as each one is hostile to the other—as one sees how heat and cold, fire and water, life and death are hostile to each other.

3. In the same way, man’s life is hostile to itself: one form <note>original: 'Gestalt'</note> is hostile to the other, not only in man but in all creatures; unless the forms of life receive a gentle, lovely Lord under whose constraint they must be, who can break their power and will. That is now the Light of Life, which is the Lord of all forms and can tame them all; and they must all give their will to the Light. They do so gladly, for the Light gives them gentleness and power, so that their harsh, stern, bitter, anxious forms are all transformed into loveliness. They all give their will to the Light of Life, and the Light gives them gentleness.

4. Thus the multiplicity is transformed into One, into one will called the Mind <note>original: 'Gemüthe'—refers to the seat of the soul's disposition and emotional core</note>, and it is the fountain-well from which the single (eternal) will can draw both evil and good. This happens through imagination, or through the mental representation <note>original: 'Fürstellung'</note> of a thing that is evil or good; for the property of that thing is capable of the same property in life. The property of life catches the property of the represented thing—whether it be a word or a work—and ignites itself within; it also infects the other forms of life so that they begin to operate <note>original: 'qualificiren'</note>, and every property burns in its own quality, whether in love or wrath, all according to the represented essence. Whatever the imagination has captured, it leads into the Mind.

5. And we thus give you to understand that when the Mind ignites itself in such a form, it ignites the whole spirit and body, and immediately leads its imagination into the innermost

### P. 3. Cap. 4. Theosophical Points.

...fire of the souls, and awakens the innermost **Center of Nature** <term>Centrum Naturæ: The foundational, energetic core of existence where natural forces are generated before they are tempered by light</term>: which, when it is ignited—be it in grimness <note>original: 'Grimm', a central term for Böhme referring to a fierce, harsh, or wrathful state of nature</note> or love—takes hold in all seven forms of nature. These forms reach for the soul’s **will-spirit**, in which the noble image <note>The 'Imago Dei' or the reflection of God within the human soul</note> stands, wherein God reveals Himself, and they lead their ignited fire into it; just as you have an analogy for this in fire: according to the matter <note>original: 'Materia'</note> in which it burns, it gives off such a glow, as we can recognize in sulfur compared to wood, and in many things more.

6. Thus one understands by this that just as the fire has a certain agony and property <term>Qual: Böhme’s specific term that plays on the German words for 'quality' (Qualität) and 'agony' (Qual). It describes the vibrating, restless tension of life</term>, the light and the power of the light also receive such a property.

7. Since our noble image of God stands in the **light of life**, in the **soul-fire**, it is highly recognizable to us how the soul’s will-spirit—as the noble image—is corrupted and ignited in the agony of grimness, and often also in the agony of love. And thus we see herein our great danger and misery, and rightly understand why Christ taught us patience, love, and gentleness: so that the soul-fire might not ignite in grimness, and also that we might not give others cause to ignite their soul-fire in grimness, so that God’s kingdom be not hindered.

8. Herein we recognize our heavy fall: that Adam introduced **earthly matter** into our soul-fire, which burns as often as an agony in the center of the grim property is awakened. And so we see how we lie captured in God’s grimness between wrath and love, in great danger.

9. And we give you this to highly recognize: You know, as we mentioned above and in all books, how the light goes forth from the fire as another **principle** <term>Principium: A fundamental realm or state of being; Böhme describes three: the dark fire-world, the light-world, and the outer world</term>, and yet that same light has the fire’s property and power, for the fire’s center gives to the light’s center; and how the light is also desiring, and has a **matrix** <note>original: 'Matricem', meaning a generative womb or source</note> of yearning desire, which becomes pregnant in its desiring with the power of the light, as with the gentleness of the light; and in that same pregnancy stands the essence of the light, that is, in the pure love of the Divine Being.

10. And then we have reported to you how the fire draws that same essence into itself, and uses it for its light-essence, and swallows it into itself, but gives forth from the essence another spirit, which is not fire: just as you then see that the fire

# VI. On Six Points. Chap. 3. 4.

...gives two spirits: namely (1) a fierce <note>original: 'grimmigen'</note>, consuming one from the grimness, as the property of the first matter <note>original: 'Materiæ'</note>; and then (2) an air-spirit, which is the property of the light's gentleness.

11. Now we must consider in what matter the fire burns in the first essence <note>original: 'Essenz'</note>; in what it has ignited itself, whether in love or malice—that is, in earthly or divine desire. Whatever the desire is, such is the fire, and it gives off such a light-fire, and also such a spirit from that light-fire.

12. If the matter of the first fire is good, wherein the fire burns, then the second light-fire also has a good property, agony <term>Qual: Böhme’s specific term that plays on the German words for 'quality' (Qualität) and 'agony' (Qual). It describes the vibrating, restless tension of life</term>, and property; it gives off a good, powerful, and lovely light, and from the center of that light, it also gives a good and powerful spirit: and that same spirit is the likeness of GOD, the noble image.

13. But if the first fire in its essence is evil, and has evil matter wherein it burns, then the light of life is also a false agony and a dark glimmer, as can be seen in sulfur-light <note>original: 'Schwefel-Licht'—Böhme often uses sulfur as a metaphor for the harsh, chemical-like tension of the lower nature before it is transformed by divine light</note>: and the center of that desiring light also brings such matter into its fire from its own property, and the fire also gives off such a spirit from itself.

14. Now it is recognizable to us which spirit may attain the freedom of GOD or not; for whichever soul-spirit or image has dark, gloomy properties in itself, it cannot be capable of the clear light of GOD. (2) Also, if it has fierce essences and properties in itself, it cannot unite with the gentleness of GOD and <term>inqualiren</term> <note>original: 'inqualiren'—to work within or harmonize with the qualities of something else</note> with it: for grimness is an enmity against love and gentleness; and love does not let grimness into itself; now they are separated: And love pushes the grimness away, and the grimness also no longer desires the property of love.

15. For as soon as the fire gives the spirit out of itself, it is complete, and separates into its property, whether it be a light-spirit or a dark, fierce sulfur-spirit; and it desires to return into the same essence from which it proceeded: for that is its property, whether it be in love or in enmity toward love.

16. Thus we now understand which spirits or souls live in the agony of enmity, and how enmity <term>urstände</term> <note>original: 'urstände'—a Böhmean verb meaning to take its origin or primordial standing</note> (originates); namely, that a life becomes an enemy to itself, moving from the first matter toward the light of life. The cause lies in the **Wheel of Nature** <term>Rad der Natur: The cyclical, self-generating motion of the seven fundamental forces of existence</term>, in the seven spirits or forms, each of which has its own property: and in whichever property the [mind]...

->müthe<- <meta>catchword: mind/disposition</meta>

<summary>This page explores how the "fire" of the human soul produces a spirit based on the "matter" (desire) it consumes, leading either to divine likeness or a dark, sulfurous enmity. Böhme explains that the soul's internal quality must harmonize with God's gentleness to achieve spiritual freedom.</summary>

### Part 3. Chap. 4. On Theosophical Points.
C 3
<meta>catchword: Envy</meta>

...mind is ignited, such a quality its soul-fire receives! together with the will-spirit; which then immediately strives for substance and being, [considering] how it might bring to fulfillment that with which the will-spirit is pregnant.

17. Now it is necessary to break the power of the earthly will, and to kill the old evil Adam <note>A common theological reference to the sinful, fallen nature of humanity that must be overcome</note>, and to lead his will-spirit out of malice by force and violence: for here in this [current] time it is possible, because the third principle <note>In Böhme’s system, the "third principle" is the visible, material world</note>, with the water that gives gentleness, is attached to the center of the inner nature, and keeps it captured within its own agony <term>Qual: Böhme’s characteristic term for the vibrant, restless tension of life, playing on the German words for "quality" (Qualität) and "torment" (Qual)</term>.

18. But when the soul's will-spirit, as the inner center of light, breaks away from the external and remains alone, then the soul-spirit remains in its [own] quality; for there is little counsel <note>meaning help or remedy</note> unless the will-spirit has, during the time of the outer life, turned toward God's love and reached it as a spark in the inner center. In that case, something may indeed happen, but in what agony and labor that occurs, the spark of love well experiences, as it must break through the dark, fierce death; it is Purgatory <note>original: 'Fege-Feuer'</note> enough for it. In what enmity life stands, in terror and anxiety, until it can sink into the spark, into the freedom of God—this is well experienced by the one who departs from this world with only a little light. The current, "too-clever" world <note>Böhme frequently critiqued the intellectual and religious authorities of his day for prioritizing dry logic over spiritual experience</note> considers this a joke; but what kind of knowledge it actually possesses, it proves through its deeds.

19. And thus we also understand the fall of the Devil, who was an angel—how he "imagined" <note>Böhme uses "imagination" to describe the creative, formative power of the will to shape its own reality</note> himself back into the center of the first property and sought great strength and power (just as the present world seeks great power and honor) and despised the light of love. Although he thought the light should burn for him in this way (and the world also hopes and thinks that the light of God should burn in their splendor), and he wanted to ignite himself even further so that he might rule mightily over all thrones and over the essence of the Godhead in gentleness—this led to his fall, as will also happen to the current world.

20. Therefore, let every person learn from this to guard themselves against pride <note>original: 'Hoffart'</note> and greed <note>original: 'Geiß'—an archaic spelling of 'Geiz'</note>: for the Devil's fall came from pride and greed, because he ignited the center of the dark world within himself. Therefore, he was also cast out of the light-world into the dark world. So it goes for all people who [go] out of gentleness and humility into wrath, pride, greed, and

<summary>Böhme explains how the human will must be forcibly turned from its "old Adam" nature toward God's love to avoid a purgatorial struggle after death. He uses the Fall of Lucifer as a warning against seeking power and pride over the gentleness of divine light.</summary>

# VI. On Six Points 3. Chap. 4.

[...step into] envy, they all imagine themselves into the center of the dark nature, as if into the original state <note>original: 'Urstand'; referring to the primal, unmanifested foundation of nature</note> of nature, and pull themselves into the dark fire of anxiety-torment <term>Qual: Böhme’s characteristic term for the vibrant, restless tension of life, playing on the German words for "quality" (Qualität) and "torment" (Qual)</term>, where the noble image <note>The 'image' refers to the divine likeness or the true spiritual form of the human being</note> is led into another torment, so that it must exist in anxiety and enmity, where every form of life is hostile to another.

21. And we also see from this precisely how God’s kingdom alone stands in the bright clear light, in freedom, in love and gentleness: for that is the property of the white bright light, as one then sees in the external being, where there is such a lovely, gentle and sweet matter for the external fire—which is nonetheless only the wrath <note>original: 'Grimm'; Böhme uses this to describe the fierce, consuming, or "angry" aspect of fire and the first principle of nature</note> of the inner fire—so that a lovely light and scent also arises from it; much more does this happen in the spirit-fire, where no tangible or external being belongs: But where the seven spirits of nature <note>Böhme’s concept of the seven foundational qualities or "source-spirits" that build all reality</note> make a fire in themselves, which is only a quality and torment <term>Qual</term> of the fire, as then the dark-world and also the light-world exist in such a spiritual quality.

22. Likewise, the inner human, who is from the Eternal and who goes into the Eternal, has only these two worlds within himself: into whichever quality he turns himself, into that same world he is also led, and he will eternally be of that world’s quality and enjoy it; either a love-torment <term>Qual</term> from the light-world of gentleness, or a hostile torment from the dark world.

23. Here [in this life] he greens and grows in the middle world <note>The physical, visible world where the light and dark principles overlap and are in balance</note> between the light and dark worlds; he may surrender himself to whichever he wills: whichever essence gets the dominion in him, the wrath or the gentleness, that same one he catches, and it begins [to work] in him and leads him; it gives him his manners and will, and unites itself entirely with him: and into that place the human leads the spiritual human, as the image that God created out of his essence, out of all three principles <note>In Böhme’s cosmology, the Three Principles are the Dark World (Fire/Wrath), the Light World (Love), and the Outward World (Nature/Matter)</note>.

24. Therefore it is said: Take the cross upon you; enter into patience, into a gentle life; do not do that to which the dark center of wrath incites you, nor that to which the falsehood and lust of this world attracts you, but rather break the will of both. Also provoke no one to anger; for if you act falsely, you anger your brother and hinder the kingdom of God.

->P. 3. Cap. 4. Theosophical Points.<-

...might see the Divine Essence in you as in a mirror, and thus also look upon you with his imagination <note>For Böhme, 'imagination' is the creative power of the mind to form a reality within itself</note>. If you do this, you lead your soul, your work, and also your neighbor or fellow brother into God’s kingdom, and you increase the heavenly kingdom with its wonders. This is what Christus taught us, saying: *If someone strikes you on one cheek, offer him the other also: if someone takes your cloak, do not stop him from taking your tunic as well;* <note>original: "Wenn dich einer auf einen Backen schläget, beut ihme auch den andern dar: nimt dir einer den Mantel, so wehre ihm auch nicht den Rock;"</note> (Matthew 5:39, 40) so that he may have a mirror in you, and go into himself, see your gentleness, and recognize that you are God’s child and that God’s Spirit drives you; so that he might also learn from you, go into himself, and seek himself. Otherwise, where you resist him with defiance and malice, his malice is ignited even more, and he finally believes he is doing right by you; but in this way [of gentleness], he must surely recognize that he is doing you wrong.

26. And just as God’s love encounters all evil people and often warns the conscience against evil, so your gentleness and patience then enter into his evil conscience, and the conscience within him brings an accusation before God’s light in the wrath; yet in this way many an evil person departs from his malice, goes into himself, and seeks himself. Then God’s Spirit reminds him of your patience and places it before his eyes, and thus he is drawn toward repentance and abstinence <note>Refers here to the turning away from worldly desires or sinful actions</note>.

27. This does not mean that one should not defend oneself against a murderer or thief who intends to kill and rob; rather, where one sees that someone is so greedy for injustice, one should often allow his falsehood to pass by publicly before his eyes with good light, and willingly offer him the Christian, loving heart. This is so that he may find through the power of the deed that it happens out of God’s zeal of love <term>Liebe-Eifer: Böhme’s term for the active, intense, and sometimes fierce energy of divine love</term>, and that God’s will and love matter more to you than earthly existence. One should not intentionally allow something zealous <note>In this context, 'zealous' refers to the 'wrathful' or 'fierce' quality of nature (Grimm)</note> or evil to happen; rather, he should see that the children of God love the love of God more and cling to it more than to all temporal goods, and that God’s children are not at home in this world but are only pilgrims who gladly leave everything of this world behind so that they may inherit the kingdom of heaven.

28. The Spirit of God presents all of this to the evildoer in the Light of Life and thereby exhorts him to return. But if he will not, the wrath of God makes him hellish...

<summary>Böhme explains that responding to aggression with Christian gentleness acts as a spiritual mirror, forcing the aggressor to confront their own conscience. # The Fourth Point.
### How the holy and good Tree of Eternal Life grows out of and through all growths of the three principles, and is grasped by none.

## The 5th Chapter.
->Summaries.<-

The light shines in nature but is incomprehensible <note>original: "unbegreifflich," meaning it cannot be seized, contained, or fully understood by natural means</note>, §. 1–3. It makes everything gentle and is a Lord in nature. 4. Conversely, the wills in harsh nature create separation, 5. for wrath is the enemy of light, even though the light shines incomprehensibly through the darkness. 6. 7. No **Principle** <term>Principle: One of the three fundamental realms of existence: the dark fire-world, the divine light-world, and the physical world.</term> grasps the light unless it enters into death and surrenders its evil will to the light; 8. thus, out of evil comes good, and the light shines out of the darkness. 9. Why do the devils and the godless not see God? 10. The world of light knows nothing of the devil, 11. and the light is only grasped by that which surrenders itself into it; just as the outer world appropriates the sun as a "natural god," 12. which is why the sun is recognized as its own principle, because it has its own natural god. 13. The sun’s **origin** is from the **number 8** <note>In Böhme’s complex numerology, the number 8 often represents the transition from the world of time into the world of eternity or the "new life."</note>, and it is God’s fire, a principle of the light-world and of the sun. 14. But the dark world is the ground of nature, and the eternal will of the dark world. 15. This outer world is enclosed in God’s wrath as if in death, 16. and has only a reflection of God. 17. The devil, however, is the poorest creature. 18.

1. A thing that dwells within itself may be grasped by nothing, for it dwells in Nothingness; there is nothing before it that may seize it, and it is also free from any thing that is outside of it.

2. Thus we give you to understand concerning the **Divine power and light**: it dwells within itself and is enclosed in nothing; nothing touches it unless it shares its property. It is everywhere in nature, yet it does not touch nature (understand here the outer nature of the world). It shines therein just as the sun shines into the elements; the sun shines into the water, also into the fire, and through the air, and yet is grasped by none—

<meta>catchword: ergrif- (grasped)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the third point on spiritual repentance and introduces the fourth point, explaining how the divine Light exists within nature without being corrupted or "grasped" by it. It uses the metaphor of sunlight passing through elements like water and fire to illustrate how God's presence remains pure and distinct from the material world.</summary>

->Part 4. Ch. 5. Theosophical Points.<-

[grasped] nor held: It gives power to all beings and makes the essential spirits lovely and full of joy. It draws the essence from the earth with its power—and not only the essence, but also the being of the essences, which provides a body out of the essence.

3. Now, what the sun does in the third principle <term>Third Principle: The visible, physical world, which acts as a mirror or reflection of the spiritual realms.</term>, by transforming all hostile essence and torment <note>original: "Qual," a term Böhme often uses to imply both "torment" and the "vibrating quality" or "source-spring" of a thing's being.</note> into gentleness, that is what God's light does within the forms of eternal nature.

4. It shines into the forms, and also out of the forms; that is, it ignites the forms of nature so that they all receive the light's will, and they make the light their own and surrender themselves entirely to it. That is: they sink down <note>original: "ersincken," implying a humble collapse or surrendering of the self-will.</note> out of their own essence and become as if they had no power in themselves, desiring only the light's power and might. Thus, the light takes their power and might into itself and shines out of that very same power. In this way, all forms of nature come to the light, and the light is of one will with nature, and the light remains Lord.

5. Otherwise, where the wills within the harsh forms of nature want to be Lord, there is a separation and an eternal enmity; for one form is always hostile to the other, each one exalting itself. From this comes adversity, such that a creature is so evil, angry, and hostile that life within itself is often at odds.

6. And just as we now recognize that the light comes to the aid of the harsh life of nature and the properties of the essences, so that a joyful life arises and is thus transformed in the light; so we also recognize that the life of dark wrath <note>original: "Grimmigkeit," referring to the fierce, burning quality of the dark world.</note> is the enemy of the light, for it cannot grasp the light. The eternal light shines through the darkness, and the darkness cannot comprehend it <note>A reference to John 1:5.</note>, for the multiplicity of wills in the dark nature are all locked in death. The light does not shine into them, but through them; they neither catch nor see the light. We understand and recognize that the light is indeed in the dark world; but it does not fill the darkness, therefore the essences of the dark world remain a hostile poison and death, where the essences are at enmity with themselves.

7. And thus, there are three principles within one another, and none grasps the other, and the eternal light can be grasped by nothing, unless it falls into death and gives up its essence voluntarily to the fire of nature, and goes with its essential...

## VI. On the Six Points, Chapter 4, Paragraph 5.

...will out of itself into the light, and surrender itself entirely to the light; and desire to want or do nothing, but rather surrender its will to the light, so that the light may be its willing.

8. Thus the light seizes him, and he the light also: And thus the evil will is surrendered to the light, and the light gives its power into the wickedness, and makes out of the wickedness a friendly, good will, which is only a desire for love; for the gentleness of the light has completely embodied itself <note>original: "eingeleibet," meaning to become part of the substance or "body" of the thing.</note> in the hostile will.

9. In this way, God's will is now done, and evil is transformed into good, and God's love shines out of His wrath and fury <note>original: "Zorn und Grimm." In Böhme's thought, "Grimm" (fury/fierceness) is the raw, harsh energy of nature that becomes a "burning" wrath if not tempered by light.</note>, and no fury is recognized in God's eternal nature. For thus we are to understand how the eternal light, as the eternal tree of power, shines through all three principles <term>Three Principles: Böhme's cosmological map consisting of the dark fire-world, the light love-world, and the physical world.</term>, though not one of them grasps it; for as long as a being is outside of God's will (meaning the gentle light-will), it remains solitary and dwells in itself, and perceives nothing of God. But if it aligns itself with God, and breaks its own will and lets it sink down, then it is one spirit in and with God, and God shines out of that same being.

10. And we also understand why the wicked soul, as well as the devil, does not see or recognize God: because their will does not want to align itself with God; it wants to be its own Lord. Thus it remains outside of God, only within itself, and God also remains within Himself; and so they dwell one within the other and know nothing of each other, for they turn their backs to one another and do not see each other's face.

11. Thus the Light-World knows nothing of the devils, and the devils know nothing of the Light-World, except for this: that they were once in it. They represent it to themselves as one who sees in his imagination, yet the Light-World no longer surrenders itself to their imagination, nor do they imagine themselves after it <note>Meaning they no longer try to model their internal images after the light.</note>, for it terrifies them, and they are also ashamed of it.

12. This is also how we should understand the outer world; God's light shines through and through, but is only grasped by that which aligns itself with it. Since this outer world is mute and without understanding toward God, it remains in its own will and carries its own spirit within it, even though God has given it a God of Nature, namely the sun, into which every being should cast its will and desire, whatever...

<summary>Böhme explains that when a hostile or "evil" will surrenders to the Light, it is transformed into a "friendly" will of love. He further describes how the Light-World and the world of the devils exist simultaneously in the same space but remain completely hidden from one another due to their opposing orientations.</summary>

### Part 4, Chapter 5: Theosophical Points

is in this world, and that which does not do this remains within itself a great wickedness, and is its own enmity.

13. And therefore this world is recognized as its own **Principle** <note>original: "Principium." In Böhme's system, a Principle is a fundamental realm of existence with its own source of life.</note>, in that it has its own Nature-God; to be compared, namely, to the sun, and yet truly the light of the Deity shines through everything, through and through. The light of the sun takes its essence from **God’s** fire, and **God’s** fire from **God’s** light. Thus the light of the sun gives that same power to the elements; they in turn give it to the creatures, and also to the plants of the earth; and everything that is of a good quality thus receives **God’s** power as a glimpse through the mirror of wisdom, from which it has its growth and life.

14. For **God** stands present to all being, but not all being receives Him into its essence; rather, it is as in the mirror of the reflection in the sun's power; for the sun originates from the eighth number <note>The "eighth number" refers to the "eternal nature" or the state of glory that exists beyond the seven basic properties of nature.</note>. Its root, from which it receives its radiance, is the eternal fire, but its body stands in this world. Its desire is directed entirely into this world, which is why it shines into the world; but its first root stands in the first world, in the fire of **God**. This world gives substance to its desire, and it gives its power to the substance, and thus fills all being of this world, just as **God’s** light fills the Divine light-world; and if **God’s** fire no longer burned, the sun would go out, and so would the Divine light-world. For **God’s** fire gives essence to both, and is a principle of both of them. And if the dark world did not exist, these two would not exist either; for the dark world provides the cause <note>In Böhme’s thought, "cause" or "Ursache" implies the friction or tension necessary to ignite a fire.</note> for **God’s** fire.

15. Thus the three worlds must also be within one another, for nothing can exist without a foundation. For the dark world is the foundation of nature; and the eternal, unfathomable will, which is called the Father, is the foundation of the dark world, as mentioned before; and the light-world is hidden in the dark, and also the dark world in the light-world.

16. To be understood thus: that this world is enclosed in the wrath of **God** as if in death; for wrath flourishes <note>original: "grünet" (literally "greens"). Böhme uses this to describe a spiritual force coming to life or manifesting in physical form.</note> in the essence of this world: were that not the case, this world's essence could well grasp <note>original: "fahen," an archaic form of 'fangen' (to catch/receive).</note> **God's** light.

17. Thus this world receives only a radiance <note>original: "Glast," meaning a reflected luster or shimmering light, rather than the original source.</note> from **God** through the power of the sun. The sun is not **God's** light, for

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sun's spiritual origin, transitioning from Chapter 5 to the summaries and introduction of Chapter 6. It explores how the physical world is an "imagination" or externalization of the inner spiritual realms.</meta>

# VI. Of Six Points, Part 4, Chapter 6.

it shines not entirely in Divine Essence, but in elemental essence. However, **God’s** fire is its root, though it is filled with the being of this world; for it is longing like a <term>magical craving<note>original: "magische Sucht." In Böhme's writing, "Sucht" refers to an intense, driving desire or a sickness of longing that pulls spirit into matter.</note></term>, and receives into its **imagination** and craving the power of the stars and elements; out of these, it also shines.

18. Although **God’s** fire is the root, it nevertheless does not belong to **God’s** Kingdom. And in this, one also understands how the Devil is the poorest creature, for he cannot move even a tiny leaf unless the **Wrath** <note>original: "Zorn." In this context, the dark, fiery, and harsh quality of nature that exists when separated from God's light.</note> is within it; thus he moves it according to the property of the **Wrath**. For the light and the power of this world are contrary to him; he does not enter with his will into the property of the light, because he simply cannot. He stands back-to-back against the light of the sun, in his figure and property; therefore, the sunlight is of no use to him, and everything that grows in the sun’s power and identifies itself with the sun, he treats as an enemy; his will does not like to enter into it.

->Chapter 6.<-
->Summaries.<-

1. The outer world has come forth from the **imagination** of both inner worlds. 2. Therefore, the <term>tincture<note>original: "Tinctur." A spiritual essence or "dye" that gives life, color, and medicinal power to physical substances like metals.</note></term> of metals also has fellowship with the heavenly. 3. Both inner worlds have longed after being. 4. Where we now swing ourselves with our will, there we are captured. 5. These three worlds stand within one another, not one below the other. 6. The <term>groundless being<note>original: "ungründliche Wesen" or "Ungrund." The abyss of pure potentiality that exists before and beyond all created things.</note></term> makes something of itself, which in itself is only a spirit or will. 7. The outer cannot grasp the inner, 8. although it receives its power from the inner. 9. If **God** were to reveal it, this entire world would be nothing but sun. 10. Thus, man is also threefold. 11. If man wants to catch **God’s** light, 12. he must be pure. 13. For the life of man is **God’s** mirror. 14. Light is **God’s** finding, and darkness is His losing. 15. Whatever man **imagines** after, that he catches. 16. [and following].

AS we contemplate all of this, and go from the inner world into this outer visible one, we find that the being of the outer world has come forth from the inner, as if from the **imagination** or desire of the inner world. And we shall find in the outer world the property of both inner worlds; furthermore, how the will of both properties is active and manifest in the outer world. And then, how the Good—namely the essence which has come forth from the Light-world—is entirely enclosed within **Wrath** and death.

<meta>This page continues the numbered summary points for Chapter 6, transitioning into the main body of the chapter's discourse on the relationship between the three worlds: the dark world, the light world, and the external physical world.</meta>

Part 4, Chapter 6. Theosophical Points. 45

<meta>catchword: Him</meta>

And how the Divine Power makes all things active, so that everything grows out of and through the <term>wrath of death<note>original: "Grimme des Todes." For Böhme, this refers to the harsh, cold, and dark foundation of nature that must be overcome by the spiritual light to produce growth.</note></term>.

2. For the earthly <term>tincture<note>original: "Tinctur." An alchemical term for a spiritual essence or "dye" that gives life, color, and medicinal power to physical substances.</note></term> has no communion with the heavenly in the Light-World. We find, however, another tincture in the earth which has communion with the heavenly, as in the precious metals, and yet it is locked within.

3. We understand, therefore, the movement and the <term>Fiat<note>original: "Fiat." Latin for "let there be." In Böhme's system, the Fiat is the "divine attraction" or the creative word that solidifies spiritual desire into form.</note></term> of both eternal worlds, namely the dark and the light; each has longed for a Being: and since God moved once, one world could not be moved without the other.

4. For the dark world contains the first <term>center<note>original: "Centrum." Böhme describes several "centers" or stages through which the divine will passes to manifest as nature or light.</note></term> of nature, and the Light-World the other center, as the Heart of God, or the Word of the Power of the Deity, and one world is not separated from the other.

5. By this we should recognize what danger we are in, and consider where we wish to swing ourselves with our will. For if we swing ourselves into the earthly <term>craving<note>original: "Sucht." An intense, driving desire or sickness of longing that pulls the spirit into lower material states.</note></term>, it captures us; then the torment of the abyss is our Lord, and the sun is our temporal god.

6. But if we swing ourselves with our will into the world beyond this world, then the Light-World captures our will, and God becomes our Lord; and we leave the earthly life of this world and take with us what came into us from the Light-World—understand: in Adam—that is carried out of this world by the will that becomes one spirit with God.

7. Reason says: Where then are the three worlds? It simply wants a separation, where one is outside the other or stands above the other, which yet cannot be; otherwise the eternal <term>groundless Being<note>original: "ungründliche Wesen." This refers to the *Ungrund*, the absolute, undifferentiated depth of God that exists before any nature or world is formed.</note></term> would have to split apart. But how can that split apart which is a Nothing, which has no place, which is itself Everything? That which has no ground, which cannot be grasped, which dwells in itself and possesses itself, certainly cannot come into <term>parts<note>original: "Particul." Individual fragments or pieces.</note></term>; rather, it goes out from itself and reveals itself out of itself.

8. It makes a thing out of itself, which in itself is only a will; in itself it is a spirit, but it makes out of itself a likeness of the spirit, and the likeness makes a Being according to the property of the spirit—just as this world is a Being, and the inner Spirit possesses it. He is in every place, yet [he does not] grasp...

<summary>This page explains that the dark, light, and earthly worlds are not spatially separate but exist within one another as different "centers" of the same divine manifestation. It warns that human will determines which of these worlds—the divine light or the abyss—claims the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between the divine spirit and the physical world. It follows the previous page's description of how the divine power moves through nature, specifically addressing how God is omnipresent but not contained by any physical location.</meta>

### VI. On Six Points, Part 4, Chapter 6.
<meta>catchword: Unleb-</meta>

The place does not contain Him; rather, He encompasses the place. The place knows nothing of Him, though it feels Him, for He is the Power and the Spirit within the place. His will moves through the essence, and the essence has no eyes to see Him; yet He is the "Seeing" of the place. He Himself is no place or locality, yet He creates for Himself an <term>unfathomable place<note>original: "ungründliche Stätte." This refers to Böhme’s concept of the *Ungrund*, the "bottomless" or "groundless" divine mystery that exists before and beyond all created space.</note></term> where there is no measurement. He is everything, and yet also like a Nothing when compared to the external world. Whatever He gives out from Himself, He also possesses; He does not "travel" into it, for He is already there before the essence occupies the place. The place catches only a <term>glimmer<note>original: "Glast." A key term for Böhme, describing a reflected radiance or a shimmering manifestation of divine light—like light reflecting off a surface rather than the light source itself.</note></term> of His will, just as someone sees their image in a mirror but cannot grasp the image itself; or as the sunshine in water cannot be seized, yet the water feels it and receives the glimmer; or as the earth receives power from the sun so that it brings forth fruit. In this way, God dwells in all beings and penetrates through everything, yet is grasped by nothing.

9. And as we understand that the earth has a great hunger and desire for the sun’s power and light—by which it draws the sun’s power and light into itself and becomes capable of what would be impossible without that desire—in the same way, the external essence hungers for the internal, for the external form <term>originates<note>original: "urständet." This implies more than just "starting"; it suggests that the very foundation and standing of the external world is rooted in the internal spiritual world.</note></term> from the internal. Thus, the external essence receives the internal form into itself as a glimmer or power; for the external essence cannot <term>grasp<note>original: "fahen." An archaic form of "fangen," meaning to capture, contain, or take hold of.</note></term> the internal spirit, because He does not dwell in the exterior, but possesses Himself within the interior.

10. But the external essence receives the form of the spirit through the mirror, just as the water receives the sun’s glimmer. We must not think that the interior is far from the exterior, like the sun’s <term>body<note>original: "Corpus."</note></term> is far from the water—although even then, the sun is not truly far from the water, for the water contains the sun’s property and essence, otherwise the water would not catch the sun’s glimmer. Although the sun is a body, the sun is also in the water, but not manifest: the physical body of the sun is what makes the sun manifest in the water. And we should recognize that the entire world would be nothing but sun, and the <term>place<note>original: "Locus."</note></term> of the sun would be everywhere, if God willed to kindle and reveal it; for every essence in this world catches the sun’s glimmer. There is a mirror in all things, so that the power and form of the sun may be grasped in all living and...

<summary>Böhme explains that God is an omnipresent spirit who encompasses all places without being limited by them, using the metaphor of the sun's reflection in water to describe how the physical world "captures" divine power.</summary>

### IV. On Six Points, Part 4, Chapter 6.

...of things without life, in all four elements and their essence and substance.

11. Likewise, it is the same with the inner **World of Light**; it dwells within the external world, and the external receives power from it. It flourishes within the external power, yet the external knows nothing of it: it only feels the power, and cannot behold the inner light, except that in its *mirror of life* it receives the <term>radiance<note>original: "Glast." A recurring term in Böhme's work referring to a reflected brilliance or shimmering manifestation of divine light.</note></term> of it; for the inner power creates an image of itself within the external form.

12. Thus, we can now understand humanity: man is both the inner and the outer world, as well as the cause of the inner world within himself; as far as he is concerned, he is also the **dark world**. He is all three worlds: and if he remains in the right order, so that he does not lead one world into the other, he is the **likeness of God**.

13. He is meant to introduce the form, as the mirror of the Light-World, into the external and also into the innermost World of Darkness, and to lead the power of the middle world—the Light-World—into the mirror; then he becomes capable of receiving the Divine Light. For the physical substance does not catch the light itself, but rather the power of the light: however, the mirror of the power catches the light, just as water catches the sun; for water is like a bright mirror compared to the earth.

14. If water is mixed with earth, it no longer catches the sun's light: in the same way, the human spirit or soul does not catch the Light of God unless it remains pure and sets its desire upon that which is pure, namely the Light; for whatever the life <term>imagines<note>original: "imaginiret." For Böhme, imagination is not just "make-believe," but a focused act of the will that shapes the soul into what it desires.</note></term> toward, that is what it catches. Human life is the form of both inner worlds: if life desires <term>Sulphur<note>Böhme breaks the alchemical word "Sulphur" into two mystical components: "Sul" (the light-flame or oil) and "Phur" (the fiery, dark, or "grim" nature). In his view, the soul must choose which part to focus on.</note></term> within itself, then the *Phur* within the *Sul* becomes its darkening. But if it desires only the *Sul*, then it receives the power of the Light, and within that power, the Light with its true quality. For in the *Phur*—the fierce or wrathful nature—life cannot remain bright like a mirror, but in the *Sul* it can. For human life is a true **mirror of the Godhead**, in which God beholds Himself. He gives His radiance and power into the human mirror and finds Himself within man, as well as in angels and in the forms of the heavens.

15. The essence of the Light-World is His discovery or revelation, and the essence of the dark world is His loss; He does not see Himself in the dark world, for it has no mirror capable of receiving the light. Everything that follows the dark
<meta>catchword: world</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that humanity is a composite of three worlds (Light, Dark, and External) and serves as a spiritual mirror where God beholds Himself, provided the soul remains pure and focused on the Light.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "mirror of life." On the previous page, Böhme explained that the human soul acts as a mirror that can reflect either the Divine Light or the darkness of the material world, depending on where its desire is focused.</meta>

...pure. Whoever imagines <term>world-essence<note>original: "Welt Essentz." The underlying spiritual substance or "being" of the physical universe.</note></term> and its properties, catches the property of the dark world and loses the mirror of God. He is filled with a dark, fierce wrath, just as when water is mixed with earth—the sun can no longer shine within it. That same water loses the sun’s reflection, and the water must either sink away from the earth again, or it will never be a mirror for the sun again, but remains imprisoned in the fierce, dark earth.

16. It is the same with human life: because it imagines toward God’s Spirit, it receives God’s power and light and recognizes God. But if it <term>imagines<note>original: "imaginiret." For Böhme, "imagination" is not just fantasy, but the creative power of the soul's will to shape itself into the likeness of what it desires.</note></term> toward earthliness or the property of the dark world, then it receives the essence of earthliness and the dark world and fills itself with the same. Now the mirror of life is shut up in darkness; it loses the mirror of the Godhead and must be born differently.

17. Thus we recognize that Adam made the pure mirror earthly in this way and lost God’s power and light, which Christ, the Son of God, brought back again. He shattered the earthly darkness and reintroduced the mirror of the Godhead with power.

18. Thus we recognize how the holy tree grows through everything and out of every being; yet it is grasped by no being except only in the mirror of purity, as in a pure human life. That life desires the same tree and cannot be grasped in any dark life. This, then, is the fourth point.

If he has a lust for evil, 39. then he is a devil in a foreign shape; 40. but if he fights against evil, 41. then God’s fire burns in him. 42. A person can perish dangerously, and it is even more dangerous to let the struggle sink. 43. A further <term>touchstone<note>original: "Probier-Stein." A stone used to test the purity of gold or silver; here, a spiritual test.</note></term>. 44. 1. Every life is a bright luster and mirror, and looks just like a flash of a terrifying sight: but when that same flash catches the light, it transforms into a gentleness and lets the terror sink, for the terror incorporates itself into the light. Thus the light shines out of the terrifying flash, for the flash is the essence of the light; it is its fire.

2. The flash contains the <term>Center of Nature<note>original: "Centrum Naturæ." The core point of tension where the forces of nature are concentrated before breaking through into spirit or light.</note></term>, for the fourth form of nature is the flash; and there life originates, which in the steadfast fire (as in the <term>Principle<note>original: "Principio."</note></term>) reaches perfection—

# VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 7.

perfection comes, but in the light it is placed as if into another <term>quality<note>original: "Qual." For Böhme, this word implies both a "quality" of being and the "torment" or "paining" through which nature works to manifest itself.</note></term>.

3. Now, however, the origin of the <term>Imagination<note>original: "Imagination." To Böhme, this is not just fantasy, but the soul's power to "form" or "image" its own internal reality and spiritual substance.</note></term> is in the first form of nature, as in the desiring <term>harshness<note>original: "Herbichkeit." This is the first property of nature: an astringent, contracting force that seeks to pull things into a center.</note></term>; it leads its form through the dark world, until it reaches the fire. For the first desire goes through all forms, makes all forms, and drives itself into the fire, into the <term>Principle<note>original: "Principium." The fundamental source or spiritual foundation of existence.</note></term>. There is the dividing-goal of the spirit; there it is born. The spirit is now free; it may go back again behind itself, into its mother—the dark world—with its imagination, or it may sink forward through death into the fire’s anguish and green forth in the light, as it wills. It stands in its choice: wherever it gives itself, there it must be, for its fire must have substance so that it has something to consume.

4. If the spirit now wants to eat from its first mother—the harshness—that is, if it wants to give the fierce essence in the <term>center<note>original: "Centro."</note></term> to its fire as food, or rather the light-essence in the light-world, that all lies within its power. Whatever its fire receives, it burns in that quality.

5. In the dark quality, it burns in the dark, harsh, severe torment, and sees into itself only as a <term>flash<note>original: "Bliß." The momentary strike of lightning that signifies the transition from darkness to light.</note></term>; it has only the mirror of darkness and looks into the darkness. And in the quality of the light, it receives the gentleness of the light, in which the light-fire burns, and it looks into the light-world. Everything is near to the spirit, and yet it may see into no other world or quality than the one in which its fire burns; the spirit is capable only of that same world. It sees nothing in the other world, for it has no eyes for it; it remains an eternal hiddenness to it, unless it had been in another world and had departed from it, and given itself into another fire, as the devils did. They indeed have a knowledge of the light-world, but no sensitivity or sight of it; the light-world is near to them, but they do not know it.

6. Thus we are now to recognize the corruption of life, which happens in the <term>Principle<note>original: "Principio."</note></term>. There is the hinge; there the will may swing wherever it wants. If it wants to go into multiplicity and wants to be lord itself, then it cannot grasp multiplicity in any other way than in the dark, severe harshness, in the dark world. But if it wants to go into the <term>Nothing<note>original: "Nichts." A state of total surrender or "resignation," letting go of the self-centered ego.</note></term>, into freedom, then it must give itself up to the fire; thus it sinks into the death of the <term>Principle<note>original: "Principii."</note></term>, and thus it greens forth from the fire's anguish into the light. For when it gives itself up, then...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 7 regarding the soul's transition through the "fire" of its own nature.
