### Part 5. Chapter 7. Theosophical Points.

the eternal will toward nature (which is God the Father) leads him out through the fire into himself. For by <term>surrendering<note>original: "Einergeben." A central concept in Böhme’s thought, referring to the total yielding or resignation of the individual self-will to the divine will.</note></term>, he falls back to the first will toward nature. That will leads him out of the <term>nature of anguish<note>original: "Angst-Natur." In Böhme's system, "Angst" refers to the literal constriction or "narrowness" of the material world before it is illuminated by the light.</note></term> through the other will (which is His Son or Heart), and places him by the Son’s will into freedom, outside the fire's <term>torment<note>original: "Qual." Böhme uses this word to mean both the "quality" of a thing and the "paining" or "torment" of its existence.</note></term>. There, instead of the "many," he receives "all"—not for his own glory or power, but for God’s glory and power. God is in him, his willing and his doing.

7. But whatever wants to be its own master within the fire, that enters into its own number, into its own essence, which it is itself. And whatever surrenders its power also surrenders its fire-burning, and falls back to Him who is the cause of the fire: the eternal will of God.

8. Thus, it has fallen into freedom outside its fire of torment, and that freedom ignites its fire. Now it has become a light and a bright mirror for it, because it has surrendered itself into freedom, as if into God. Thus, its fire is a shine and radiance of the Majesty of God.

9. But he who does not will this, but wants to be master himself, remains his own; in his own forms, he can lead himself no higher than to the fire, and even then only to the <term>flash<note>original: "Blitz." This refers to the sudden, jarring emergence of light in the darkness of nature, which is terrifying unless it is transformed into the steady light of the "Second Principle."</note></term>. No bright fire can burn in him, for he has no bright essence in him to fuel the fire. The <term>Center of Nature<note>original: "Centrum Naturæ."</note></term> has nothing in itself from which a bright shine might arise; rather, the freedom outside of nature is the cause of the shining. Whatever surrenders itself into nature, yet does not desire nature’s property but rather freedom, that soul will be ignited in its flash of life by freedom, in the same way that the <term>second Principle<note>original: "ander Principium." The realm of light and love, as opposed to the "First Principle" of darkness and fire.</note></term> was ignited.

10. Thus we understand how a life is corrupted—that is, how it leads itself into anguish and torment in the darkness. It happens when life wants to be its own master and desires "multiplicity." If it will not surrender itself to death, it cannot reach any other world.

11. For every life arises in the torment of anguish, in nature, and has no light in itself unless it enters into that which causes nature; only there does it receive light.

12. For everything that is in nature is dark and in anguish, as can be recognized in this world; if the sun were taken away, there would be nothing but pure anguish and darkness.

He warns that those who insist on their own mastery remain trapped in a dark, constricted state, unable to progress beyond a momentary "flash" of insight.</summary>

### VI. On Six Points, Part 5. Chapter 7.

Therefore God himself has moved, that he might give a light to this world, so that the outward life might stand in the light.

13. But concerning the inner life of the soul, it has another form: the inner life cannot reach the outer; if the soul-fire does not have God’s light, then the soul’s will also cannot enter into God’s light; it must remain in the darkness of eternal nature.

14. Outer reason thinks that when the outer eye sees, it is well, and that there is no other seeing besides. Yes, evil enough, if the poor soul borrows only the outer mirror and must make do with the outer alone: but where does its seeing remain when the outer mirror breaks? With what will it see then? With the <term>anxious fire-flash<note>original: "ängstlichen Feuer-Blitz." In Böhme's philosophy, this is the "lightning" or "spark" of natural life that can either become a light in God or remain a terrifying, burning spark in the darkness.</note></term> in cruelty, in darkness, for otherwise it can see nowhere.

15. Therefore it often happens, when the poor captive soul looks into its inner root and thinks about what will follow when its outer mirror breaks, that it is terrified and plunges the body into anxiety and doubt.

16. For it can look nowhere where its eternal rest might be; rather, it finds that within itself it is in pure unrest, and furthermore a darkness, and possesses the outer mirror only <term>as a lease<note>original: "Lehens-weise." This implies the physical world's light is only a temporary loan or tenure, not a permanent possession of the soul.</note></term>.

17. For while the soul is stuck in this outer body, it may well make use of the sun-mirror; for the sun has the inner fire within its root, as the <term>Principle<note>original: "Principium." Böhme uses this Latin term to describe the fundamental originating forces or "worlds" of existence: the dark fire-world (the Father), the light-world (the Son), and the outward physical world.</note></term> of the Father. From that same fire it receives a luster or mirror, of which the essence of the body is a cause, so that it can thus be in joy in this earthly, transitory life: but when the outer mirror breaks, it is over, and the soul-fire goes into the eternal house of mourning, as if into the center of darkness.

18. The soul has, during the time of the outer body, three mirrors or eyes of all three worlds; whichever mirror it turns into, from that one it sees. But it has no more than one by right of nature, which is the fire-flash, as the fourth form of the dark world, in the place where the Principle has its <term>origin<note>original: "urständet." A verb derived from "Urstand" (original foundation), referring to the arising or emerging of a fundamental reality.</note></term>, where the two inner worlds divide—one into darkness and the other into light. Therein is its eternal foundation. Into whichever world it now introduces its will, in that same world it also receives a substance, as a spiritual body; for that same substance becomes food for the soul-fire, or the material for its burning.

19. And therefore God has [led] the soul into flesh and blood—

<meta>catchword: led-</meta>

<summary>This page warns that the soul's ability to see through the physical body is only temporary, likening the material world to a "leased mirror." It explains that the soul must orient its will toward the divine light while still in the body, or risk being trapped in the "eternal house of mourning" after death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's nature and its relationship to the divine light. On the previous page, Böhme described how the soul must surrender its "self-will" to escape the "fire" of its own harsh, natural state. This page moves into Section 7, detailing how God provided the physical world (the "outer mirror") as a temporary light for the soul, and the danger of the soul relying solely on this temporary, physical vision.</meta>

## VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 5. Section 7.

<meta>catchword: led-</meta>

Therefore God has moved Himself to give this world a light, so that the outer life might stand in the light.

13. But regarding the inner life of souls, it is of a different form: the inner life cannot reach the outer. If the soul-fire does not possess God’s light, then the soul’s will cannot enter into God’s light; it must remain in the darkness of eternal nature.

14. Outer reason thinks that if the outer eye sees, then all is well, and there is no other seeing. Indeed, it is bad enough when the poor soul borrows from the outer mirror <note>The "outer mirror" refers to the physical world and the sun's light, which reflect a glimmer of divine reality but are not the reality itself.</note> and must rely on the outer alone: but where does its seeing remain when the outer mirror breaks? With what will it see then? With the anxious fire-flash <note>original: "Feuer-Blitz." In Böhme's thought, this is the sudden ignition of consciousness or desire that can either become a light or a consuming fire.</note> into cruelty, into darkness; otherwise, it can look nowhere.

15. Therefore it often happens, when the poor imprisoned soul looks into its inner root and thinks about what will follow when its outer mirror breaks, that it is terrified and plunges the body into anxiety and doubt.

16. For it can look nowhere where its eternal rest might be; instead, it finds that it is in pure unrest, and moreover in a darkness, and possesses the outer mirror only as a lease <note>original: "Lehens-weise." The soul does not "own" its physical sight; it is a temporary loan from the material world.</note>.

17. For because the soul is stuck in this outer body, it may well make use of the sun-mirror; for the sun contains within its root the inner fire, as the <term>Principle<note>original: "Principium." Böhme uses this Latin term to describe one of the three foundational realms or "worlds" of existence: the fire/power of the Father, the light/love of the Son, or the physical world.</note></term> of the Father. From that same fire, it receives a luster <note>original: "Glast." A brilliance or shining reflection.</note> or mirror, of which the essence of the body is a cause, so that it can thus be in joy in this earthly, transitory life. But when the outer mirror breaks, it is over, and the soul-fire goes into the eternal house of mourning, as into the center of darkness.

18. The soul has, during the time of the outer body, three mirrors or eyes of all three worlds; whichever mirror it turns toward, it sees through that one. But it has no more than one by natural right—that is the fire-flash, as the fourth form of the dark world, in the place where the <term>Origin<note>original: "Urstand." This refers to the primal source or the "standing forth" of an entity from the divine depths.</note></term> begins, where the two inner worlds divide: one into darkness, and the other into light. There is its eternal origin. Into whichever world it now leads its will, it also receives <term>Substance<note>original: "Wesen." This can mean "being," "essence," or "matter." In this context, it is the spiritual "food" the soul consumes to build its eternal body.</note></term> in that same world, as a spiritual body; for that same substance becomes food for the soul-fire, or the material for its burning.

19. And for this reason, God has led the soul into flesh and blood—

<summary>Böhme explains that while the physical sun and senses provide a temporary "mirror" for the soul to see by, the soul's true vision depends on which eternal realm—light or darkness—it directs its will toward. He warns that when the physical body "breaks," the soul will be left only with the light or darkness it has internalized.</summary>

<keywords>soul-fire, sun-mirror, fire-flash, Principle, origin, darkness, luster, substance, spiritual body</keywords>

### Part 5. Chapter 7. On Theosophical Points.

19. ...guided, so that it might not so easily become capable of the <term>fierce nature<note>original: "Grimmen Wesens." This refers to the wrathful, harsh, or "grim" quality of the dark fire-world which Böhme believes exists at the root of all nature.</note></term>; thus it finds its joy meanwhile in the mirror of the sun, and rejoices in the sidereal essence. And there stands against it: (1) the Light-world in its right fire, as in the <term>First Principle<note>original: "primo Principio." In Böhme’s system, the First Principle is the dark, fiery power of God's wrath and the root of existence.</note></term>. And (2) the dark world in the fire-root; and (3) the outer elemental world, in the <term>stellar source-power<note>original: "Sternen-Qual." This is a pun in Böhme's German: "Qual" means torment/pain, but he links it to "Quelle" or "quellen," meaning to spring or well up. It refers to the active, driving forces of the stars.</note></term>. There, between these, hovers the Great Mystery of the soul-fire.

20. Into whichever world the soul now adopts and gives itself, from that same world it receives a substance in its <term>imagination<note>original: "Imagination." For Böhme, this is not just "fantasy" but a creative, shaping power that "images" or forms the soul into a specific likeness.</note></term>. But because the soul has turned itself, along with Adam, into the spirit of this world and led its imagination into it; so now its highest desire stands in the source-power of the sun and stars, and it constantly draws into itself the spirit of the outer world, with its essence of the four elements. It has its greatest joy therein, in which it is a guest in a foreign inn (for the abyss stands beneath it), and there is great danger.

21. Now outer reason says: "Has not God created the soul in flesh and blood, into the outer world? What harm can that do it?" This reason knows no more of the soul's <term>origin<note>original: "Urstand." The primal state or original ground of being.</note></term> than a cow knows of a new stable door; she looks at it, and it seems strange to her. In the same way, the inner world seems strange to outer reason.

22. It perceives itself in the outer world and strives after what the outer world has, and yet it perceives within itself the inner world, which constantly accuses the soul before God’s wrath. Furthermore, it also perceives the Light-world, where the inner desires of the soul's principle tend. It indeed perceives the longing for God; but the outer world prevents this and covers it up, so that the desire for God’s world might not ignite the fire within itself. If that were to happen, the Light-world would become manifest in the first principle, and the noble image according to God would become manifest.

23. This is also prevented by the devil, who possesses the root of this world in the soul-fire, and always holds evil earthly things before the soul; or he stirs up the root in the <term>center of nature<note>original: "Centro der Natur." A technical term in Böhme's philosophy for the swirling, hungry forces that precede the manifestation of light.</note></term> in fierceness, so that the poor soul is ignited either in the fire of anger in the evil poisonous torment, or indeed in anxiety and doubt of God’s love. There he has won, and he sets before the soul outward power, authority, and honor, as well as the luster and splendor of the outer world; there she bites the bait, and—

<summary>Böhme describes the soul as hovering between the light, dark, and elemental worlds, warning that it has become dangerously preoccupied with the physical world. He explains that "outer reason" is blind to the soul's true spiritual origins and that the devil exploits this ignorance to keep the soul trapped in earthly desires or despair.</summary>

### Part 5. Chapter 7. On Theosophic Points.

...guided, so that it might not so easily become capable of the <term>wrathful nature<note>original: "Grimmen Wesen." In Böhme's thought, "Grimm" refers to the harsh, fierce, or stinging quality of nature when it is not tempered by divine love.</note></term>; thus, it takes its joy meanwhile in the **solar-mirror**, and rejoices in **sidereal essence**. And there stand against it: (1) the **Light-world** in its right fire, as in the first Principle; and (2) the **dark world** in the fire-root; and (3) the **outer elementary world**, in the <term>starry source-torment<note>original: "Sternen-Qual." Böhme often puns on the word "Qual" (torment/pain) and "Quelle" (source/wellspring), implying that the stars are the churning source of our physical reality.</note></term>, and between these hovers the great mystery of the soul-fire.

20. Into whichever world the soul now adopts and gives itself, from that same world it receives its substance through its <term>imagination<note>For Böhme, "imagination" is not just fantasy, but a creative power that literally "images" or forms the soul into what it desires.</note></term>. However, since the soul has turned itself with Adam into the spirit of this world and led its imagination into it, its highest desire now stands in the source-torment of the sun and stars. It constantly draws into itself the spirit of the outer world, with its essence of the four elements, and finds its greatest joy therein—even though it is merely a guest in a **foreign inn** (for the **abyss** stands beneath it), and there is great danger.

21. Now **outer reason** says: "But did God not create us in flesh and blood, in the outer world? What harm can that do the soul?" This reason knows no more of the soul's <term>original state<note>original: "Urstand." The primordial or essential state of a being before its fall.</note></term> than a **cow knows of a new stable-door**; she looks at it, and it seems strange to her. In the same way, the inner world seems strange to outer reason.

22. It perceives itself in the outer world and strives after that which the outer world possesses; yet it still feels within itself the inner world, which constantly accuses the soul before **God's wrath**. Furthermore, it feels the Light-world, toward which the inner desires of the soul's Principle lean. It certainly feels the longing for God; but the outer world prevents this and covers it up, so that the desire for God's world might not ignite the fire within itself. If that were to happen, the Light-world would become manifest in the first Principle, and the **noble image** according to God would be revealed.

23. This is also prevented by the **Devil**, who possesses the root of this world in the soul-fire. He always holds evil, earthly matters before the soul, or indeed stirs up the root in the **Center of Nature** in wrath, so that the poor soul ignites either in the fire of anger—in the <term>poisonous source-torment<note>original: "Gift-Qual."</note></term>—or indeed in anxiety and doubt regarding God's love. In that moment, he has won. He presents the soul with outward power, authority, and honor, as well as the luster and splendor of the outer world; then the soul bites the hook and—

<summary>Böhme describes the soul as suspended between the light, dark, and elementary worlds, warning that "Reason" is blind to the soul's true origin. He explains how the Devil uses the distractions and power of the physical world to keep the soul's inner divine light from igniting.</summary>

# VI. On Six Points
## Part 5. Chapter 7.

...tickles itself therein with **imagination**, and yet cannot properly enjoy it, for it is only a borrowed mirror.

24. Thus the poor soul is drawn away from God's light and sinks ever further into destruction, as if into the **Dark-House of misery**, into the dark world. Adam prepared this for us when he introduced his desire into earthly things; so now the poor soul swims in earthly flesh and blood, and eats constantly from the **Tree of Temptation** <note>original: "Versuch-Baum," usually referring to the Tree of the Knowledge of Good and Evil.</note> of both evil and good, and is pulled violently by both; and the **Serpent’s Monster** <note>original: "Schlangen Monstrum," representing the distorted, selfish nature the soul inherited after the Fall.</note> sits in the center, in the torment of wrath, and constantly breathes out fury and anger.

25. Then the noble **little Lily-branch** <note>A signature Böhme metaphor: the "lily" is the new spiritual life or "new man" that grows out of the dark, "wrathful" soil of the soul.</note> can find no rest anywhere, and often cannot even recognize itself; it is often so overwhelmed with the wrath of wickedness that it seems as if it were entirely corrupted. Indeed, it would be corrupted if the **Mirror of the Deity** did not stand before it, in which the willing-spirit of the poor captive souls may find recovery and be born anew within it.

26. For in the mirror of the Light-world, the **incarnation of Christ** stands before the soul-spirit; and the Word that became man stands in the sound <note>original: "Schalle." Böhme uses "sound" or "resonance" to describe the active, creative expression of God's Word through the universe.</note> and is active. The soul-spirit can find restoration and be born anew within it; otherwise, it would often be all over for the poor soul if it were to become "bedeviled" in the wrath and poison of the dark world.

27. Thus we fundamentally understand what the corruption of the noble tree—the **Image of God**—is, namely this:

28. The whole human being, in his essence, is the **three worlds**. (1) The soul's center, as the root of the soul-fire, contains the **dark world**, and the soul-fire contains the **First Principle** <note>original: "erste Principium." In Böhme's philosophy, this is the foundational, dark, fiery power of God's potential energy or "wrath."</note>, as the true fire-world. (2) And the noble image (as the tree of divine growth), which is born out of the soul-fire and blossoms through fierce death into freedom—into the Light-world—contains the **Light-world**, as the **Second Principle**. (3) And the body, which in the beginning was created from the mixed essence produced in the creation from the light- and dark- as well as the fire-world, contains the **outer world**, as the **third mixed principle**.

29. And the true soul is the spirit of these three worlds, just as God’s Spirit is the spirit of all three worlds. (1) In the dark world, He is fierce, severe, and an earnest torment, and is called **God’s wrath**. (2) In the Light-world, He is lovely,

<meta>catchword: gentle</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains the tripartite nature of the human soul, which exists simultaneously in the dark world of wrath, the light world of God's love, and the physical world. He emphasizes that the soul can only find rebirth through Christ, the "Mirror of the Deity," which protects the fragile spiritual "lily" from being consumed by inner darkness.</summary>

# Part 5. Chapter 7. 
## On Theosophical Points

...gentle and rich in joy, and is the Spirit from God’s heart, as the Holy Spirit. (3) And in the external world, he is the spirit of the air, as well as of fire and water, and allows himself to be used as man wills, all for the sake of great wonders.

30. Thus, according to his person, man is the **Great Mystery** <term>Mysterium: a hidden spiritual reality or source containing all possibilities</term> in the three worlds: whichever one he turns himself toward, in that one he works fruit; that world becomes lord in him, and that same world becomes manifest in him, while the other two remain hidden; just as fire lies hidden in wood, so also the Light, or the **Light-world**, remains hidden in the fierce, dark world, as well as in the wickedness and the craving of the inner world within the external world.

31. However, if the Light-world cannot become manifest in man so that it becomes Lord, then the soul remains—at the breaking of the external world <note>This refers to the moment of death, when the physical body and the "outer world" pass away.</note>—purely in the dark world; for there it can no longer be that the Light-world is ignited. There is no longer any mirror for the light within it to face the soul; the Heart of God is not manifest therein, nor can it ever be; for the dark world must exist, otherwise the Light would not be manifest, but here in this world, it can be so.

32. And even if a soul is deeply immersed in the deepest abyss and stuck in the **Wrath of God** <note>original: "Grimme GOttes," the harsh, consuming, and fiery quality of spiritual existence apart from God's love.</note>, the Light-mirror of the sun still stands before it in the external light, wherein the Divine power manifests itself, as well as the mirror of the **Incarnation of Christ**, which will never be recognized in the inner dark world for all eternity.

33. And our entire teaching is nothing else but how man should ignite God’s Light-world within himself. For when this is ignited, so that God’s light shines in the soul’s spirit, then the whole body has light, as Christ says: 

> If the eye is light, the whole body is light; (Matthew 6: 22, 23.)

He understands this as the soul-eye. And when the wrath of the dark world is ignited, then body and soul are dark, and have only a glimmer from the sun. When the Divine light is ignited, it burns in love and gentleness: and when the wrath of the dark world is ignited, it burns in prickly envy and hatred, in fierce anger, and flies out in pride within the external mirror of the sun’s light, always wanting to rise above the **source-quality** <note>original: "Quall," a specific Böhme term for the active, bubbling quality or "drive" of a spiritual state.</note> of love; then follow mockery and contempt for gentleness, and for everything that is lowly.

<summary>Böhme explains that man contains all three spiritual worlds and must choose which one to "ignite" within himself during his earthly life. He warns that if the soul does not find God's Light-world before death, it will remain trapped in the dark world of wrath without a "mirror" to see God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s condition after death, specifically how its internal desires and "will-spirit" determine its ultimate form and destination. # VI. On Six Points. Chapter 5. 7.
## Db

34. And here, a person should test themselves to see which world is master within them. If he finds that wrath, fury, envy, falsehood, lies, and deception are his desire; and then pride, greed, and a constant craving for honors and external pleasure, such that he is merely a constant addiction to lechery and unchastity, then he may very well take stock of himself. He can know for certain that with this wrath, fury, envy, falsehood, lies, and deception, he burns in the darkness as if in the fire of the dark world: for that same fire provides such an essence, desire, and will.

35. And the other desire—namely external pleasure, pride, ambition, greed, and a constant, bestial craving for unchastity—is the fruit that grows out of the dark world into the external world.

36. Just as love grows green out of death (when the **will-spirit** <term>Willen-Geist: the core intentionality of the soul that directs its spiritual energy</term> yields itself into the fire of God and sinks down as if into death, yet blooms forth in God's kingdom with a friendly desire to always do good); so too has the will of malice yielded itself into destruction, as into the fierce, severe, eternal death. It grows its branches out into the external nature of this corrupted world and bears such fruit.

37. By this, everyone should learn to recognize themselves. He need only investigate his own character to see what his will constantly drives him toward; in that kingdom he stands. He is not a human being, as he considers and portrays himself to be, but rather a creature of the dark world: such as a spiritual dog, a proud bird, an unchaste beast, a fierce serpent, or an envious toad full of poison, and so on. All these properties well up within him and are his "wood," from which his fire burns. When the external wood—that is, the substance of the four elements—leaves him at his death, only the internal, poisonous, evil **torment** <note>original: "Quall." Böhme uses this term to mean both "torment" and a "welling source" or "quality."</note> remains.

38. What kind of figure shall stand in such a property? None other than whichever of these properties was the strongest; that one will be shaped into its form by the hellish **Fiat** <term>Fiat: Latin for "let it be done," referring to the creative power of the Word that gives shape to spiritual reality</term>. It may be shaped into a poisonous serpent, a dog, or something similar, or another beast, and so on. Into whichever property the will-spirit has yielded itself, that same property is thereafter the image of the soul; and this is one part of it.

39. Furthermore, a person should examine themselves in their desires; (for every person has these evil properties within them;)

<summary>Böhme explains that a person’s inner desires—whether toward vices like pride and greed or toward divine love—determine whether they belong to the "dark world" or "light world." After death, the soul loses its physical body and is shaped by the "hellish Fiat" into a form reflecting its strongest vice, appearing as a spiritual beast or serpent.</summary>

<keywords>will-spirit, self-examination, dark world, four elements, soul-image, Fiat, spiritual transformation, Jacob Böhme, vices, virtues</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the "test" or self-examination of the soul, building on the previous page’s description of the soul as a mirror or creature of different "worlds." It focuses on the internal struggle between the lower "serpent" nature and the higher "divine" light.</meta>

# Part 5. Chapter 7. On Theosophical Points.

Whether he also finds a constant desire within him to kill this poison and malice? Whether he is an enemy to this poison? Or whether he takes his joy in it, constantly putting that false poison into practice through: pride, greed, envy, unchastity, lies, and deception?

40. If he now finds within himself that he takes his joy in these things, and is always eager to put them into practice, then he is not a human being, as he considers himself to be; rather, the devil—in a foreign form—deceives him into believing he is a human. But he carries not God’s image, but the image of the serpent. So long as he remains in this condition—where this nature is the master—he is only a likeness of a human form in the external realm.

41. But if he finds a struggle within himself, where his inner will always—indeed, hourly—strives against these evil qualities, suppresses them, and does not allow them to manifest as an evil being, so that he would always gladly do good; and yet he finds these evil qualities hindering him, so that he cannot always put into practice what he desires; and if he finds a desire for abstinence and repentance, such that a constant longing for God’s mercy wells up in him, and he would gladly do good if only he could;

42. Then such a person may consider, and know for certain, that God’s fire is smoldering in him and is always working toward the light. It would gladly burn, and it constantly provides essence for the blaze, but it is suppressed by the evil dampness of this world, which Adam <note>Refers to the concept of "Original Sin" or the fallen state of humanity inherited from the biblical first man.</note> introduced into us.

43. So, when the external, evil body with its vapors finally breaks apart, so that it can no longer hinder the smoldering wick; then the Divine fire ignites in its essence, and the Divine image is formed <note>original: "figuriret"</note> once again, according to the strongest drive <term>Qual: a Böhmean term combining "torment" (Qual) with "source" (Quelle); it refers to the intense, vibrating quality or inner tension that gives rise to a specific state of being</term> that the person carried in his desire while here, according to his strongest attribute. However, if he does not remain in this aforementioned knighthood <note>A metaphor for the spiritual struggle or "warfare" of the Christian life.</note>, but allows the struggle to subside, he may very dangerously fall back into ruin.

44. The third test <note>original: "Proba"</note> is this: that a person recognizes what kind of being or figure they stand in. If he finds that he has a constant desire for God, and is so powerful in his desire that he can repeatedly break the evil essences whenever a drive <gloss>Qual</gloss> is ignited in him, and transform them into gentleness—that is, to step into patience—so that he is master of his own being, and lets everything sink...

<sig>D 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: everything</meta>

<summary>This page describes the spiritual psychology of self-examination, where the presence of a struggle against one's own "poisonous" impulses is seen as proof that a divine spark still flickers within the soul. Böhme emphasizes that our true spiritual form is determined by our deepest desires and our persistence in the "spiritual knighthood" or struggle against our lower nature.</summary>

<keywords>self-examination, serpent's image, divine fire, spiritual knighthood, Qual, essence, internal struggle, repentance, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "test" or self-examination of the soul, building on the previous page’s description of the soul as a mirror or creature of different "worlds." It moves from the internal struggle to the description of the "new man" and begins Chapter 8 regarding the human essence and the role of baptism.</meta>

[The person who rejects] all that glitters and shines in this world; who can do good for evil; who has mastery over all his external being—whether it be money or goods—to give to the needy, and to leave it all for the sake of God’s truth; and who willingly enters into misery for God’s sake, with certain hope of the Eternal: in him the Divine power wells up, so that he may kindle the light of the Kingdom of Joy <note>original: "Freudenreich"</note> therein; he who tastes what God is: he is the most certain, and carries the Divine Image with heavenly essence within himself, even during the time of the external body.

45. There, JESUS is born of the Virgin <note>This refers to the "mystical birth" of Christ within the soul of the believer, a central theme in this tradition where the soul becomes a vessel for the divine.</note>, and the person never dies eternally; he only lets the external realm depart from him, which in this time was an opposition and hindrance to him, with which God had concealed him. For God will not "throw pearls before swine" <note>A reference to Matthew 7:6; the spiritual mysteries are hidden from those who would mistreat or misunderstand them.</note>; they are hidden in Him.

46. That same new man <term>New Man: In mystical theology, the spiritual self that is born again through grace, as opposed to the "Old Man" or the fallen, ego-driven nature.</term> does not stand in this world; the devil does not know him either, but is only hateful toward his essence <note>original: "Essenz"</note>, which holds the inner Center <term>Center (Centrum): The core of a being where the spiritual forces originate and the divine light is kindled.</term>; for it prevents his will from being done. Therefore, he incites the evil animal-men <note>Those living only by their lower, instinctive, or "bestial" natures rather than their spiritual ones.</note> against him, so that they plague and persecute him, in order that true humanity might remain concealed.

# Chapter 8.
## On the Right Human Essence from God’s Being.
### Summary of Points.

1. The right, true human essence is not earthly, nor from the dark world, 2. although it lies within the external man, 3. and first becomes active in the Rebirth <note>Wiedergeburt: The spiritual transformation and awakening of the soul.</note>. 4. Where already in the Baptism of children, the Word of the Deity betroths itself. 5. But Christ wants to have little children, not animals, 6. so that he who only wills it himself can indeed be helped. 7. But it is also not the meaning that Baptism is the first fuel for the Divine Fire, 8. as that fire already glows in the mother’s womb; 9. and even if the child should die before Baptism. 10. 11. For the parents' Baptism is also the child's; 12. godless parents, on the other hand, beget godless children; 13. God’s water must baptize, 14. which does not give itself over to Wrath <term>Wrath (Grimm): A technical term for the harsh, fire-like quality of nature or the "dark side" of God's power when it is not tempered by love and light.</term>, 15. and can already happen in the mother’s womb, if the Divine Essence is active in the child: for the Incarnation <note>original: "Menschwerdung"</note> of Christ does not wait for human power, but for the Goal, 16. which reaches behind and before itself. Part 5. Chapter 8. On Theosophical Points.

17. The Covenant of Grace was established with Adam; 18. baptism, however, is the seal of this covenant. 19. Though God’s wrath swallows many a soul 20. when the false human brings forth false seed <note>Referring to children born of parents who are spiritually "false" or ungodly.</note>. 21. For as the seed is in marriage, so is the fruit. 22. Thus the Devil sows his weeds into the mind <note>original: "Gemüth"; refers to the soul's disposition, temperament, or inner emotional state.</note>, 23. so that godless spouses often curse each other into the very soul. 24. Nevertheless, the covenant remains with baptism, and God’s Spirit baptizes everyone, but according to the child’s own nature. 25. It is the same with the use of the Lord’s Supper <note>original: "Abendmahl"</note>, which the godless receive to their own judgment. 26. 27. It would be better if a completely godless child were not baptized. 28. As the soul is, so is the medicine <note>original: "Artzney"; the author views the sacraments as a form of spiritual medicine for the soul.</note>. 29. And whatever the will of a thing desires, that it receives. 30. And what we make of ourselves, that is what we are. 31.

1. The right, true human essence <term>Essence (Essenz): The spiritual substance or "inner body" of a person that belongs to the divine realm, distinct from the physical body.</term> is not earthly, nor does it come from the dark world; it is born solely in the World of Light. It has no communion with either the dark or the external world; there is a great closure <note>original: "Schluß"; implies a total separation or a final barrier.</note>, like death, between them.

2. This is not to suggest that nothing of the true essence lies within the external human; it does lie therein, for it was given to Adam in his image. However, it is locked away and lies in death; it cannot operate <note>original: "qualificiren"; a technical term meaning to interact or manifest its qualities.</note>, nor does it have any stirring or movement within itself, unless it is stirred by the power of the Godhead. Just as it was stirred in the Virgin Mary through God’s movement and entry, the true human essence then came back to life.

3. Likewise, in us, the true human essence is not stirred unless we are born of God in Christ.

4. In the baptism of children, the Word of the Godhead joins itself to them and enters into a covenant with them. This is the first stirring in this world, like punk-wood <note>original: "Moder"; refers to soft, decayed wood used as tinder to catch a spark and begin a fire.</note> that begins to glow; but the little wick is often later darkened and extinguished. Furthermore, it is not well-received in many who are begotten from a completely godless essence.

5. For Christ said: "Let the little children come to me, for of such is the kingdom of God." (Mark 10:14.) Not dogs, wolves, toads, or snakes, but children in whom the essence is not entirely devilish—though some are baptized in the wrath of God, for which the parents bear the guilt. For "a bad tree brings forth bad fruit," says Christ.

6. And although He came into this world to save that which is lost, it still depends on the essence whether it will allow itself to be helped. For a beast-man <term>Beast-man (Thier-Mensch): A person who lives entirely by their animal instincts and worldly desires, having no awareness of their spiritual nature.</term> may well [have] the im-

# VI. On Six Points Part 5. Chapter 8.

[knowledge] to attain, if he turns back and allows himself to be drawn by the Word (which became human); if not, he remains an evil beast in his animal essence.

7. Yet one should not be of the opinion that baptism lays the first foundation for the human essence, or is entirely the first kindling or tinder <note>original: "Moder oder Zunder"; Böhme uses these terms to describe the latent spiritual material that can be ignited by the Divine fire.</note> of the Divine fire; no, that is not it, for a child becomes a spirit from the parents' essence, along with flesh and blood, through the marriage with the constellation of the spirit of the *Greater World* <term>Majoris Mundi: Latin for the "Greater World" or Macrocosm. Böhme refers here to the external universe and its astrological/spiritual influences on the physical body.</term>.

8. At the same time when a child receives life in the mother's womb, a Divine or hellish essence immediately glimmers forth from its first origin and descent.

9. And because even a small amount of kindling or tinder of the Divine essence is active <note>original: "räge"; implies a stirring or awakening of spiritual potential.</note>, the child is capable of baptism. And even if it were to die and not be baptized, that tinder or kindling is still within God’s Mystery <note>original: "Mysterio"</note>, and it glimmers in God’s Kingdom, and is ignited in the fire of God; for it dies in the Mystery of the Father and glimmers upward in the Mystery of the Son, who became man.

10. The parents' baptism and covenant is the child's own baptism and covenant; the reconciliation has occurred in human blood, in the right, true human essence. God’s Word or Heart has given itself into the enclosed, dead human essence—not into the earthly part, not at all into the earthly part, but into the heavenly part. Not into the part that Adam introduced through his imagination <note>In Böhme’s thought, "imagination" is the soul's power to form itself; Adam's fall was a misdirection of this power toward the earthly.</note>, which is earth; but into the part that was given to Adam from the Angelic World <note>original: "Englischen Welt"; referring to the realm of angels, not the nation of England.</note>, which he corrupted and poisoned with earthly craving <note>original: "Sucht"; refers to a disordered longing or passion for the material world.</note>, so that in this craving it became earthly, clumsy, animal flesh.

11. That same part contains the true human essence; and in that same part, God became man; and that same part has the foundation of the Angelic World, for it originates from the angelic world.

12. However, if godless parents often immerse themselves entirely in the wrath of God and thus conceive children in wrath, then their seed is indeed enclosed in death and has nothing of the true human essence stirring within it, except for that which the constellation in the spirit of the *Greater World* <term>Majoris Mundi: original Latin, referring to the Macrocosm or the outer universe.</term> contains; there, the Divine power is somewhat active. But the power of wrath is the opposite and is heavy, yet it is possible, for God’s Incarnation—

### Part 5. Chapter 8. On Theosophical Points.

[The Incarnation] is set over against the Light of Life in all souls.

13. But Baptism holds a different significance: God’s Essence (as the water of eternal life born out of God’s gentleness <note>original: "Sanftmuth"; in Böhme’s thought, this represents the light-world or the "Second Principle," which is characterized by love and stillness as opposed to the "Wrath" of the first principle.</note>) must stir the true human essence which was enclosed in death with Adam, and there surrender itself as a new life (or a living essence). God’s water must baptize; the Holy Spirit must be the master builder.

14. But I say according to my knowledge, that the water of eternal life, upon which the Holy Spirit hovers, will hardly surrender itself into the poison of wrath and death unless an essence of desire <note>Böhme often uses "desire" (Begierde) as the fundamental "magnetic" force that pulls spiritual substance into form.</note> is within it.

15. I say therefore, as I recognize it, that even a child (as soon as it has life in the mother's womb), provided that the Divine Essence (which consists in the heavenly part) is active <note>original: "räge"; meaning stirring, moving, or awakened.</note>, is already baptized by the Holy Spirit and reaches the Incarnation of Christ <term>Menschwerdung Christi: The process of Christ becoming human, which Böhme believes can be spiritually replicated or "attained" within the individual soul.</term>. For baptism does not stand in the priest's power, as if the Holy Spirit had to wait for him: for the Incarnation of Christ did not wait for human power, but for the goal <note>original: "Ziel"; also translates as "aim" or "mark."</note> that God set in His covenant; that goal was blessed. Therefore the Angel said to Mary: *You are the blessed among women*; the goal was set in her, that was blessed, and that also blessed her when God’s Heart made the goal active.

16. That same goal reached backward even unto Adam, and forward unto the very last human: and when God became man, the goal in the heavenly part was made active; not alone in Mary, but also in Adam and Eve, and all their children who had surrendered themselves into God; they were all blessed in the goal.

17. For that is the Covenant of Grace which God established with Adam and Eve; the same covenant stands in all human essence, but not in devilish essence.

18. Baptism, however, is the seal that God attached to the covenant, just as circumcision was in the Old Testament: in baptism, God gives Divine water to the human race as a pledge and a seal; but the covenant is already there before baptism, it was made in Paradise, indeed, before the foundation of the world. As soon as a soul is active <note>original: "räge"</note> in the mother's womb, so that the Principle <note>Böhme's term for the fundamental source or "starting point" of a specific reality (i.e., the dark world, light world, or outer world).</note> and a human soul are born, it is in the covenant; for

<summary>Böhme explains that Baptism is a physical seal of a spiritual Covenant of Grace that exists even before birth. He argues that the Holy Spirit can "baptize" a child in the womb through the stirring of the Divine Essence, independent of a priest's ritual.</summary>

### VI. Concerning Six Points. Chapter 8.

Christ has entered into the fire of God, into the Principle <note>original: "Principium"; Böhme uses this term to describe the fundamental realms of reality—specifically the first principle (the fire of God's wrath) and the second principle (the light of God's love).</note>, and has fulfilled the Covenant, and has become the prize <note>original: "Außbeute"; literally "booty" or "yield," referring here to the spiritual inheritance won by Christ.</note> of the Testament.

19. This same prize does not wait for any external order, or for the delusions of the external man; rather, as soon as a soul is born out of the Principle, it is within the prize of the Testament, provided that the Divine Life is active <note>original: "rege"; stirring or awakened.</note> in the soul. But this is not so in godless souls; there, the Divine Life must first be born. God’s wrath swallows many a soul, even while still in its essence <term>Essence: In Böhme's vocabulary, "Essenz" refers to the spiritual substance or "stuff" that makes up the soul and its tendencies.</term>, before it reaches the Principle, because it is composed of a false essence from the evil seed of the parents.

20. Reason says: "What is a child to do if the parents are godless?" Yes, and what is God to do? Does it not lie within the parents' power to make a child? What is God’s fault in this, that harlots and rascals crawl together? Although the false tree does not originate from this lineage alone, but also within marriage: yet the human being is free; if he awakens no life, his seed remains an essence. Should God, then, for the sake of the child's innocence, throw pearls before swine? Yet the Kingdom of Heaven stands open to him; he may enter, for God excludes no one.

21. But an evil person is locked away in body and soul; why then should he not also be locked away in the seed? For the seed is indeed the fruit of his body. If one wishes to harvest good wheat, it is only right to sow wheat; but if thistle-seed is sown, a thistle grows from it—should God then transform it into wheat? Does the sower not have the power to sow whatever he wishes upon his field? Or would you say: "What is the thistle’s fault, that it is a thistle and stings?" It does not belong among the wheat, yet it grows up alongside it.

22. Indeed, God would be well pleased if no thistle-child grew at all; it is not His order. Rather, the devil sows weeds <note>original: "Unkraut"; often translated as "tares" in biblical contexts.</note> among the wheat, namely into the human mind. Why does the human being allow this and corrupt himself, so that his essence becomes a thistle-seed and carries weeds to the fire in the wrath <note>original: "Grimme"; referring to the dark, consuming aspect of the first principle.</note> of God? Moreover, not everything depends on the seed, but also on the field; many a noble, beautiful grain perishes in the essence of the evil field. Heaven with the sun gives life and power to all growth: The sun

### VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 5, Section 8.

Christ has entered himself into the fire of God, into the Principle <term>Principium: In Böhme's cosmology, the first of three realms, representing the fiery, energetic foundation of God’s power from which the Light (Second Principle) emerges.</term>, and has fulfilled the Covenant, and has become the fruit <note>original: "Ausbeute"; literally the "yield" or "spoils," referring to the spiritual profit or benefit gained through Christ's victory over death.</note> of the Testament.

19. This same fruit does not wait for any outward order or for the delusions of the external person; instead, as soon as a soul is born out of the Principle, it is within the fruit of the Testament, provided that the Divine Life is active <note>original: "rege"; stirring, moving, or awakened.</note> within the soul. But this is not so in godless souls; in them, the Divine Life must first be born. God’s wrath swallows many a soul even while it is still in its essence <term>Essenz: The formative spiritual substance or "stuff" that makes up a being.</term>, before it even reaches the Principle, because it arises from a false essence, from the evil seed of the parents.

20. Reason asks: How is the child at fault if the parents are godless? Indeed, what is it to God? Is it not within the parents' power to produce a child? Why is it God’s concern when harlots and rascals crawl together? Although the "false tree" does not originate from this lineage alone, but also within marriage: for man is free; if he awakens no life, his seed remains a mere essence. Should God, then, for the sake of the child's innocence, throw pearls before swine? Yet the Kingdom of Heaven stands open to him; he may enter, for God excludes no one.

21. But an evil person is closed off in body and soul; why then not also in the seed? For the seed is the fruit of his body. If one wishes to harvest good wheat <note>original: "Weißen"; an archaic spelling of "Weizen" (wheat).</note>, it is only right to sow wheat. If, however, thistle seed is sown, a thistle grows from it; should God then transform it into wheat? Does the sower not have the power to sow what he wishes upon his field? Or would you say: "How is the thistle at fault for being a thistle and stinging?" It does not belong among the wheat, yet it grows up alongside it.

22. God would be well pleased if no "thistle-child" ever grew, for it is not His order; rather, the devil sows weeds among the wheat—that is, into the human mind <term>Gemüthe: A central term in Böhme’s writing referring to the seat of the soul’s will, thoughts, and emotions.</term>. Why does man allow this and corrupt himself, so that his essence becomes a thistle seed and carries weeds into the fire of God’s fury? It does not depend entirely on the seed, but also on the field; many a noble, beautiful grain rots in the essence of an evil field. The heavens with the sun give life and power to all that grows. ### Part 5. Chapter 8. On Theosophical Points.

...produces no weeds, nor does it desire any; however, the essence in the field often produces something else and corrupts what is good.

23. It is the same within a human being; many a curse clings, which one person wishes upon another, when the other has invited the curse and is capable of receiving it. This is common among godless married couples, where one wishes the devil and hellfire upon the other. If they are both godless, should their godless will not then be fulfilled, so that they beget godless children? Since there is nothing good in them, what good could come out of them? How can God be blamed for this? He places His Word and teaching before them and announces their destruction; if they do not want it, they go where they choose to go. Such is also their seed, and thus many a child is born as a thistle and an evil beast, and is baptized in the wrath of God <note>In Böhme’s thought, "Wrath" (Grimm) is not just an emotion but a fundamental, dark spiritual principle—the fire of existence without the light of God's love.</note>.

24. For whatever essence the soul-spirit is made of, in such an essence it also receives the Divine Being in the Covenant; one in the power of light, in love—the other in the power of wrath, in darkness.

25. The Covenant of Baptism remains: every child is baptized in the Covenant, and the Spirit of God baptizes every child if the custom is observed, but according to the child's own quality <note>original: "Eigenschaft"; this refers to the child's inherent spiritual nature or disposition.</note>. Often the father and mother, as well as the one performing the baptism, are godless and are merely evil beasts, and they are not in earnest; they care most for outward display and money, merely despising the Mystery <term>Mystery: Böhme uses the Latin/Greek "Mysterium" to refer to the sacred, hidden reality of the sacraments.</term>, and the child too is of the quality of wrath. Who then shall baptize? None other than God’s wrath in His Covenant, because the sacrament is only being mocked.

26. Thus the source of wrath <note>original: "Zorn-Quell"</note> seizes the new spirit, works powerfully within it, and brings forth fruit unto destruction—just as St. Paul says regarding the Last Supper and the other Testament: that the godless person receives it to judgment because he does not discern the Lord’s body (1 Cor. 11:29). That is, he does not distinguish the heavenly part of his essence within him from the earthly, nor does he set his will toward the heavenly and offer that up to God; instead, he treats it all as common, just as an ox eats its fodder.

27. Therefore the wrath of God wells up within him, because he does not break his will away from the earthly and enter into repentance for his wickedness; therefore his heavenly part cannot partake in God’s body, because he [possesses] the essence of the heavenly part...

# VI. Concerning Six Points. Chapter 5, Paragraph 8

27. ...cannot make it <term>active</term> <note>original: "räge"; Böhme uses this to describe a spiritual principle being stirred into motion or awakened.</note>: So the soul also has no mouth to receive God’s Body, for the mouth lies locked in death; nonetheless, the earthly part receives Christ’s Body, but according to the <term>disposition</term> <note>original: "Eigenschaft"; refers to the inherent quality or nature of a thing.</note> of wrath, according to the disposition of the dark world; for the Testament <note>Refers to the Sacrament of the Altar or Holy Communion.</note> must stand firm.

28. It is the same in the case of Baptism: as the essence of the soul is in its being, so it also enjoys God’s Covenant. It would be better if a completely godless child were not baptized; and if a godless person in his sins, without conversion, did not touch God’s Testament; for to both of them it brings only the power for destruction. For God’s Covenant is stirred, and that never happens without fruit. God works in His Covenant according to His Word.

29. As the soul is which stirs the Covenant, so also is the medicine within the Covenant; and in such power the Spirit of God works in love and in wrath, for He is the Spirit of all life and likens Himself to all life. He is in every thing according to that thing’s will and disposition, for one disposition grasps the other: what the soul wills, that also wills He into whom she turns herself.

30. Everything is magical: whatever the will of a thing desires, that it receives. A toad takes only poison into itself, even if it were sitting in the finest apothecary, and likewise a snake. Every thing takes only its own disposition into itself; even if it ate the substance of a good disposition, it would still turn everything into its own nature. Even if a toad ate honey, it would still become poison within it. Just as the devil was once an angel, but because he desired nothing good, his heavenly being became hellish poison to him, and his evil will remained evil forever.

31. Therefore, we must highly consider our lives, what we wish to do and intend; we have both evil and good in us. Whichever one we draw our will from, its essence becomes <term>active</term> within us, and we also draw such a disposition into ourselves from the outside. We have both Mysteries, the Divine and the Devilish, within us from both eternal worlds, as well as from the outer world. What we make of ourselves, that is what we are; what we awaken in ourselves, that is what is active within us. If we lead ourselves toward the good, God’s Spirit helps us; but if we lead ourselves toward evil, God’s <term>fierceness</term> <note>original: "Grimm"; a technical term in Böhme’s theosophy for the harsh, fire-like quality of God’s nature that becomes "wrath" when separated from His light and love.</note> and wrath help us. What we will, that is our disposit—

<meta>catchword: —ion (Eigenschaft)</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that the sacraments are not "magic charms" that work regardless of the recipient's heart; rather, one's internal spiritual nature (disposition) determines whether God's power is received as life-giving love or destructive wrath.</summary>

Part 6. Chapter 9. On Theosophical Points.
Sig: E
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

we obtain a leader, and we lead ourselves into that state: for it is not the will of the Godhead that we perish, but rather the will of His <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Zorn"; in Böhme’s thought, this is the harsh, consuming fire of God’s nature when it is not tempered by His love.</note>, and our own will.
Thus we understand the fifth point: how a life might be destroyed, how from something good something evil may arise, and from something evil something good, whenever the will turns itself around.

## The Sixth Point.
### Concerning the Life of Darkness, wherein the Devils Dwell, and what Kind of Birth and Torment it has.
#### Chapter 9.

->Summaries.<-

The forms of darkness are hostile to one another. §. 1, 2. The devil is a liar, 3. an eternal death and enmity. 4. This realm of <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimmen-Reich"; the domain of fierce, uncontrolled spiritual fire.</note> cannot be tamed by anything, except solely by God's light; 5. yet it must exist. 6. Its life is not uniform, 7, 8. but stands in poison, which the light alone resists; 9. it is a languishing poison; 10. the evidence of <term>pride</term> <note>original: "Hoffart"; often used by Böhme to describe the self-centered ego that seeks to rise above God.</note>, 11. a rising up within itself, standing in seven forms. 12. The greater the anguish: the greater is the poisonous life. 13. The devil does not despair; but the light lays low his pomp: 14. therefore he rages and storms against God. ibid. The more evil the creature of the dark world is: the greater is its power. 15. This external world is an image of the dark one, 16. and stands upon that same foundation. 17. Therefore humans and animals are against one another, ibid. according to the property of the dark world. 18. The birth of the dark world stands in the first four forms, 19. wherein the various torments make the eternal wonders manifest. 20. Therefore fallen man shall learn how he may resist the wrath. 21. If he walks in love and gentleness: he has no law. 22. Everything that has grown from one root belongs to one tree; 23. but if it deviates into another <term>torment</term> <note>original: "Qual"; for Böhme, this refers both to suffering and to the "welling up" or "quality" of a spiritual force.</note>; then it lies in strife with the first: 24. thus was man created for love. ibid. But because the wrath has overcome the love: he must now be born anew from the first root. 25. So it now stands in his power to give birth to the spirit of wrath or of love. 26. 27. Hence the great struggle within man. 28. When life gives itself entirely over to the wrath, it destroys the first image, 29. so the soul falls home to the world of wrath. 30. The devil has an image according to the figure of wrath. 31. Hell is in everyone; each carries it within themselves, 32. although the hellish property is felt more in one place than in another. 33. Accordingly, every human being carries heaven and hell within themselves. 34. The devil dwells in the abyss, and for all eternity has no place or location, 35. and lies imprisoned in a dark dungeon. 36. All strange <gloss>extravagant or vain</gloss> clothing is a prefiguration of the dark world, 37. just as a proud person is a prefiguration of the hellish one. 38.

# VI. On the Six Points. Chapter 9.
<meta>catchword: stands</meta>

The life of darkness is contrary to all life of light: for the darkness gives a fierce and hostile essence, and the life of light gives a love-essence.

2. In the darkness, there is in the essence only a constant stinging and breaking, where every form of the essence is hostile to the other—a contrary being. Every form denies itself, and one says to another that it is evil and opposed to it, being a cause of its unrest and <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimmigkeit"; in Jakob Böhme’s writing, this refers to the harsh, bitter, and "biting" quality of the spiritual fire when it lacks the softening influence of God's love.</note>: each thinks within itself, "if only the other form were not there, you would have rest"; and yet each one is evil and false. From this it follows that everything born of the dark, wrathful property is lying, and always lies to the other forms, saying they are evil: and yet it is itself a cause of it; it makes them evil with its poisonous <term>infection</term> <note>original: "Inficirung"; the process by which one spiritual quality spreads its nature to another, like a contagion.</note>.

3. Thus they all are, and lying is their truth: when they speak lies, they speak of their own forms and properties; and so also are their creatures. Therefore Christ said: *The devil is a liar and a murderer from the beginning.* (John 8:44.) For every form desires to murder the other, and yet there is no murdering <note>Böhme suggests that because these are eternal spiritual principles, they cannot be "killed" or annihilated, only perpetually contested.</note>, but rather the greater the strife is, the greater becomes their murder-life.

4. Therefore it is called an eternal death and enmity, where a total contrariety arises: for there is nothing that could abolish the strife; there is nothing that could tame a single form; the more it is resisted, the greater the wrath would become, like a fire that is poked so that it only burns more fiercely.

5. Thus the wrathful realm may be extinguished by nothing except solely by God's light; through that, it becomes entirely gentle, lovely, and full of joy; and yet that also may not be: for if the dark realm were to be ignited with the light <note>Meaning, if the darkness were completely replaced by light rather than serving as its foundation.</note>, then the light would have no root for its nature and property: no fire could be born, and there would be no light, nor any <term>omnipotence</term> <note>original: "Allmacht"; Böhme argues that God's power requires the "friction" or "fire" of the dark principles to manifest as active light and power.</note>, but everything would be a nothingness.

6. Therefore the realm of wrath must exist, for it is a cause of the fire- and light-world, and it is all God's. But not all is recognized or named "God," because the dark world has another property; and the light-world is also a cause of the wrath and terror of the dark property: for the darkness is terrified before the light, and

<summary>This page describes the chaotic and self-conflicting nature of the "dark world," where spiritual forms exist in eternal strife and mutual accusation. Böhme argues that this "wrathful realm" is actually the necessary foundation or "root" that allows the light and power of God to manifest.</summary>

# Part 6. Chapter 9. Theosophical Points. 67

[The life of darkness] stands in eternal <term>terror</term> <note>original: "Schrack"; in Böhme’s system, this is the sudden shock or trembling "crack" that occurs when the dark, harsh qualities of nature encounter the light, creating a flash of life.</note>, because the Light-world dwells within it; it trembles eternally before the light and yet cannot grasp it; rather, it is only a cause of life and movement, and thus everything must serve God's glory.

7. The life of darkness has many kinds of forms; it is not of a single quality, just as we can recognize this in the creatures of this world, where one is more evil than the other, and exists in a different torment than the other; yet all of these still live in the sun's power and light, by which they are softened.

8. But should this light be extinguished, the deep would become fierce and prickly; then one would soon see the quality of the dark world, how all creatures would thus become poisonous and evil.

9. For all life consists in <term>poison</term> <note>original: "Gift"; Böhme uses this term not just for toxins, but for the raw, sharp, and biting energy of the first principles of nature that would be unbearable without the light.</note>, and the light alone resists the poison, and yet the light is also a cause that the poison lives and does not wither away.

10. Therefore, we are to recognize that the life of darkness is only a withered poison, like a dying torment; and yet there is no dying there, for the Light-world stands opposite the mirror of darkness, which is why the darkness stands eternally in terror.

11. The dark life is like a terror where the flash and shock always rise up, as if wanting to escape from life and break out beyond it; and from this arises <term>pride</term> <note>original: "Hoffart"; the spiritual arrogance exemplified by Lucifer, who sought to exalt himself above the divine center.</note>, so that the devil always wants to be above God; it is his quality, the figure of his life is such, and he cannot do otherwise. Just as a poison rages and stings, as if it wanted to tear itself out of the limb;

12. So also is the life of darkness within itself; the poisonous <term>essences</term> <note>original: "Essentien"; the fundamental spiritual properties or "tastes" of a being's nature.</note> create such a <term>mind</term> <note>original: "Gemüthe"; the deep seat of the soul’s inclinations and will.</note>, and from the mind proceeds such a will-spirit. There is such a quality within it, and it stands primarily in seven forms, according to the center of nature, with its principle. Just as the life of joy stands in seven forms according to the law of nature, so also does the life of sadness: what gives joy in the light gives sadness in the darkness.

13. And yet it is not to be thought that the life of darkness sinks into a misery where it forgets itself, as if it were mourning: It is no mourning, but rather, what is sadness for us on earth according to this quality, that is power and joy in the darkness, according to the quality of the darkness: For

# VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 9.

sadness is a thing that sinks in death. However, death and dying are the life of darkness, just as anxiety is the life of the poison; the greater the anxiety in the poison, the stronger the poison-life becomes, as can be perceived in external poison.

14. We cannot say of the Devil that he sits in sadness, as if he were wavering or timid; there is no wavering in him, but a constant will to further kindle the <term>poison-torment</term> <note>original: "Gift-Qual"; Böhme uses this to describe the sharp, agonizing, and burning energy that forms the base of existence when not tempered by the Light.</note>, so that his <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimm"; often translated as "fierceness" or "grimness," it refers to the raw, violent power of the first principle of nature.</note> becomes greater, for it is his strength, from which he draws his will to soar above the thrones and set them ablaze. He wants to be a powerful lord in the poison-torment, for it is the strong and great life; but the Light is his misery and trembling. It exposes his arrogance, before which he is terrified, for it is his true poison which plagues him. Because he has abandoned it, it now stands against him; of this he is greatly ashamed, that he is thus an unformed angel in a foreign image. He would be satisfied with the fierce torment, if only the Light were not so near to him; therefore the shame within him is so great that he grows desperate and kindles his poisonous torment ever more fiercely, so that his figure becomes more and more hideous, and the Divine Image can no longer be recognized in him. Therefore, he strives only to rage and rave against GOD, as if he were something foreign, or a foreign power, as if he had a foreign kingdom; yet he is poor, and the dark kingdom is not his, but he is only a prisoner within it. It is God’s <term>abyss</term> <note>original: "Abgrund"; the bottomless depth or foundation of all things.</note>, and he is only a creature within it; he wants to be lord there, and yet is only a <term>juggler</term> <note>original: "Gauckler"; a trickster or charlatan. Böhme implies the Devil's perceived power is a hollow, deceptive performance.</note> with the fierceness, though he must act as the property of that quality requires. And this is also a wonder before the severe power of eternity. It is just like a play or a game, wherein the severe power works through its outcomes, so that it may be distinguished what is Evil or Good, Joy or Sorrow; and so that the creatures in the Light-world have cause to humble themselves: although God created no devil, nor did He create Lucifer for the dark world; and this is the enmity within Lucifer: that he was once an angel, and that the Light is so near to him, and yet he is a rebel.

15. Otherwise, there is no woe in the creatures that were created in the dark world, for they are of the fierce property and know nothing of the Light. Fierceness is their strength and power, and enmity is their will and life.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "dark world" (the first principle of nature) and its relationship to our physical world. On the previous page, the author described the Devil's internal torment and his desire to fuel his own "wrath-fire." This page expands that vision into a general rule of power: that in the dark world, power is synonymous with hostility and malice, providing the "essence" or root for the evil behaviors we see in our own world.</meta>

->Part 6. Chapter 9. On Theosophical Points.<-

The more evil and hostile a creature is in the dark world, the greater its power. Just as the mighty tyrants of this world often display their power through malice, so that they must be feared—or as tame animals fear the wicked, fierce ones—so it is also a characteristic of the dark world.

16. If we wish to rightly consider the nature of the dark world, we need only look at the malice and pride of this world; it is a prototype <note>original: "Fürbilde"; Böhme suggests our world is a physical reflection or "shadow-image" of the spiritual principles behind it.</note>. For all malice, falsehood, pride, and greed have their roots in the dark world. This is the dark world’s property, whether it is recognized in humans or in animals.

17. For this world stands upon the foundation of the dark world; the dark world provides this world with its essence, will, and property. If the Good had not been created alongside it, there would be no other action or will in this world than that of the dark world. But the Divine Power and the light of the Sun prevent this—though it can still be seen among humans and animals in their biting, feuding, striking, and in that proud self-will where each wants to rule over the other, to strangle others, to devour, and to exalt themselves alone; and also to trample everything through cunning, fierceness, malice, and falsehood, making themselves the master.

18. Thus the dark world also has such a property. What all malicious people do in this world in their wickedness and falsehood, the devils also do in the dark world. And what the poisonous, evil worms and animals do in their malice, the other creatures in the dark world do as well. Although they are without such a physical body, they nevertheless have these properties in their spiritual body; and though they have a body, it is of a spiritual nature, such as the devils have.

19. The birth, being, essence, and rule of the dark world consist primarily in the first four <term>Forms of Nature</term> <note>Böhme's system describes seven "forms" or qualities of nature. The first four—contraction, friction, anguish, and fire—represent the harsh, painful "foundation" of existence before it is transformed by the Light.</note>, particularly in the <term>anguish-torment</term> <note>original: "Angst-Qual"; here, "Angst" refers to a cosmic, pressurized state of friction and "Qual" to a driving, agonizing energy.</note>, in a very strong and mighty dominion where everything in the essence is, as it were, audible. For <term>Gentleness</term> <note>original: "Sanftmuth"; the softening quality of the Light-world.</note> is the enemy of the <term>Power of Wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimmen-Macht"; the raw, violent energy of the dark principle.</note>, and they are always set against one another.

20. Otherwise, if it were all to be one, there would only be one kind of torment and only one kind of will; then the eternal wonders could not be revealed. But the mani-

### VI. On the Six Points. Chapter 9.

->VI. On Six Points. Ch. 9.<-

...this kind of <term>torment<note>original: "Qual." Böhme uses this word as a play on the Latin *qualitas* (quality) and the German *Quelle* (source/well) and *Qual* (torment). It refers to the internal tension or "drive" that powers all life and being.</note></term> makes the eternal wonders manifest: For eternity would not otherwise be revealed, nor come into being, except through the ignition—that is, in the severe, harsh contraction in which the dark world stands, and from which the fire-world originates, as well as the light-world. It is all but a single essence, yet it divides itself into three properties of the torments: No property is separated from the other, but each gives rise to the other, as can be seen in fire and light, as well as in the material from which the fire burns.

21. And it is not necessary for man to search deeper, for he is himself the essence of all essences; it is only necessary for him to search because he, in his creation, has turned away from his inherent order and has introduced and awakened another torment within himself. [He must learn] how he may return into his eternal order and torment and be born anew; and then, how he may again extinguish the fierce <term>wrath<note>original: "Grimm." In Böhme’s thought, this is the "fierceness" or "fury" of the first principle of nature when it is not tempered by the light of Love.</note></term> that is active within him, since everything is active in him and pulls at him, both evil and good. Thus, he should learn how to resist the wrath and walk in gentleness, in the torment of light and love.

22. Otherwise, man has no law, provided he does not ignite himself in the property of the dark world and walk according to that same property; otherwise, everything is free to him. Whatever he does in gentleness and love is free to him and is his own essence; it depends on no one's name or opinion.

23. Everything that has grown from one root belongs to one tree; it is one and the same fruit, unless it corrupts itself so that it transforms that same essence.

24. As long as a thing remains in the essence from which it arose, it has no law; but if it departs from that into another torment, then the first torment clings to it and lies in conflict with the other. Now the law follows, requiring that it return to that which it was in its <term>original state<note>original: "Urstande." The primal foundation or the beginning of a thing's existence in God's order.</note></term>, and be one, not two. For one thing should have only one governance, and not two. Man was created in the governance of love and gentleness, as in the essence of God; he should have remained therein.

25. But because he has awakened another governance in himself—namely, the wrath—he is now in conflict and has laws, so that he [must]...
