### Part 6. Chapter 9. On Theosophical Points.

->Part 6. Chapter 9. On Theosophical Points.<-

...[If the] <term>Wrath<note>original: "Grimm." In Böhme’s thought, this is the "fierceness" or "fury" of the first principle of nature—the raw, unrefined energy of life that becomes "hellish" if not tempered by the Light of Love.</note></term> should kill and abandon [the soul], and be in a <term>dominion<note>original: "Regiment." This refers to the governing force, "rule," or "regime" of a specific spiritual principle over the individual.</note></term> once again; if both dominions have become powerful within him, and the dominion of Wrath has overcome Love, then he must be completely broken in his essence and be born anew from the first <term>root<note>original: "Wurzel." In Böhme’s botanical-spiritual metaphor, the root is the origin of the soul's being in the divine nature.</note></term>. Therefore, in this dual essence, he has laws for how he should behave and how he must birth a Spirit of Will for the eternal dominion.

26. All this now stands in his power: he may birth the spirit of Wrath or the spirit of Love, and according to that choice he is separated—deciding where and to which world he belongs. For he separates himself.

27. But the law remains over them as long as he stands in this life (this <term>field<note>original: "Acker." A biblical metaphor, likely referring to the Parable of the Tares, representing the human body and earthly existence where good and evil grow together.</note></term>); then, when the weeds separate from this field of the body, it is again in a single dominion where it shall remain eternally. For after that, there is nothing more to give it law, because it is entirely unified in its will, whether to do evil or good.

28. But in this outer life, man stands in conflict: two dominions rest within him, as do two kinds of torment and law. (1) The Divine, leading to love and righteousness. (2) The wrathful, in the rising of pride in the power of fire, in the severe, harsh, hellish spirit, envy, anger, and malice. Whichever the spirit adopts for itself, to that dominion it belongs. The other side clings to him and rebukes him to his face as a perjurer and an apostate, yet it still pulls him and wants to have him. Thus life sits between both in the <term>press<note>original: "Presse." A metaphor for the intense pressure or "squeeze" of spiritual trial, like grapes being crushed in a winepress to produce juice.</note></term> and is at odds with itself.

29. But if it ventures forth and gives itself entirely over to the Wrath, then the Wrath destroys the first <term>image<note>original: "Bildniß." The original divine "likeness" or blueprint of man intended by God.</note></term> after **GOD**. If the Divine power prevents this from happening completely, the Wrath seeks to topple the whole man, and many a person is plunged into doubt and such anguish that they bring death upon themselves.

30. Thus the soul falls home to the image of the wrathful, dark world, and the image is transformed into a hellish figure, into a form reflecting the properties it held here [in life]. For this is also what happened to the devils, who lost their first image.

31. Every devil now has an image according to his property, according to the figure of the Wrath, according to his torment; such as...

### VI. On Six Points. Chapter 9.

->VI. On Six Points. Chapter 9.<-

...[they] are terrible worms or evil beasts, and the lost soul has the same to expect.

32. External reason thinks that Hell is far away from us; but it is near us, everyone carries it within themselves; unless they kill the hellish poison with God’s power and sprout forth from it as a new branch, which the hellish torment may not seize or disturb.

33. And although it is indeed true that Hell’s <term>wrath<note>original: "Grimm." In Böhme’s theology, this is the "fierceness" or raw, consuming energy of the first principle of nature—the quality of fire without light.</note></term> is more recognizable in one place than in another, all according to the hellish <term>dominion<note>original: "Regiment." This refers to the governing force or spiritual regime that rules over a particular state of being.</note></term>, where the superior dominion is powerful in various places in the <term>location<note>original: "Loco."</note></term> of this world: all according to the first ignition of King Lucifer. For in many places on the earth, as well as in the depths between the stars and the earth, the hellish quality is felt more than in other places, where the inner wrath reaches into the outer <term>principle<note>original: "Principium." One of the three foundational realms or "births" of existence in Böhme’s system: the dark world, the light world, and the material world.</note></term>. There, different dominions of devils exist, as well as other hellish qualities; there, the wrath of God ignited itself so violently and now burns until the Judgment of God.

34. Every person in this world carries Heaven and Hell within themselves; whichever quality they awaken, that same quality burns within them, and the soul is capable of that fire. And when the body dies, the soul does not need to travel anywhere, but instead is cast back into the hellish dominion to which it belongs. The same devils who are of its quality wait for it and take it into their dominion until the Judgment of God. And although they are not bound to any one place, they still belong to that same dominion and have the same torment everywhere; for wherever they may go, they are in the same dominion and torment: for the <term>Abyss<note>original: "Abgrund." The "unground" or bottomless void; the spiritual state outside of God's light and structured time/space.</note></term> has no place, neither time nor space. Just as it was before the times of the world, when there was no place; so it is and remains eternally in the Abyss.

35. And although the <term>place<note>original: "Locus."</note></term> of this world was given to Lucifer as a kingdom, for he was created within it; he has now been cast out of place and position and dwells in the Abyss, where he can never reach any place in the Angelic Realms, and yet is enclosed within his kingdom in the Abyss, where he must then bear eternal mockery as a prisoner; just as one does to a criminal, whom [a person...]

<summary>This page explains that Hell is not a distant location but an internal state of "wrath" that exists within the soul, emphasizing that humans carry both Heaven and Hell within them. It describes the "Abyss" as a timeless, spaceless dominion where the soul remains after death if it has aligned itself with the dark principle.</summary>

P. 6. Cap. 9. On Theosophical Points.

...[one] is led into a dark hole away from all beings of this world, where he must do without all the world's joy and pleasure and bear the mockery of his crime.

36. So it also goes for the devils and all damned souls; they lie imprisoned in the dark dungeon. They do not even desire to come out, because of the great mockery of their hideous shape and image. And even if they were to go anywhere, they would eternally enjoy no good thing; there is no <term>refreshment<note>original: "Erquickung." Often used by Böhme to describe the soothing, cooling effect of the Light upon the fiery hunger of the soul.</note></term> with them, but they lie in Hell like the dead, or like those eternally starved, famished, and thirsty. And they are only an evil <term>poisonous torment<note>original: "Gift-Qual." In Böhme's specialized vocabulary, "Qual" refers both to "torment" and to the "quality" or "source-spring" of a thing's being. Here, it is the agonizing nature of the dark principle.</note></term>; everything is contrary to them. They have only a thirst for anguish and malice, which they devour eternally within themselves, and give birth to blasphemy against God over themselves. The more hideous they can make their appearance, the more they like it; just like foolish people on earth who always want to be the greatest fools, making themselves look ghastly and taking joy in it. Thus they also do eternally in Hell; therefore they begin the game here on earth. Just as the tyrant takes joy in tormenting people and flaunting their sweat in foolish, strange clothing and gestures, and acting foolishly; so too do the devils in Hell. And this world's opulence, in strange dress, is a true prototype of the hellish world.

37. All strange shaggy tufts and rags which the proud person invents and clothes his foolish self with, so that he wants to be distinguished from the true children of God—these are prototypes of the hellish world. For all his decorating, glinting, and parading, by which he withdraws from humility, is all a hellish mirror. For the devil’s <term>pride<note>original: "Hoffart." A strong term for arrogance or "lofty-behavior" that separates one from the communal body of God.</note></term> wants to be like no one else; it distinguishes itself in this world. And the blind person does not understand how the devil mocks and deceives him, and only models his proud <term>mask<note>original: "Larva." From the Latin for a ghost, specter, or mask; Böhme uses it to describe the false, distorted appearance assumed by those who turn away from God.</note></term> in order to mock God—so that the poor person acts as he does, yet imagines he is beautiful and better than other people, though we all <term>originate<note>original: "urständen." A Böhme coinage meaning to have one's primordial source or foundation.</note></term> and come from one body and spirit. But before God and His angels, he is recognized only as a devilish mask and is an abomination before Heaven. Just as a fool is only an abomination to wisdom; so too is...

## Chapter 10.
### Concerning the four elements of the devil and the dark world, and how one should recognize them in this outer world.

->Summary.<-

The 4 elements of the devil. §. 1. Upon these the outer world stands, 2. and one gives birth to the other. 3. In today's world and religions, the son of the 4 elements reigns, who is called Falsehood, 4. and has 2 kingdoms on earth. 5. Everyone wants to put on this coat, 6. in which the devil mocks God when man does his [the devil's] will. 7. Therefore, the outer world is a murder-pit, 8. and pious people are the mockery of the godless. 9. Are there such evil beasts among humans? 10. Their first virtue is pride <note>original: "Hoffart." In Böhme’s theology, pride is the primary root of the fall, seeking to be "above" others and God.</note>, 11. which devours the sweat of the miserable; 12. the second virtue is greed <note>original: "Geitz."</note>, 13. which plagues itself. 14. Pride always plays the harlot with greed. 15. The third virtue is envy <note>original: "Neid."</note>, 16. which is the devil’s spirit-of-will; 17. the fourth virtue is wrath <note>original: "Zorn."</note>. 18. These are the 4 elements with which the devil reigns on earth and always creates conflict for a Christian. 19. One element always ignites the other. 20. Therefore, let everyone take heed of what qualities reign within them and fight against them; 21. for these elements must not be the master, but rather God’s 4 elements <note>This likely refers to the spiritual equivalents: humility, generosity, love, and patience.</note>. 22. Thus, man must fight against himself and be sober and temperate, 23. for he has many enemies. 24. Conflict is necessary for him—not with mouth and sword, but with spirit and mind <note>original: "Gemüthe." This refers to the inner seat of the soul's disposition, desires, and thoughts.</note>. 25. May he only remain faithful and think that God wishes to rescue him thus from the devil and lead him into His kingdom. Amen. 26.

The first element of the dark world and of the devil is pride, the second is greed, the third is envy, the fourth is wrath. These four elements are always breeding and

### Part 6. Chapter 10. Theosophical Points. 75

and eternally brings forth a young son, who is named Falsehood. This same son is also a true son of the corrupted Adam, whom he has left behind as a lord of the world; he has become king in the world and has possessed the entire world; he reigns in every corner in the third *Principle* <note>original: "Principio." In Böhme’s cosmology, the Third Principle is the visible, material world we inhabit, which is a mixture of light and dark spiritual forces.</note>. Whoever truly recognizes this king, recognizes the four *Elements* of the devil; for in the dark world these four *Elements* hold the entire government, in spirit and body, and in everything that is called "being."

2. And we see clearly by this, that this outer world stands upon the foundation of these same four *Elements*, and receives from them its inclination, as well as its torment and will; for the son of these same four *Elements* reigns on Earth. He wishes to have everything under his obedience, and has four kinds of generations of subjects: (1) The generation of Pride <note>original: "Hoffart." This is the first "element," the desire to be superior and unequal to others.</note>, which wants to be above everything else and will not be equal. (2) The generation of Greed <note>original: "Geitz."</note>, which wants to possess everything alone, to subdue it, and wants to have it all. This second generation is the son of the first, for Pride also wants to have everything, so that it alone may be everything. (3) The third generation is Envy, which is the son of Greed: when he sees that he cannot have everything alone, he stings like a poison and begrudges no one anything; his will is in all things, either to draw them to himself and possess them alone, or else to rage within them with an evil will. (4) The fourth generation is Wrath, which is the son of Envy; whatever he cannot achieve with an evil will, he sets ablaze in the fire of wrath, and breaks it with violence, inciting war and murder, and wanting to shatter everything; this generation wants to subdue everything by force.

3. These, then, are the four *Elements* of the devil, all four of which are within one another as one; one always proceeds from the other, and each begets the other; they originate from the dark nature, such as from Harshness, Bitterness, Anguish, and Fire <note>These four qualities—Herbe, Bitter, Angst, and Feuer—represent the "first four properties" of eternal nature in Böhme's system, which, without the light of God, constitute the "dark-world" or hell.</note>.

4. ### VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 10.

->God’s radiance on the tongue, and the devil’s power and poison in the heart.<-

5. This one is king on earth, and governs two kingdoms: the first is called Corruption <note>original: "Verderbniß." In Böhme's thought, this refers to the state of spiritual decay following the Fall.</note>; and the other is Babel, which is a confusion <note>A reference to the Tower of Babel; for Böhme, "Babel" represents the confused, superficial state of institutional religion and worldly systems.</note>. To the kingdom of corruption, this king has added strength and power, which is the clothing of that same kingdom. To the other kingdom, Babel, he has put on a white, glittering garment; this must serve as God for him, with which the king on earth rules, as if he were God. And the peoples worship this same garment: and under the garment is the man of falsehood and deceit, and he has his mother—the four elements—within him, namely **Pride**, **Greed**, **Envy**, and **Wrath**.

6. Thus the four elements of the devil rule under a glistening robe, and people scramble for this same robe; everyone wants to put it on: but whoever puts it on, puts on hell and God’s wrath. Such a robe is honored in God’s stead, and yet it is the robe that the wrath of God put on Adam and Eve <note>original: "Adā und Heva." This refers to the "coats of skins" from Genesis 3:21, symbolizing the mortal, animal-like nature humans received after the Fall.</note> when the devil deceived them so that they fell from God’s obedience. And it is exactly that little robe <note>original: "Röcklein."</note> against which God has warned us since the world began; we should not put it on, for the devil is at home within it: when we put it on, we move into the devil’s lodging, and must do what he wants, for he is the host in that same house and rests in that same little robe.

7. Because he is a prisoner of God, he puts his little robe on us and sends us with it toward Babel into his service; there we must mock God. For we have God’s little robe on <note>This refers to the outward appearance of holiness or the "Tongue of God" mentioned at the start of the page.</note>, and have the devil as a guest and lodging underneath: thus the tongue gives God good words, but the heart has the spirit of the four elements of fury; and thus God is mocked by the devil, so that God should see that he, the devil, is lord and king over mankind. He regards God’s lordship in man as nothing but a glittering robe, wherein he, the devil, is the master, and has captured man in his arms. He indeed covers man with the little robe and lets man be called God's child, but man only does the devil's will in this robe; everything that the devil cannot or may not do in the outer kingdom, man does for him in his service. ### Part 6. Chapter 10. Of Theosophic Points.

...gladly to please: also, the devil cannot use God's creatures <note>In Böhme’s theology, the devil has no creative power of his own and must rely on the misuse of God's creation by humans.</note>, but man misuses them to please him, and to mock God thereby; with them, he practices pride and greed, as well as falsehood and malice, and accomplishes everything through them that the devil desires; he also shines with them as if he were God.

8. Therefore, the external realm has become a constant murder-pit of the devil; and the false, supposed human (who calls himself a human, but is not), carries out the murder and increases God’s wrath, setting the dark world on fire within this external one, so that God’s wrath burns perpetually in this world.

9. Thus, God’s kingdom is hindered, and the devil’s will is done, and the devil remains a prince on earth; whereas otherwise he would have no success on earth, the supposed human is in his service and carries out his will. Thus, two generations of humans live together on earth: one is the true humans, who serve God in the garment of humility and misery; the devil mocks them and plagues them through the other generation, performing all his wonders <note>In this context, "wonders" (Wunder) refers to the miraculous or spectacular displays of worldly power and deception.</note> upon them through those who serve him.

10. And the other generation also calls itself human and walks in human form, but they are evil beasts; they put on their king’s clothing, which is called Falsehood, and live in the power of their king’s four elements: namely **Pride**, **Greed**, **Envy**, and **Wrath**.

Böhme identifies this as the first "virtue" or characteristic of the fallen nature.</note> is the first virtue; it tears the bread out of the true human’s mouth and forces the wretched to satisfy its needs. It does not want anything to be its equal; it alone wants to be the most beautiful child in the house. It has put on the glittering little robe <note>original: "Röcklein."</note> and wants to be called pious; it demands honor and that people bow before it. Nothing should equal it; it wants to be master and says: "I am modest in my gestures."

12. ### VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 10. Concerning Theosophical Points.

9. This refers to two spiritual lineages or "kinds" of people rather than biological races.</note> of men live alongside each other on earth: the first are true humans, who serve God in the garment of humility and misery <note>original: "Elendes." In Böhme's vocabulary, this often implies being an exile or an outsider to the worldly system.</note>; the devil mocks them and plagues them using the other race, and performs all his [dark] wonders upon them through those who serve him.

10. And the other race also calls itself human, and even walks in human form, but they are evil beasts; they put on their king's garment, which is called **Falsehood**, and live in the power of their king's four elements: namely **Pride**, **Greed**, **Envy**, and **Wrath**.

11. **Pride** is the first virtue <note>Böhme uses "virtue" ironically here to describe the primary characteristic of this corrupted nature.</note>; it snatches the bread from the mouth of the true man and forces the poor to satisfy its desires. It does not want anything to be its equal; it wants to be the only "beautiful child" in the house, and it has put on the glittering little robe <note>original: "gleiſſende Röcklein." This refers back to the deceptive, shiny exterior of worldly systems mentioned on the previous page.</note>; it wants to be called pious, it demands to be honored and to have people bow before it; also, nothing should be its equal; it wants to be lord and says: "I am modest in my gestures."

12. But its heart is **Greed**, which is the wolf, and it devours the sweat and labor of the poor; it climbs above everything; it delves daily into the wonders of God [to see] how it might glisten: it presents itself as friendly and modest, as if it were a virgin full of discipline, and yet it is a fragile whore <note>original: "brüchige Hure." Böhme uses this harsh, traditional imagery to describe a soul that is spiritually unfaithful to God and "broken" or unreliable in its virtue.</note>, and in its heart, it hates all virtue, discipline, and justice; it is

<summary>Böhme describes the division of humanity into two spiritual "races": those who serve God in humility and those who serve the devil through Pride and Greed. He characterizes the worldly person as a "false man" who wears a glittering exterior to mask a predatory, "wolf-like" heart.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the "Four Elements" of the King of Falsehood, focusing on the completion of the description of Pride (Point 11) and moving into the descriptions of Greed (Point 13) and its consequences (Point 14).</meta>

### Part 6. Chapter 10. Of Six Theosophic Points.

...a constant enemy of love and humility; whatever is simple, she <note>Böhme personifies Pride (die Hoffart) as a female figure.</note> despises, and yet she forces the simple under her yoke. She says to the true human: "You are my dog, I hunt you wherever I wish; you are foolish, and I am wise"; and she herself is the greatest fool; she gambles away God and the kingdom of heaven for a brief moment's lust of the eyes; she throws herself into the darkness and puts on the robe of anguish.

13. The second virtue of this King of Falsehood is **Greed**, which pulls everything to itself and darkens the glittering ornament of Pride; it pulls both evil and good together into itself and constantly keeps Pride filled to the brim: and when it has filled her, it takes its son, **Envy**, and torments Pride with him, so that she has no rest in her splendor. Envy always stings at the craving of Greed as if it were mad and insane, and tortures Pride day and night so that she never rests. Greed is truly a coarse, swinish beast; it craves more than it can or may devour: its gullet stands wide open day and night; it does not let a person rest and constantly plagues them in their own filth, so that the person lusts after the earth and those things which the earth provides; yet no greed is needed for this—only labor is required, and no greed at all.

14. Greed plagues itself and is its own enemy, for it fills itself with woe and unrest, and obscures a person’s understanding so that they cannot recognize that everything comes from God’s hand. It darkens the light of a person’s life, consumes the body, and robs them of divine senses and glory; it casts them into the pit of death and brings them temporal and eternal death. It pulls dark substances into the noble image of man and turns an angel into a fierce devil: it brings the <term>Turba</term> <note>original: "Turbam", the Latin term for "turmoil" or "disturbance." In Böhme's theology, the Turba is the "awakened wrath" or the destructive power that shatters any form that has turned away from the divine light.</note> over body and soul, and is the hideous beast in the abyss of hell, for it is the cause of torment and pain; without it, no torment could arise. It creates war and strife, for it is never satisfied; even if it had the whole world, it would still want the abyss as well, for no place was born for its rest. It builds lands and kingdoms and breaks them apart again, driving man into vain toil and unrest; it is simply the heart and will of the devil.

<meta>catchword: 15. For</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes the vices of Pride and Greed, personifying them as destructive forces that enslave the human spirit and lead to perpetual unrest. Greed is specifically identified as the source of the "Turba," the chaotic wrath that destroys divine harmony in both the soul and the world.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Greed, Envy, Turba, Jacob Böhme, spiritual death, King of Falsehood, lust of the eyes, unrest, divine order</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Four Elements of the Devil" within the Kingdom of Falsehood. Having previously defined Pride and Greed, Böhme now explores their incestuous relationship and introduces the third "virtue": Envy. The previous page ended with Greed casting the soul into the pit of death; this page picks up with the birth of Pride from that Greed.</meta>

### Part 6. Chapter 10. Of Six Theosophic Points.

15. For **Pride** <note>original: *Hoffart*. Böhme treats Pride as an external, illusory "beauty" that masks a corrupt internal state.</note> is the beautiful spirit that grows out of **Greed**; she is the beautiful child that was meant to possess Heaven, but Greed has turned her into a child of whoredom and led her into **Babel**, into the "Mother of the Great Whoredom on Earth" <note>A reference to the "Whore of Babylon" from the Book of Revelation, symbolizing spiritual corruption and the false church.</note>. There, Pride constantly commits harlotry with Greed, and is nothing but a child of whoredom before GOD. She cannot possess Heaven; she has her kingdom of heaven on earth and woos the King of Falsehood. He accepts all her labor and hands it over to the four elements of the devil in the dark world. There, Pride must follow after Greed when the **anguish-greed-sack** <note>original: *Angst-Geisack*. A vivid, earthy metaphor for the mortal body or the self-centered heart that hoards desires and fears.</note> breaks apart; for the devil is so "just" that he takes his Greed with him into the abyss so that Pride may have her joy therein—like a fool in his fool’s clothing, who toils and frets only to give birth to folly and to please his onlookers by being a senseless fool. In this same way, Pride and Greed are God’s fools and the devil’s **jugglers** <note>original: *Gauckler*. A traveling performer or mountebank; Böhme suggests they are mere entertainers in a tragic, demonic comedy.</note>, in whom the devil takes delight, because he can turn the image of GOD into the image of a fool.

16. The third virtue <note>Böhme uses "virtue" ironically here to mean a characteristic power or property.</note> is **Envy**, located in the four elements of the devil within the Kingdom of Falsehood. He is a sting, a rager, and a blusterer, like a wicked poison. He can stay nowhere and has no place of rest; his mother, Greed, allows him no peace, so he must always rage and storm. He must enter into things in which he was not born. He is the mouth of Greed, a constant liar and slanderer; he stings his neighbor’s heart and wounds it. He devours himself with a poisonous hunger and yet is never satisfied. He creates unrest without goal or measure; he is the greatest poison and the **Eye of Hell** through which the devil looks into the soul and body of man. Nothing is equal to him; he is not a fire himself, but he is the **sting of the fire**. He causes all evil and yet finds no rest; the more he drives himself, the more insane he becomes. He is a parched poison; he requires no substance, yet he rages within substance. He makes a person more than insane, so that they desire to rage and storm against GOD. He is the **essence** <note>original: *Essenz*. The fundamental nature or "being" of a thing.</note> of hell and of wrath; he turns love into the greatest enmity. He begrudges everyone everything, and yet he himself is a famished Nothing.

17. This is the devil’s **Will-Spirit** <note>original: *Willen-Geist*. The driving force of the will when it is directed toward the self and away from God.</note>: whichever person...

<summary>Böhme describes Pride as a hollow "child of whoredom" born from Greed, which eventually leads to the birth of Envy—the restless, poisonous "Eye of Hell" that destroys human relationships and peace.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Greed, Babel, Envy, Four Elements of the Devil, Falsehood, Spiritual Anatomy, Wrath</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Böhme’s exploration of the "four elements" of the devil's kingdom—the spiritual vices that corrupt the human soul. Having discussed Greed and Pride on the previous page, he now addresses Envy and introduces the fourth "virtue": Wrath.</meta>

### VI. Of Six Points. Chapter 10.

...takes him in as a guest, takes the Devil along with God’s wrath; for he carries within him the hellish torment and woe; he is the eternal, hostile plague and unrest, and destroys the noble image of God <note>original: *das edle Bildniß*. In Böhme's theology, this is the "Imago Dei," the divine spark or blueprint within humanity that the Fall corrupted.</note>, for he is the enemy of God and all creatures.

18. The fourth virtue, in the four elements in the kingdom of the devil’s falsehood, is **Wrath** <note>original: *Zorn*</note>, or malice; this is the true hellish fire, for Wrath is born between Greed and Envy; it is the fire and life of Envy. What Envy cannot accomplish, Wrath carries out. Wrath seizes body and soul together and runs like a raging devil, wanting to murder and break everything; it runs against walls and castles: and even if it bursts itself apart, it remains as frantic as a mad dog that bites and murders everything; and it is so poisonous in its rage that what it cannot overpower, it poisons nonetheless. This is the true **gout** <note>original: *Podagra*. A painful inflammatory arthritis; Böhme uses it as a metaphor for a spiritual sickness that inflames, swells, and cripples the soul.</note> of the world: when **Pride** <note>original: *Hoffart*</note> in her glittering little coat cannot gain power through cunning and falsehood, then the fourth virtue must step in; it strikes with its fists and starts wars. Oh, how joyful the devil is when his four elements rule in this way; then he still imagines he is master on Earth. Though he is a prisoner, the **beast-men** <note>original: *Thier-Menschen*. Humans who have lost their divine likeness and live only by animalistic instincts and passions.</note> carry out his office well enough for him, and he merely mocks mankind—seeing that they are worse and do more than he himself is able to do.

19. These, then, are the four elements of the dark world in which the devil imagines himself to be a god; with them he rules on Earth, along with his faithful son, **Falsehood**. This Falsehood is at first like a **dressed-up kitten** <note>original: *geschmückte Käzlein*. A predatory animal hiding behind a cute or harmless appearance; a metaphor for deceptive charm.</note> that gives good words to your face while always aiming for the mouse; if she can only snatch it, oh how happy she is to bring the roast to the devil. Man is surrounded by these four elements and is a guest in the land of the false king; every hour they shoot at his heart, wanting to murder his noble image. He must always be at war against these, for they are lodged with and within him; they always prick at him and want to murder his best **jewel** <note>original: *Kleinod*. Often used by Böhme to refer to the soul's purity or the "pearl" of divine wisdom.</note>.

20. When only one among these four elements gains the power to **characterize** <note>original: *qualificiren*. A central term in Böhme’s writing, meaning to imbue with a specific quality, to operate within, or to dominate a person's spiritual disposition.</note> a person, that one ignites...

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>Böhme describes the destructive nature of Envy and Wrath, characterizing them as the final "elements" of a dark spiritual world that wars against the soul's divine image. He warns that humans often act out the devil's malice more effectively than the devil himself through these animalistic passions.</summary>
<keywords>Wrath, Envy, Four Elements of the Dark World, Noble Image, Beast-men, Falsehood, Spiritual Warfare, Gout, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "four elements" of the devil—vices that have taken root in the human soul—and contrasts them with the four divine elements that a true Christian must cultivate through action rather than mere words.</meta>

P. 6. Chap. 10. Theosophical Points.

...attaches itself to all the others and instantly robs man of his noble image <note>original: *Bildniß*. In Böhme's thought, this is the "Imago Dei," the divine spark or blueprint within humanity that the Fall corrupted.</note>, making a mask of the devil out of him. And no man who allows these four elements the power to qualify <note>original: *qualificiren*. A technical term in Böhme’s writing meaning to operate, characterize, or determine one's inner spiritual nature.</note> within him can truly say he is a human; for he qualifies in the devil’s property and is an enemy of God. And even if the devil clothes him in a **glittering robe** <note>original: *gleissenden Rock*. A frequent metaphor in Böhme's work for hypocrisy or a false outward appearance of piety.</note> so that he can speak fair words and knows how to behave finely so that one thinks he is a child of God, he is nevertheless no human as long as these four elements hold the upper-regime <note>original: *Ober-Regiment*. The governing authority over the soul's inclinations.</note> in him; rather, he is a devilish man, half devil and half human, until he fills his measure; then he is a total devil in human form.

21. Therefore let everyone learn to know themselves and what properties rule within them: If he finds that all these four elements, or even just one, rule in him, then he has time to go to war against them, or things will not go well; he will not dare to comfort himself with the kingdom of heaven. Let him not allow the devil to wrap him in that glittering cloak, as is happening now, where people live in these four elements and only **tickle** <note>original: *kützelt*. To stroke or soothe oneself; Böhme uses this to mock those who use the doctrine of Christ's sacrifice as a "cover" to avoid actual moral transformation.</note> themselves finely with the suffering of Christ—that must be the rogue’s cover. The rogue would like to keep his regime, if only he did not tickle himself with Christ’s satisfaction <note>original: *Genugthuung*. The theological concept of Christ's sacrifice paying the debt for human sin.</note>.

22. Oh, how the glittering robe of Christ shall be stripped from you! Then one shall see the **Whore of Babel** <note>original: *Hure Babel*. A reference to the corrupt church or worldly systems in the Book of Revelation.</note> standing there with her four "virtues." It is not enough merely to take comfort; one must resist the rogue <note>original: *Schalcke*. The deceptive, lower self or the corrupt nature of man.</note> so that he does not become master in the house. He must not have the regime; instead, Justice, Love, Humility, and Chastity must rule, always eager to do good; not in pride, greed, envy, or wrath, but in humility, in doing good with a kind heart. Not to play the hypocrite and give fair words, but in the doing: **it must be doing**; one must resist the devil’s will, be satisfied with little, enclose oneself in patience and hope in God, resist the four evil elements, and take in God’s four elements, which are **Love, Gentleness, Mercy, and Patience in Hope**: these are God’s four elements; man should awaken these within himself and constantly struggle with them against the devil’s four elements.

F
<meta>catchword: 23. The</meta>

<summary>Böhme warns that internalizing the devil's four vices (Pride, Greed, Envy, and Wrath) destroys the divine image in man, turning him into a "half-devil" despite outward displays of piety. He calls for a spiritual war to replace these vices with the four divine elements: Love, Gentleness, Mercy, and Patience.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the tenth chapter of the "Six Theosophical Points." It follows the discussion of how the devil’s vices take root as "elements" within the human soul, arguing that a true Christian must actively fight against these internal qualities to maintain their divine nature.</meta>

# VI. On Six Theosophical Points.

23. Man must be in a struggle against himself here, if he wishes to become a heavenly citizen; he must not be a lazy sleeper in gluttony and drunkenness, merely filling his belly so that the devil's elements begin to take effect <note>original: *qualificiren*. This is a central term in Böhme’s philosophy, meaning to manifest specific qualities, interact, or determine one's spiritual nature.</note>; rather, he must be moderate, sober, and vigilant, like a soldier standing before his enemy: for God's wrath fights always against him, and he will have quite enough to do simply to defend himself.

24. For the devil is his enemy, his own corrupted flesh and blood is his enemy; God’s wrath is his enemy within him, and the entire world is his enemy: wherever he looks, he sees enemies who all wish to rob him.

25. Therefore it is called a struggle—not with the mouth and sword, but with the spirit and the mind <note>original: *Gemüthe*. In the context of 17th-century German mysticism, this refers to the deepest seat of the soul’s desires, will, and emotional character.</note>; and he must not give up. Even if body and soul should break, God must remain the heart’s comfort, as King David says: *Even if my body and soul break, yet you are my God, and the comfort and confidence of my heart* <note>original: *Ps. 73: 26*. This is a paraphrase of Psalm 73:26: "My flesh and my heart may fail, but God is the strength of my heart and my portion forever."</note>: and even if a person saw that the entire world was godless, if he intends to become a child of God, he must remain steadfast.

26. And even if it seems to him that he is alone on such a path, and the whole world says: "You are a fool, and are insane"; he should nonetheless be as if he were dead to the world, and hear those things as if they were being said by the devil, who is his worst enemy; and he should not deviate in any direction, but think that in his purpose he pleases God, and that God Himself is the purpose within him: That He thus wishes to rescue him from the devil, and lead him into His kingdom. Amen.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the general Table of Contents to the beginning of the actual treatise. It contains the Preface and the introduction to the First Point regarding the spiritual nature of the soul’s components.</meta>

# SIX MYSTIC POINTS,
<note>original: *Sex Puncta Mystica*</note>

->Or<-

## A brief explanation of the following Six Mystic Points.

### Preface:

Precious knowledge is not attained, unless the soul has triumphed over the storm and struck down the Devil, so that it receives the Knight’s wreath <note>original: *Ritter-Kränzlein*. In Böhme's imagery, this represents the spiritual crown of victory awarded to the soul that overcomes earthly and demonic temptations.</note>, which the lovely Virgin of Discipline <note>The "Virgin of Discipline" or "Virgin of Chaste Purity" often refers to Divine Wisdom (Sophia), who guides the seeker.</note> places upon it as a sign of victory; that it has triumphed in its precious Knight, Christ. Then the wonderful knowledge arises, though not in full perfection.

### I.

## Concerning the Blood and Water of the Soul.

->Summaries.<-

1. The soul's blood and water is magical. 2. For the soul is a magical fire. 3. The origin of the blood. 4. The Tincture <term>Tincture (*Tinctur*): In alchemy and Böhme's thought, a spiritual essence or "inner power" that transforms and brings life to matter.</term> transforms the water into blood. 5. 6. And Magic makes the Divine Blood substantial. 7. The soul's flesh and blood is Divine Essence. 8. Every magical fire has its clarity and darkness within itself, for which reason a final Day of Separation is established. 9.

1. Everything that is substantial and tangible belongs to this world. Since the soul is not a substance or being of this world, its blood and water are also not a substance or being of this world.

2. It is true that the soul, with its blood and water, is within the external blood and water, but its substance is magical. For the soul is also a magical fire, and its image or form is born out of the magical fire within the light (in the power of its fire and light), and yet it is a true image, in flesh and blood, but in the original state <note>original: *Urstand*. This refers to the primal, uncorrupted foundation of a thing before it enters physical manifestation.</note> of the same.

<summary>This page introduces the "Six Mystic Points" with a preface on spiritual victory and begins the first point, defining the soul as a "magical fire" whose true essence is spiritual rather than physical.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, soul, magical fire, tincture, spiritual victory, Sophia, blood and water, divine essence</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the first of the Six Mystic Points, focusing on the magical nature of the soul's composition and the relationship between the inner spiritual substance and the outer physical form.</meta>

# VII. Concerning the first of the Six Points.

3. Just as God's Wisdom possesses an essence, and yet she—Wisdom—is not a "being" or substance herself; so too the soul possesses an essence along with its image, yet the soul itself is merely a magical fire, while its nourishment is drawn from its own essence.

4. Just as a fire must have an essence <term>Essence (*Wesen*): In Böhme's writing, this refers to the substantial "fuel" or reality that allows a spiritual force to manifest.</term> if it is to burn, so too the magical fire of the soul possesses flesh, blood, and water. For there would be no blood if the Tincture <term>Tincture (*Tinctur*): A spiritual, alchemical essence that transmits life and quality from the spiritual realm into the material.</term> from the fire and light were not present in the water. This Tincture is the *Ens* <term>Ens: A Latin term for "being" or "fundamental entity"; the essential "stuff" of a thing.</term> or life of Wisdom; it contains all the forms of nature within itself and is the second magical fire.

5. For this Tincture provides all colors, and from its form the Divine Power flows out into the gentle essence of the light—understand this as being according to the property of light. Yet, according to the property of fire, it is a "sharpness" or intensity of transformation: it can lead all things to their highest degree, even though it is not a living spirit in itself, but rather the highest *Ens*.

6. Thus, it is also such an *Ens* within the water, and it carries the properties of fire and light into it, along with all the powers of nature, whereby it then transforms the water into blood. It performs this in both the outer and inner water, as well as in the outer and inner blood.

7. The inner blood of the Divine Essence is also magical, for Magic makes it into a substance. It is spiritual blood, which the external being cannot touch <note>original: *rügen*. Here meaning to affect, reach, or interfere with.</note>, except through the Imagination. The inner Imagination leads the external will into the inner blood; by this, the blood and flesh of the Divine Essence are corrupted, and the noble image of the likeness of God is darkened.

8. The flesh and blood of the soul exist within the highest Mystery <term>Mystery (*Mysterium*): Not simply a secret, but a foundational, hidden reality or "womb" from which things are generated.</term>, for it is Divine Essence. When the external flesh and blood die, they return to the external Mystery, and the external Mystery returns to the internal.

9. And every magical fire carries its own clarity and darkness within itself. Because of this, a final Day of Separation <note>The "Day of Separation" refers to the Last Judgment or the final cosmic sorting of spiritual qualities.</note> has been established, when everything shall pass through a fire and be tested to see what is capable of enduring it and what is not. Then every thing enters into its own Magic, and remains thereafter as it has been from eternity.

<sig>II.</sig>

<vocab>Divine Wisdom, Soul, Image, Magical Fire, Tincture, Ens, Imagination, Mystery, Day of Separation, Magic</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the spiritual alchemy of the soul, describing how the "magical fire" of the soul requires a spiritual "essence" (flesh and blood) to exist, and how the human imagination can either preserve or corrupt this divine likeness.</summary>
<keywords>soul, divine wisdom, magical fire, tincture, ens, spiritual blood, imagination, final judgment, transformation, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the first "Mystic Point" concerning the substance of the soul and the divine "Tincture" to the second point, which explores the metaphysical roots of predestination and the nature of reality as divided into three spiritual realms.</meta>

<margin>Point 2. of the Mystic Points.</margin>
<margin>F 3</margin>

## II.

# On the Election of Grace, and of Good and Evil.

### Summaries.

The three Principles in God are eternally distinct. §. 1. 2. Desire creates substance in every Principle, 3–5. and life consists in the fire. 6. That which is human stands between Light and Darkness. 7. If it is free of desire, it falls home to the Light-fire. 8. It contains all three worlds within itself. 9. 10. Just as the first essence is, so too is every life, whether good or evil. 11. 12. It is a turning wheel, 13. and a threefold essence of life; 14. out of which it originates, and from which it also burns. 15. 16. The will of the fire is a flying <note>original: *fliegender*; here meaning a volatile or ungrounded</note> will. 17. The choice lies within the will. 18.

God is all things alone from eternity; His being divides into three eternal distinctions. One is the Fire-world; the other the Dark-world; and the third the Light-world. And yet there is only one being within the other, though none is the same as the other.

2. These three distinctions are equally eternal and unmeasured, and are not enclosed in any time or place. Each distinction shuts itself within itself into a being; and according to its property, so too is its quality <note>original: *Qual*. Böhme uses this word to imply both a "quality" and a "source-springing" or "torment/agony."</note>, and in its quality is also its desire, as the center of nature.

3. And the desire is its making: for it makes substance <term>Substance (*Wesen*): In Böhme's philosophy, this refers to the actual "stuff" or reality that emerges from a spiritual force or will.</term> where there is none, and does so in the essence of the desire, according to the property of the desire; and all together it is only one Magic <note>original: *Magia*</note>, or a hunger for being.

4. Every form creates substance in its desire, and every form leads itself out from the mirror of its own radiance, and has its vision in its own mirror. Its vision is a darkness to another mirror: its shape is hidden from another eye, but in feeling there is a distinction.

5. For every form takes its feeling from the origin <term>Origin (*Urstand*): A Böhmean term for the primal foundation or "original state" from which something arises.</term> of the first three forms in nature: namely, from the Astringent, the Bitter, and the Anxiety <note>These are the first three "qualities" of Böhme's seven properties of Eternal Nature.</note>: and yet in these three there is no woe in itself; rather, the fire creates woe within them, and the light transforms it back into gentleness.

6. The true life stands in the fire; there is the hinge <note>original: *Angel*, meaning the pivot point or hook upon which a door turns.</note> to Light and Darkness. The hinge is the desire: with which...

<meta>The catchword "sich" indicates that the next page will describe how the desire "turns itself" toward one of the two spiritual poles.</meta>

<summary>Böhme outlines the three eternal "Principles" or worlds—Fire, Light, and Darkness—that constitute the divine reality and explain the origins of good and evil. He argues that "Desire" is the fundamental magical force that gives these spiritual qualities a substantial form.</summary>
<keywords>Election of Grace, Fire-world, Dark-world, Light-world, Desire, Magic, Eternal Nature, Seven Properties, Predestination</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "second point" regarding the election of grace. It specifically explores how the human soul acts as a pivot point between the dark fire of selfish desire and the light fire of divine love, emphasizing that the soul's "imagination" or internal focus determines its spiritual destination.</meta>

<header>VII. On the Six Points, Point 2.</header>

...that fills itself; its fire is the desire, and its light shines out of that fire. That same light is the vision of that same form or life; and the substance introduced into the desire is the wood for the fire, from which the fire burns, whether it be harsh or gentle, and that is also its kingdom of either heaven or hell.

7. Human life is the hinge <note>original: *Angel*; can mean a pivot, a hook, or a hinge—the point upon which something turns.</note> between fire and darkness: to whichever it attaches itself, in that same world it burns. If it gives itself into the desire of the essence, then it burns in anguish, in the fire of darkness.

8. But if it gives itself into a Nothingness <note>In Böhme’s thought, "Nothingness" (*Nichts*) refers to the state of being free from self-centered desires, allowing God to work through the person.</note>, then it is free of desire and falls home to the Light-fire; thus it can burn in no torment. For it introduces no substance into its fire from which a fire could burn <note>meaning a painful, consuming fire</note>. Since there is no torment within it, life can catch no torment, for there is none in it. Now it has fallen home to the first Magic <note>original: *Magiæ*; refers to the primal creative power of God's will.</note>, which is God in His Trinity.

9. When life is born, it has all three worlds within itself; by whichever world life possesses, by that same world it is held, and in that world's fire it is ignited.

10. For when life ignites, it is drawn by all three worlds, and these stand in agitation <note>original: *Rügen*; a state of stirring or movement</note> within the essence, as in the first ignited fire: whichever essence life invites into its desire and receives, the fire of that essence burns.

11. If the first essence, in which life ignites, is good, then the fire is also lovely and good. But if it is evil and dark, arising from a fierce property, then it is a fire of wrath, and it possesses such a desire again, according to the property of the fire.

12. For every imagination <term>Imagination (*Imagination*): Not mere fantasy, but the soul's creative power to "image" itself into a specific state or reality.</term> desires only a substance of its own kind, in which it has its origin.

13. The life of man in this time is just like a wheel, where soon the bottom-most is at the top, and it ignites itself on all substances and defiles itself with all substances; but its bath is in the movement of the Heart of God, a water of meekness. Out of this, it may introduce substance into its fire-life; the choice of God does not stand in the first essence <note>meaning God does not arbitrarily pre-determine the soul's nature from the start</note>.

14. For the first essence is only the mystery <term>Mystery (*Mysterium*): The hidden spiritual core or "chaos" out of which a being emerges.</term> of life, and the first life with its ignition properly belongs to its mystery from which it proceeded. It may be a completely...
<meta>catchword: fierce-</meta>

<summary>This page explains that human life sits at a crossroads between light and darkness, and its spiritual fate is determined by what the 'imagination' focuses on. Böhme emphasizes that the soul can find peace by letting go of self-will and returning to the 'Nothingness' of God's creative power.</summary>

<header>Point 3. On Mystical Points.</header>
<sig>F 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: then</meta>

...wrathful essence <note>original: *grimmige Essenz*; refers to the harsh, consuming quality of the "dark" fire of existence</note>, or a mixed one, or a light-essence according to the Light-world.

15. From whichever quality life has its primal source <note>original: *urständet*; from *Urstand*, meaning the foundational or original state of being</note>, from that same quality the light of its life also burns. That life has no choice [in itself]; no judgment passes over it, for it stands in its own primal source and carries its judgment within itself. It separates itself from all other qualities of torment, for it burns only in its own torment, in its own magical fire.

16. The choice <note>referring to the "Election of Grace" or *Gnadenwahl*</note> concerns that which has been invited—whether it belongs to the light or to the darkness. For as a quality is, so too is the will of its life. It is recognized whether it belongs to the wrathful essence or the essence of love. So long as it burns in one fire, it is forsaken by the other, and the choice of that fire in which it burns passes over that life, for the fire desires it; it belongs to its own quality.

17. But if the will of that fire (acting as the flying hinge <note>original: *fliegende Angel*; Böhme uses this to describe the soul’s capacity to pivot or swing between spiritual realms</note>) swings itself into another fire and ignites there, it may ignite the whole of life with that same fire, provided it remains within that fire.

18. Now life is born anew, either to the Dark-world or to the Light-world, depending on where the will has ignited itself. Because of this, another choice follows. This is the reason why God allows teaching, and so does the Devil: each wants the will of life to swing into his fire and ignite; thus one Mystery <note>original: *Mysterium*</note> captures the other.

# III.
## Concerning Sin: What Sin is, and How it is Sin.

->Summaries.<-

From the twofold will comes enmity against God. §. 1. 2. Love and enmity are two [forces] in One. 3. The primal source of sin. 4. 6. Unity is love; multiplicity <note>original: *Vielheit*; the state of being divided into many selfish parts</note> is enmity. 7. 8. Therefore, focus your imagination <note>original: *imaginire*; the act of directing the soul's creative desire</note> into the One, not into multiplicity. 9-11. For whatever shall endure in God must be one will with God. 12-14. But the multiplicity of the will separates us from God. 15. Sin is self-will <note>original: *Eigen-Wille*; the egoistic will that acts for itself rather than for the whole</note> or desire. 16-18. From this comes separation and enmity, 19. greed and pride. 20. 21. All selfhood <note>original: *Eigenheit*</note> is sin. 22. Therefore, a new will is necessary. 23. Place your desire in the Word of God. 24. The new will is the new birth. 25. To be sure, wrath <note>original: *Grimm*</note> is the fire of life, 26. but self-will must be fought against; 27.

# VII. On Six Points, Point 2

...that which fills itself, its fire is Desire <term>original: *Begierde*; the driving, hungry force of existence that seeks to "pull" reality into itself</term>, and its light shines out of the fire. That same light is the image-forming <note>original: *Gestaltniß*; the way a being takes shape or form</note> or the vision of that same life; and the essence introduced into the Desire is the wood of the fire, from which the fire burns, be it harsh or gentle, and that is also its kingdom of heaven or its realm of hell.

7. Human life is the hinge <note>original: *Angel*; Böhme uses this to describe the soul as a pivot or swinging door between different spiritual dimensions</note> between fire and darkness: to whichever it attaches itself, in that same one it burns. If it gives itself into the desire of the essence, then it burns in anguish, in the fire of darkness.

8. But if it gives itself into a Nothing <note>original: *Nichts*; referring to the *Ungrund*, the divine state of "No-Thingness" that exists before and beyond all desire and manifestation</note>, then it is free of desire and falls home to the fire of light; thus it can burn in no torment, for it introduces no essence into its fire from which a [painful] fire can burn. Since there is no torment within it, life can grasp no torment, for none exists within it: now it has fallen home to the first Magic <note>original: *Magiæ*; the divine, creative power of God</note>, which is God in His Trinity.

9. When life is born, it possesses all three worlds within itself; to whichever world life attaches itself, by that same world it is held, and in that same fire it is ignited.

10. For when life ignites itself, it is drawn by all three worlds, and these stand in the stirring <note>original: *Rügen*; a state of agitation, movement, or "calling out"</note> of the essence, as in the first ignited fire: whatever kind of essence life invites and receives into its desire, that is the fire that burns.

11. If the first essence in which life ignites itself is good, then the fire is also lovely and good; but if it is evil and dark, arising from a wrathful quality <note>original: *grimmiger Eigenschaft*; the harsh, biting quality of the first principle of nature</note>, then it is also a fire of wrath, and in turn possesses such a desire, according to the quality of the fire.

12. For every imagination desires only a being of its own kind, in which it had its primal source <note>original: *geurständet*; from *Urstand*, meaning the original ground or foundational state of a being</note>.

13. The life of man in this time is just like a wheel, where the bottom is soon at the top; it ignites itself on every essence and defiles itself with every essence. But its bath is in the movement of the heart of God, a water of gentleness <note>original: *Sanftmuth*; a central Böhmean term for the cooling, loving nature of the Light-world</note>; from this, life may introduce essence into its fire-life. The Choice of God <note>referring to the Election of Grace</note> does not stand in the first essence.

14. For the first essence is only the mystery of life, and the first life with its ignition properly belongs in its mystery, from which it proceeded. Be it a completely wrath-

# Point 3. Mystical Points

...fierce essence <note>original: *grimmige Essenz*; referring to the harsh, wrathful quality of the first properties of nature</note>, or a mixed one, or a light-essence according to the light-world.

15. From whichever quality life originates, from that same quality the light of its life also burns; and that same life has no choice, for no judgment passes over it from the outside; rather, it stands in its own origin and carries its judgment within itself. It separates itself from all other torment <note>original: *Qual*; Böhme frequently puns on the words *Qual* (torment) and *Quelle* (source/fountain), implying that a being's "source" is also its "pain" if it is separated from God</note>, for it burns only in its own torment, in its own magical fire.

16. The choice <note>referring to "Election" or the determination of one's spiritual fate</note> concerns that which has been invited: whether it belongs to the light or to the darkness. For according to its quality, so also is the will of its life. It is recognized whether it belongs to the fierce essence or the essence of love; and as long as it burns in one fire, it is forsaken by the other. The choice of that fire in which it burns then claims that life, for the fire desires it; it is of the fire's own quality.

17. But if the will of that same fire (acting as the flying hinge <term>original: *Angel*; Böhme uses this to describe the soul as a pivot, door-hinge, or "sting" that can swing between realms</term>) swings itself into another fire and ignites therein, it may ignite the whole life with that same fire, provided it remains within that fire.

18. Now the life is born anew, either to the dark-world or to the light-world, whichever one the will has ignited itself within; and upon this follows a different choice. This is the reason why God allows teaching, and the Devil does as well: Each one wants the will of life to swing into his fire and ignite; and thus one mystery <term>original: *Mysterium*; a foundational spiritual reality or hidden power</term> seizes the other.

->Summaries.<-

From the two-fold will comes enmity against God. §. 1. 2. Love and enmity are two in One. 3. The origin of sin. 4:6. Unity is love; multiplicity is enmity. 7. 8. But the multiplicity of the will separates us from God. 15. Sin is the self-will <term>original: *Eigen-Wille*; the human desire to be its own master apart from the divine unity</term> or desire, 16–18. from which comes separation and enmity, 19. greed and pride, 20. 21. All self-centeredness is sin. 22. Therefore a new will is necessary. 23. Place your desire in the Word of God. 24. The new will is the new birth. 25. Truly, the fierceness is the fire of life, 26. but the self-will must be fought against. 27.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial letter 'E' containing an illustration of a seated figure or putto.</image-desc>A thing that is One has neither command nor law; but if it mixes with another, then there are two beings in One, and there are also two wills, where one runs against the other; therein originates enmity.

2. Thus we are to consider the enmity against God: God is One and Good, beyond all torment; and although all torment is in Him, it is nevertheless not manifest: For the Good has swallowed up the Evil or the adverse within itself, and holds it in check within the Good, as if captive, where the Evil must be a cause of life and light, but not manifest; rather, the Good dies to the Evil, so that it may dwell in the Evil without torment or sensation, within itself.

3. Love and enmity are but One thing, but each dwells in itself, which makes Two things: death is the boundary-mark <note>original: *Scheide-Ziel*; literally a parting-goal or point of separation</note> between them, and yet it is no death, except that the Good dies to the Evil, just as the light has died to the fire’s torment and feels the fire no more.

4. Thus we are now to fathom sin in human life: For life is one and good; but if another torment other than the good is within it, then it is an enmity against God, for God dwells in the highest life of man.

5. Now, nothing "groundless" can dwell in that which has "ground" <note>This refers to Böhme's concept of the *Ungrund* (the Groundless or Abyss) versus the *Grund* (the foundation or established nature)</note>: For as soon as the right life awakens torment within itself, it is no longer like the Groundless, in which there is no torment; thus one immediately separates from the other.

6. For the Good or the Light is as a Nothing; but if *Something* enters into it, then that same *Something* is different from the Nothing, for the *Something* dwells in itself, in torment: for where *Something* is, there must be a torment <note>In Böhme’s thought, "Something" requires a contraction or "friction" of properties to exist, which he characterizes as *Qual*</note> that makes and holds the *Something*.

7. Thus we are to consider love and enmity; love has only One torment and will, it desires only its like, and not much: For the Good is only One, but the torment is manifold, and whichever human will desires much—

### Point 3. Of the Mystical Points.

F 5

[lead]s, it carries into the One (wherein God dwells) the torment <note>original: *Qual*; Böhme frequently uses this term to denote both "torment" and the "welling up" of a source (*Quelle*). It describes the restless agitation of existence when it is separated from God’s peace.</note> of Multiplicity.

8. For the "Something" is dark and darkens the light of life; and the One is Light: for it loves itself, and there is no desire for anything more.

9. Therefore the will of life must be directed into the One (as into the Good), so it remains in one state; but if it imagines <note>This refers to the act of "forming an image" or focusing the soul's creative power on a specific object.</note> into another torment, then it becomes pregnant with the thing for which it lusts.

10. And if that same thing is without an eternal ground <note>original: *Grund*; Böhme uses this to mean the ultimate foundation or origin of a thing’s existence.</note>, existing in a fragile root, it seeks a root for its preservation so that it may remain; for all life stands in the <term>magical fire</term> <note>By "magical fire," Böhme refers to the spiritual energy or "will-force" that brings ideas into substantial reality. It is the transformative power that fuels all life.</note>; thus every fire must have a substance within which it burns.

11. Now it must make for itself a substance according to its desire, so that its fire has something to consume: yet no fire-torment can subsist in the free fire <note>The "free fire" represents the pure, unconditioned light of God's presence.</note>, for it does not reach that state, because it is only a thing of its own [selfhood].

12. Everything that is to subsist in God must be empty of its own will; it must have no "own fire" burning within it, but God's fire must be its fire: its will must be united in God, so that God's and man's will and spirit are but One.

13. For what is One does not harbor enmity against itself, for it has only One will; wherever that will goes, or whatever it does, it is One with it.

14. A will has only one <term>Imagination</term> <note>In Böhme’s vocabulary, *Imagination* is the "imaging-power"—the faculty by which the soul's inner desire takes on a specific form or "body."</note>; thus the imagination creates or desires only that which is similar to itself: we are also to understand the "will-against" <note>original: *Wiederwillen*; resistance or opposition.</note> in this way.

15. God dwells in everything, and nothing grasps Him unless it is One with Him. But if it goes out from the One, it goes out of God into its own self, and is something other than God, and it separates itself. There the law arises: that it should go out of itself again into the One, or else be separated from the One.

16. Thus it is clear what sin is, or how it is sin: namely, when the human will separates itself from God into a Selfhood <note>original: *Eigenes*; this refers to the "I-will" or the ego that seeks to be its own center instead of letting God be the center.</note>, and awakens its own self, and burns in its own torment; then that same "own fire" is not capable of receiving the Divine Fire.

### VII. Of Six Points: Point 3.

17. For everything into which the will enters, and wishes to have as its own, is something foreign within the Single Will of God; for everything is God’s, and the human own-will possesses nothing. But if he is in God, then everything is also his.

18. Thus we recognize that desire is sin; for it lusts out of the One into the many, and leads the many into the One. It wants to possess, and yet should be without will. With desire, <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*; in Böhme’s writing, this refers to the substantial reality or "being" that gives form to a spirit.</note> is sought, and within that essence, desire kindles the fire.

19. So now every fire burns according to the property of its essence. Now separation and enmity are born, for Christ says: *He who is not with me is against me, and he who does not gather with me scatters.* (Luke 11:23.) <note>original: *Wer nicht mit mir ist, der ist wieder mich...*</note> For he gathers outside of Christ, and what is not in Him is outside of God.

20. Thus we see that greed is sin, for it is a desire outside of God; and we see also that <term>pride</term> <note>original: *Hoffart*; specifically an arrogant or high-minded self-exaltation.</note> is sin, for it wants to be its own master and separates itself from God, who is the One.

21. For whatever wishes to be in God must walk in Him within His will; since we are but One in God, though in many members, it is indeed against God if one member withdraws itself from the other and makes a lord of itself, as pride does. It wants to be lord, and God alone is Lord. Now there are two lords, and they part from one another.

22. Therefore, everything is sin and a <term>contrary-will</term> <note>original: *Wiederwille*; this term implies a will that stands in opposition or "against" the primary divine flow, creating friction and spiritual conflict.</note> that the desire possesses for its own, whether it be food or drink; if the will <term>imagines</term> <note>In Böhme's system, "imagination" is a creative act where the will fixes its focus on an object to "give it a form" or bring it into being.</note> into it, it fills itself with the same and kindles its fire. Then another fire burns within the first, and thus there is contrary-will and error.

23. Therefore, a new will must grow out of the contrary-will, which surrenders itself back into the single unity, and the contrary-will must be broken and killed.

24. And here we must consider the Word of God, which became man; if man places his desire into it, he departs from the <term>torment</term> <note>original: *Qual*; Böhme often uses this to describe the restless, "welling-up" agitation of life when it is separated from the divine peace.</note> of his own fire and is born anew in the Word. Thus the outgoing will dwells in God, and the first [will] remains in desire, in <term>earthliness</term> <note>original: *Jrdigkeit*</note>, and in multiplicity.

25. Thus the multiplicity must break along with the body, and to the—

<header>Point 3. Of Mystical Points.</header>

...to die to the outgoing will, and the outgoing will is recognized as a new birth: for it takes everything back into the One, but not with its own desire, but with its own love, which is united in God, so that God may be All in all, and His will be the will of all things, for in God there exists a single will.

26. Thus we find that evil must serve good for the sake of life, provided the will exits from the evil and returns into the good; for <term>wrath</term> <note>original: *Grimm*; in Böhme's thought, this refers to the harsh, fiery, and rigorous quality of nature that provides the energy for life, but becomes "evil" when not tempered by love.</note> must be the fire of life.

27. But the will of life must be directed in conflict against itself, for it must flee from the wrath and not desire it; it must not want the desire which nevertheless desires its fire and must also have it; therefore it is called being born again in the will.

28. Every will-spirit that remains in the desire of its life's fire (as the wrath of wood belongs to the fire) or enters into it and possesses the earthly, is separated from God for as long as it possesses that which is foreign (the earthly).

29. Thus one recognizes how an abundance of food and drink works sin: For the pure will, which goes out from the life's fire, is drowned and captured in the desire, so that it becomes too weak in the struggle; for the torment of the fire (that is, of the desire) holds it captive and fills it with <term>craving</term> <note>original: *Sucht*; can also imply a sickness or an obsessive passion.</note>, so that the same will <term>imagines</term> <note>original: *imaginiret*; for Böhme, "imagination" is the process by which the will gives form to its desires, literally "making itself into an image."</note> into the desire.

30. The will in food and drink with that same desire is earthly and separated from God; but the same will that escapes the earthly fire burns in the inner fire and is Divine.

31. That same will which flees from earthly desire does not arise from the earthly fire; No! It is the soul’s fire-will, which is captured and covered by the earthly desire; it does not want to remain in the earthly desire, but wants to go into its "One," into God, from which it originally sprang.

32. But if it is held captive in the earthly desire, it is locked in death and suffers torment; thus is sin to be understood.

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the Divine Will's movement through history.
